Docs/User guide

The ForgeQE user guide. Every capability, and how to ask for it.

Install ForgeQE, learn the shell, and find every capability with what it needs, how to ask for it, and its limits, in one document.

  • Get startedInstall ForgeQE, set up a model, and run your first goal.
  • Using ForgeQEThe shell, how to ask, and what each run leaves behind.
  • Every capabilityEach feature: what it does, how to ask for it, what it needs.
  • ReferenceMCP, tuning, logs, environment variables, and every command.
01

Overview

What ForgeQE is, and everything it can do

ForgeQE is a browser agent CLI. Say what you want in plain language: a goal to carry out in a browser, test cases to run from Jira, Azure DevOps, Xray or a file, a test to write, or a CI pipeline to set up. ForgeQE's agent works out what that needs, the ForgeQE engine drives a local Chrome or Chromium by default or BrowserStack or TestMu AI/LambdaTest when configured, and a run that passes becomes a test in your repo, written by Scribe, ForgeQE's coding agent.

No server, database, or Docker. You configure a model and run it from the terminal.

DocPurpose
This guideSetup and how to run
docs/user-guide.htmlThis guide as one page, with every feature: what it needs, how to ask for it, and its details
docs/WEB_APP.mdThe web app (forgeqe web): setting up, the chat, every page, and Settings
forgeqe-architecture.htmlSingle-view map: subagents, tools, and capabilities
docs/ARCHITECTURE.mdArchitecture reference: modules, protocols, and loops
README.mdOverview

After install:

bash
forgeqe docs                 # print the paths of the bundled docs
forgeqe docs --guide         # open the docs in the browser, offline: the pages of forgeqe.github.io/docs/, with search, and the architecture map
forgeqe docs --open          # open the architecture page in the browser
forgeqe docs --copy ./docs   # copy the guide, the architecture page, the reference and the docs pages into a folder

Every feature

Browser

  • Saved logins for the application under testSign in to the admin site and keep the session
  • Record a web test by handI'll show you the checkout steps in the shop: record them

  • Write test cases from a storyWrite test cases for story 1234 and save them
  • Domain and regulatory test designWrite test cases for story 1234, covering our DORA and PSD2 obligations
  • Exploratory testing of a storyExplore story 1234 and find bugs
Setup
  • Test environments and their appsPlace an order in the shop, then check its invoice in Billing, in UAT
  • What ForgeQE wroteHow much of the code ForgeQE wrote is still there?
  • Every forgeqe command from the shellShow me the context budget
  • Setup, skills, and documentationWhat can you do?

  • Automate Windows desktop appsCreate an invoice for Contoso in the Billing app and save it as a test
Mobile

  • Background jobs on a scheduleRun the release-2.3 pack every weekday at 2am in the background
  • Teams and email notificationsPost the results to the QA channel

  • API and contract testingTurn the last run's API calls into tests

  • Accessibility and page performanceIs the checkout page accessible?
  • Visual comparison with baselinesWhat changed visually since the last run?
  • Defect intelligence: trends and predictionWhich defects keep coming back?

  • Requirement coverage and release readinessAre we ready to release?
  • Reports you can shareCreate a release report for all projects
Systems
  • Work with Azure DevOps, Jira, GitHub, and ConfluenceShow the open bugs in this sprint

Not supported

  • Load, stress, or soak testing with many simultaneous users. Instead: per-page Core Web Vitals with budgets.
  • Penetration testing or dynamic security scanning. Instead: security findings in pull request reviews.
02

Get started

Install, configure a model, and sign in to your tools

What you need to run

Required

RequirementNotes
npm (Node.js 18+) or pip (Python 3.9+)To install @capagents/forgeqe or forgeqe-cli; either one gives the same forgeqe command
A terminalmacOS Terminal, iTerm2, VS Code or Cursor's terminal, Windows Terminal
A modelA profile in llms.json with a working key, or Microsoft Entra ID sign-in for Azure (Azure OpenAI, OpenAI, an OpenAI-compatible gateway, or Ollama), or a GitHub Copilot seat (see GitHub Copilot seats as the model)
Internet access on first useForgeQE downloads Bun, the agent runtime, uv, Python, and the engine into ~/.forgeqe once

Optional

OptionalWhy
Google ChromeUsed when installed; otherwise ForgeQE installs Chromium. Or set browser.provider to BrowserStack or TestMu AI/LambdaTest
A repo with testsWhere passed runs become tests (--repo, or code.repo); without one, tests go in the current folder
Jira, Azure DevOps, Xray, TestRail, Zephyr Scale, qTest, ConfluenceSources of test cases and requirements, and where results go back
gh or azFor pipelines: pushing, secrets, and triggering runs
Teams webhook or SMTPMonitoring alerts

Not required

  • Installing Python, Bun, browsers, or agent runtimes yourself: ForgeQE downloads what it needs, without admin rights and without touching PATH
  • A server, database, or Docker

Quick start

bash
npm install -g @capagents/forgeqe     # or: pip install forgeqe-cli
forgeqe init                          # forgeqe.yaml, llms.json, and .forgeqe/ (prompts, skills, knowledge) in this folder
export WP_AZURE_API_KEY=...            # the key your llms.json profile names
forgeqe models                        # check the profiles
forgeqe start                         # the shell

With GitHub Copilot licences instead of a model key, sign in rather than exporting one. The sign-in lists your seat's models, recommends one (it takes screenshots and, where the plan has one, uses no premium requests), and writes your choice to llms.json as the copilot profile, made the default. Everything then runs on your Copilot seat: ForgeQE's agent, Scribe, and the browser, desktop and mobile engines. No other model is needed:

bash
forgeqe setup                         # the engine and GitHub's Copilot SDK
forgeqe auth login copilot            # opens GitHub in your browser to sign in, then pick the model
forgeqe doctor                        # checks the engine, the SDK, the agent runtime, the sign-in and each model

In the shell, type a request and press Enter:

text
Go to booking.com and search for hotels in Mumbai for next weekend
Run SHOP-12 and SHOP-14 from Jira, two browsers at once
Write a Playwright test for that run in ../app

Or in your browser, with the same agents, a page for each kind of work, and every setting as a form:

bash
forgeqe web                           # opens http://127.0.0.1:…/#token=… in your browser: start at Settings › Set up

Without the shell:

bash
forgeqe run "Search hotels in Mumbai on booking.com" --plain
forgeqe suite cases.yaml --parallel 3        # what pipelines call

forgeqe models lists the profiles ForgeQE sees and marks the active one. With no profile set up, the shell's footer reads Model none set up and any request explains how to add one.

With more than one profile, llm.routing: auto gives each kind of work (agent, browser, coding, review, analysis, planning) the configured model it needs; forgeqe models --routes shows the picks. See docs/MODEL_ROUTING.md.

Install

bash
npm install -g @capagents/forgeqe
# or
pip install forgeqe-cli
forgeqe --help

That is the only install step on Windows, macOS and Linux. npm installs Bun with ForgeQE (through your npm registry, so a company mirror works); with pip, or when npm skipped it, the first run downloads Bun into ~/.forgeqe/tools (unless a Bun 1.3+ is already installed), and forgeqe setup downloads the agent runtime and uv, which fetches Python. Nothing is added to PATH or shell profiles; deleting ~/.forgeqe removes it all.

Set up the ForgeQE engine once (the first live run does it automatically if you skip this):

bash
forgeqe setup

It creates ~/.forgeqe/engine/.venv, installs the engine's Python libraries, and prints the Python, the engine version, the browser it found, and the agent runtime. Google Chrome is used when installed; otherwise Chromium is installed. forgeqe setup --force rebuilds the environment.

Updating

bash
forgeqe update            # every forgeqe on PATH to the latest release
forgeqe update --check    # show what would change and the commands it would run
forgeqe update 0.2.7      # a given version
forgeqe --version

forgeqe update finds each forgeqe on PATH (a bun, npm or pip install; often more than one) and updates each with the tool that installed it, skipping that tool's cached copy of the version list. Right after a release, npm and PyPI accept the upload several minutes before installers can fetch it, and bun add or npm install in that window says No version matching; forgeqe update waits for the registry instead (up to 20 minutes, --wait MIN to change). The first forgeqe on PATH is the one your shell runs; open a new terminal (or run hash -r) after updating.

From a checkout instead of the registries:

bash
cd ForgeQE
bun install
bun src/index.ts --help

Company networks

On a company laptop, ForgeQE sets itself up for the network without anyone changing settings:

What the network doesWhat ForgeQE does
Inspects HTTPS (Zscaler, Netskope, a firewall), with the company's own certificateUses the certificates this computer trusts: Windows' Trusted Root store, or the macOS System keychain. ForgeQE, the agent runtime, Playwright and Copilot's runtime get them through NODE_EXTRA_CA_CERTS (a copy in ~/.forgeqe/cache/system-ca.pem, refreshed weekly); the Python engines check with the system through truststore; uv with UV_SYSTEM_CERTS. Certificates are still verified.
Sends traffic through a proxy set in WindowsReads Windows' proxy setting (or the one proxy its automatic configuration script names) and passes it on as HTTPS_PROXY. ForgeQE's own servers on 127.0.0.1 always bypass it.
Blocks downloads from GitHub or the Playwright CDNnpm brings Bun through your registry; Microsoft Edge is used when Google Chrome is not installed, so no Chromium is downloaded; mirrors can stand in for the rest (below).

To see where the network stops ForgeQE, run:

bash
forgeqe doctor

It shows the proxy and certificates in use, checks each address ForgeQE needs (GitHub downloads, Python packages, the npm registry, your models, Azure DevOps or Jira), and for anything blocked prints what to ask IT for, ready to forward.

For IT: mirrors and settings. Set these as environment variables (for example by Group Policy):

VariableWhat it does
FORGEQE_GITHUB_MIRRORA mirror of GitHub release downloads (https://artifacts.example.com/github, laid out as <owner>/<repo>/releases/download/<tag>/<file>): Bun, uv, the agent runtime, Python builds and Copilot's runtime come from it. Checksums are still checked.
UV_DEFAULT_INDEX (or PIP_INDEX_URL)A mirror of PyPI for the engine's Python packages.
PLAYWRIGHT_DOWNLOAD_HOSTA mirror for Chromium, needed only without Chrome or Edge.
HTTPS_PROXY, NO_PROXYA proxy other than Windows' setting. A proxy that needs a sign-in takes http://user:password@host:port.
NODE_EXTRA_CA_CERTSYour own certificate file instead of the system's roots.
FORGEQE_SYSTEM_CERTS=offLeaves certificates to each tool's built-in list.
FORGEQE_HOMEWhere ForgeQE keeps its tools (default ~/.forgeqe), for machines where programs may run only from certain folders.

What still needs IT: a proxy that signs in with your Windows account only (NTLM or Kerberos), and policies that block programs in the user folder (set FORGEQE_HOME to an allowed folder). On macOS, the company's certificate must be in the System keychain, where device management puts it.

Scaffold config

bash
bun src/index.ts init
# or
bun src/index.ts init --global --profile azure-gpt4o

Edit llms.json endpoints, then give ForgeQE the keys its profiles name (WP_AZURE_API_KEY, OPENAI_API_KEY, WP_API_KEY): forgeqe auth login <NAME> saves one in your system's credential store, or put them in a .env next to forgeqe.yaml; the engine process inherits them.

Signing in to the model without a key (Microsoft Entra ID)

Where keys are turned off (disableLocalAuth on the Azure OpenAI or Foundry resource, managed identities, zero trust), set auth: entra on an azure profile instead of api_key_env. Key profiles keep working, and both kinds can sit side by side in one llms.json:

json
"azure-entra": {
  "provider": "azure",
  "model": "gpt-4o",
  "deployment": "gpt-4o",
  "endpoint": "https://YOUR_RESOURCE.openai.azure.com/",
  "auth": "entra",
  "managed_identity_client_id": "00000000-0000-0000-0000-000000000000"
}

ForgeQE asks Microsoft Entra ID for a token wherever it runs, trying in this order:

Where ForgeQE runsIdentity it uses
An Azure VM, VM scale set, self-hosted agent, App Service, Functions, Container AppsThe user-assigned managed identity named by managed_identity_client_id (or AZURE_CLIENT_ID); without one, the system-assigned identity
AKSWorkload identity (AZURE_FEDERATED_TOKEN_FILE, set by the webhook)
Azure PipelinesAn AzureCLI@2 task on a service connection with workload identity federation: ForgeQE uses that task's sign-in, with no secret
GitHub Actionsazure/login with OIDC, then ForgeQE in the same job
Your machineaz login
Anything elseA service principal in AZURE_TENANT_ID, AZURE_CLIENT_ID and AZURE_CLIENT_SECRET (or AZURE_CLIENT_CERTIFICATE_PATH)

Give that identity the Cognitive Services OpenAI User role on the resource. Tokens are renewed before they expire, so long suites and coding sessions keep running. The browser engine signs in itself; the coding agent's runtime only takes a key, so ForgeQE gives it a gateway on 127.0.0.1 that accepts a one-time random key and passes each request on with a current token. No key is stored anywhere.

auth: entra also works on an openai_compatible profile, for a gateway such as API Management that checks Entra ID tokens: set token_scope to the scope it expects (for example api://<app id>/.default; the default is https://cognitiveservices.azure.com/.default). The engine needs the azure-identity package, which forgeqe setup installs (and adds to environments set up before it was needed); with FORGEQE_PYTHON, install it there yourself.

GitHub Copilot seats as the model

With GitHub Copilot licences (Pro, Pro+, Business or Enterprise), a github_copilot profile runs the browser engine and the desktop and mobile agents on your Copilot seat, through GitHub's Copilot SDK. Copilot gives other tools no model API of its own, so ForgeQE starts a small gateway on 127.0.0.1 that runs the SDK and speaks the OpenAI chat API to the engine. It signs in with a GitHub token, so no other app is authorized on your GitHub account.

json
"copilot": {
  "provider": "github_copilot",
  "model": "gpt-4.1"
}
FieldWhat it is
modelThe Copilot model, as the Copilot SDK names it (gpt-4.1, claude-sonnet-4.6). It must take screenshots. The sign-in lists your seat's models and which of them take images.
engine_modelOptional. Another Copilot model for the browser engine and the desktop agent.
api_key_envOptional. A variable that must hold the GitHub token for this profile. Without it ForgeQE uses COPILOT_GITHUB_TOKEN, then a token forgeqe auth login copilot saved, then the browser sign-in.

Sign in once per machine (Windows, macOS or Linux):

bash
forgeqe auth login copilot

It offers two ways, the ones GitHub's Copilot SDK recommends:

  • Sign in with GitHub in the browser (recommended on your own computer). ForgeQE runs GitHub's own Copilot sign-in (copilot login from GitHub's Copilot CLI, downloaded once into ~/.forgeqe/tools and checked against GitHub's checksums). Your browser opens GitHub; approve GitHub Copilot CLI there and you are signed in. GitHub's CLI keeps the sign-in in your system's credential store (Windows Credential Manager, the macOS Keychain); ForgeQE never sees a token. On a remote machine it shows a code to enter at github.com/login/device instead.
  • A fine-grained token, for machines without a browser. ForgeQE opens GitHub's token page: set Resource owner to your personal account and add the Copilot Requests permission, then paste the token (github_pat_...). Each character shows as *, and ForgeQE confirms what it got (github_pat_11AB…Wz9Q (93 characters)). The token goes to your system's credential store like ForgeQE's other secrets. Classic tokens (ghp_) do not work with Copilot.

Either way ForgeQE checks the sign-in by asking Copilot for your seat's models, then asks which one to use (recommended first; models your seat has but has not turned on are named, with the settings page) and writes it to llms.json; the first time, the Copilot SDK downloads Copilot's runtime from GitHub (checked against GitHub's checksums). forgeqe auth shows the sign-in on its Copilot line; forgeqe auth logout copilot makes ForgeQE stop using it (GitHub keeps a browser sign-in until you revoke GitHub Copilot CLI at github.com/settings/applications).

If your organization uses SAML single sign-on, GitHub asks you to authorize the organization during the browser sign-in. A Copilot Business or Enterprise seat needs your organization to allow Copilot CLI in its Copilot policies.

A token reaches only the gateway's process. The engine gets the gateway's address and a one-time key that works nowhere else; the gateway refuses requests without that key and any request a web page sends. Model-run shell commands never see the token or the key.

Which part uses what:

PartModelSigns in with
The browser engine (every browser run: run --direct, suites, monitoring)engine_model or model, on the Copilot seatforgeqe auth login copilot
The desktop and mobile agentsengine_model or model, on the Copilot seatforgeqe auth login copilot
ForgeQE's agent and Scribemodel, on the Copilot seatforgeqe auth login copilot

Copilot runs one SDK session per model request, with none of Copilot's own tools and your request's system prompt in place of Copilot's. The agent's tools are offered to the model as the session's only tools; when it calls one, the gateway ends the turn and hands the call back to ForgeQE's agent runtime, which runs it (with the same permissions as on any other model) and sends the result with the next request. The gateway itself runs nothing.

With Copilot and a key-based model both in llms.json, Copilot comes first: a sign-in makes it the default, and without a "default" it is the active profile. Before a model is used, ForgeQE checks that it works (its sign-in or key, and one live call, kept for a day once it passes). If Copilot does not work (not signed in, the Copilot SDK missing, the model not on your seat), the other model is used and ForgeQE says why and how to fix Copilot; if a key-based model you made the default does not work, Copilot is tried first. A model that fails its check is never used, and when none works the error lists each one with what to do. This happens with llm.routing off too: routing picks a model for each kind of work, the fallback only replaces one that does not work. --profile turns it off for one run, and llm.fallback: false in forgeqe.yaml for good: the model you chose is used as it is, and its own error is shown. Each request is billed to the seat (GitHub AI credits or premium requests, by your plan), and GitHub may rate-limit frequent automated requests. If a model is refused, check that your organization's Copilot policies allow it. ForgeQE shows no cost for a github_copilot profile unless you give it a price.

In pipelines. forgeqe suite ... --no-code uses only the browser engine. Set COPILOT_GITHUB_TOKEN to a fine-grained token with the Copilot Requests permission, stored as a pipeline secret. GitHub also documents the workflow's own GITHUB_TOKEN with permissions: copilot-requests: write for organization repositories whose Copilot policy allows it (server tokens). A Copilot seat belongs to one person: prefer an organization-billed token over signing a shared pipeline in to someone's seat.

GitHub Models, which ForgeQE used for the engine before, was retired by GitHub on July 30, 2026. A profile that still has engine_model: "openai/..." or github_org needs updating: drop github_org, and use a Copilot model id. A profile whose endpoint is GitHub Models (models.github.ai) is never called: remove it from llms.json. Copilot needs no GITHUB_TOKEN or GH_TOKEN; ForgeQE does not pass them to Copilot.

When something goes wrong.

ForgeQE saysWhat to do
GitHub Copilot is not signed inRun forgeqe auth login copilot (or /auth copilot in the shell) and sign in in the browser, or set COPILOT_GITHUB_TOKEN.
The GitHub sign-in did not finishThe browser sign-in was cancelled or denied. Run forgeqe auth login copilot again; with SAML single sign-on, authorize your organization when GitHub asks.
GitHub Copilot does not take classic tokens (ghp_)Sign in in the browser instead, or make a fine-grained token with the Copilot Requests permission, owned by your personal account.
GitHub Copilot did not accept the tokenThe token has no Copilot Requests permission, has expired, or the account has no Copilot seat.
GitHub Copilot refused the sign-in or refused the token (Authorization error. Your credentials may be expired or invalid)Copilot received the credential and would not take it. A token wins over the browser sign-in: if COPILOT_GITHUB_TOKEN is set, or you once pasted a token, that is what was refused; remove it and run forgeqe auth login copilot in the browser. Otherwise sign in again. If it still fails and your seat comes from your company, a GitHub admin must allow Copilot CLI in the Copilot policies: the Copilot SDK runs on it.
… must be a Copilot model id, engine_model must be a Copilot model idFix that field in llms.json: the sign-in lists the seat's models. A GitHub Models id such as openai/gpt-4.1 is not a Copilot id; use gpt-4.1.
No model in llms.json can … right nowEach model is listed with why it cannot be used and the fix. forgeqe doctor checks everything at once.
GitHub's Copilot SDK is not installed in the ForgeQE engineRun forgeqe setup. It installs the SDK from pypi.org; if that is blocked, forgeqe doctor says what to ask IT for.
Model for agent: main, because copilot failed its live check …ForgeQE used your other model because Copilot did not answer; the message ends with the fix.
The model is refused, or not foundThe seat's plan or your organization's Copilot policies do not allow that model: pick one the sign-in listed, or ask a Copilot admin to enable it.
GitHub Copilot runs through the ForgeQE engine, which is not set up (or could not be set up (…))Run forgeqe setup; the reason in brackets says what failed.
Copilot is slow, or a run waits on it ("Starting the browser…", "working out the first step…")~/.forgeqe/logs/copilot-gateway.log has a line for each Copilot request: when it started, Copilot's session, when each turn ended, and how long it took. It never holds prompts, replies or tokens. A turn that ends without Copilot saying it is idle (a known Copilot SDK issue) is answered 5 seconds later instead of after the 5-minute limit, and a session the run no longer needs (Stop, an error) is stopped.
CERTIFICATE_VERIFY_FAILED while downloading Copilot's runtimeYour network inspects HTTPS. Update ForgeQE and run forgeqe setup, then sign in again: it uses the certificates your computer trusts (see Company networks). If it still fails, run forgeqe doctor and send its output to IT.

The model's context window

Each agent has its own context: ForgeQE's agent, Scribe, the reviewer, and the browser agent each keep their own conversation with the model. The Context panel shows the working agent's (or the last one to call the model), named on its Agent row, as a share of what it can hold before the agent runtime compacts it. When it compacts, the runtime summarises the conversation so far to make room, Activity says so, and details from early on may be gone. The browser agent never compacts: it keeps its last steps only.

ForgeQE takes each model's window from its name (GPT-5: 400K, of which a prompt may take 272K; GPT-4.1: 1M; GPT-4o: 128K; o-series and Claude: 200K; Gemini: 1M; anything else: 200K) and gives it to the agent runtime, which compacts at that window less room for the answer. Where a deployment or server allows less, say so with "context" in the profile in llms.json:

json
"ollama-llama": { "provider": "ollama", "model": "llama3.2", "endpoint": "http://127.0.0.1:11434/v1", "context": 32768 }

For Ollama this matters: a prompt longer than the server's context length is cut from the start without an error. Without "context" ForgeQE assumes 32K for an Ollama profile; start the server with OLLAMA_CONTEXT_LENGTH set to the same number.

Signing in to your tools

Instead of copying tokens into .env, sign in once and ForgeQE saves the token in your system's credential store: the macOS Keychain, Windows Credential Manager, or the Secret Service keyring on Linux. On a machine without one, such as a headless Linux box, it uses ~/.forgeqe/credentials.json, readable only by you.

bash
forgeqe auth login            # pick a system
forgeqe auth login jira       # or name it: azure_devops, github, copilot, jira, confluence, testrail, zephyr, qtest, xray
forgeqe auth                  # what is signed in, and how
forgeqe auth logout jira

In the shell, type /auth jira (or /auth alone for the status). Tokens are typed into the prompt box unseen, shown as dots, and never sent to the agent.

SystemHow you sign in
Azure DevOpsSign in with Microsoft in the browser through the Azure CLI (az login); ForgeQE saves nothing, and the Azure CLI renews the sign-in. Or paste a personal access token: the token page opens with the scopes to tick. Azure DevOps Server (on-premises) uses a personal access token.
GitHubSign in with GitHub in the browser through the GitHub CLI (gh auth login): ForgeQE shows the one-time code and opens the page. Or paste a token. Needs gh installed. forgeqe auth login copilot can use this sign-in for a github_copilot profile.
GitHub Copilotforgeqe auth login copilot: Sign in with GitHub in the browser (GitHub's own Copilot sign-in), or paste a fine-grained token with the Copilot Requests permission. ForgeQE checks it with Copilot and lists the models your seat has. For a github_copilot profile; see GitHub Copilot seats as the model.
Jira, ConfluenceYour Atlassian API tokens page opens (Cloud) or your personal access tokens page (Server/Data Center); create one and paste it. Confluence on the same Cloud site as Jira reuses the Jira sign-in.
TestRail, Zephyr Scale, qTest, Xray CloudThe page with your API key opens, or ForgeQE says where to find it; paste it.
Anything elseforgeqe auth login OPENAI_API_KEY (any variable name) saves that one secret: a model key, a Teams webhook URL, an SMTP or saved-login password.

Atlassian's OAuth sign-in needs a client secret, which a command-line tool cannot keep secret, so Jira and Confluence use API tokens: they are what Atlassian recommends for scripts and tools.

Each token is checked against the system before it is saved, and the system is switched on: a saved Jira sign-in sets the site URL too, so sources.jira is not needed in forgeqe.yaml. Saved values go in under the variable names the config already reads (JIRA_API_TOKEN, ADO_PAT, ...). A variable exported in the terminal is used before a saved sign-in, and a saved sign-in before a .env file, so pipelines keep using their secrets. The shell commands the agents run never see the saved secrets.

Choosing the model and the browser

forgeqe.yaml selects the active profile:

yaml
llm:
  models_file: ./llms.json
  active: azure-gpt4o
  routing: auto          # off | shadow | auto: each area of work gets the model it needs
  # routes:              # limit or pin an area: browser: [gpt4o, gpt54], coding: gpt54

browser:
  headless: true
  max_steps: 40          # a checkpoint: a run still making progress carries on, up to 3x
  timeout_seconds: 600
  vision_detail: low
  allowed_hosts: []
  judge: false           # true: a second model call checks each finished run and can fail it
  overlay: true
  window: maximized      # visible browser: maximized | viewport (a window of viewport's size)
  video: true            # video.mp4 of each run, shown in its report
  # start_url: https://staging.example.com

judge is off by default. When it is on, one more model call reads the finished run's steps and screenshots and can fail a run the agent completed, for example when five scores were read off one results page rather than one step per item. That strictness sent runs into loops of re-checking, so turn it on only when you want that second opinion.

Each step waits for the tab the agent is on to finish loading (up to 8 seconds, 3 for a tab that stays blank) before the page is read, so a result that opens in a new tab is not mistaken for a blank page.

With a visible browser (headless: false or --headed), ForgeQE opens on its own start page, and every page it drives gets a glowing edge and a bar along the bottom showing, in plain words, what the step is for and what the agent is doing ("Step 19 · Check whether the results can be sorted by Fastest · Looking through the page's buttons"). Your mouse and keyboard are locked while the agent thinks and unlocked while it clicks and types, so a stray click can't change the test. None of this appears in the screenshots the model sees or in the report. overlay: false turns it off. forgeqe login <name> shows the same bar without the lock, since you are the one signing in.

A visible browser opens maximized. window: viewport opens it at viewport's width and height instead; a headless page is always that size (1280×800 by default). Each run is recorded to video.mp4 in its run folder, and the report plays it on the run's page; video: false turns recording off. The video needs packages that forgeqe setup adds to the engine (an engine set up before this version gets them on its next run).

start_url is optional. Leave it out and the agent picks the site from the goal; ForgeQE never guesses a URL from the goal text. allowed_hosts empty allows every host. Set ["example.com"] to keep the browser on that domain and its subdomains. prompts.step_instruction is appended to the engine's system prompt; the lines of prompts.guiderails are guardrails for every agent (see Guardrails above).

03

Using ForgeQE

The shell, its commands, and running without it

Shell

bash
bun src/index.ts start

Type what you want and press Enter. The prompt wraps long text and grows as you type; Ctrl+J (or Shift+Enter, Alt+Enter where the terminal reports it) starts a new line, so you can paste or write several steps at once:

text
Go to booking.com and search for hotels in Mumbai
Run SHOP-12 and SHOP-14 from Jira, two browsers at once
Run test plan 12 suite 34 from Azure DevOps and don't post results
Write a Playwright test for that run
Add a GitHub Actions workflow that runs tests/cases.yaml on every PR, and make it green

ForgeQE's agent reads the message and decides what it needs: a browser run, test cases from Jira, Azure DevOps, Xray, a file, or steps you pasted, a test, or a pipeline. There is no command to pick one. When a reference is ambiguous (is 12 a work item or a test plan?), it asks, and you answer in the next message; the session remembers the conversation. Its replies appear in the transcript, and so does everything its tools do: browser steps, suite progress, and the test commands ForgeQE runs.

For a browser goal, the browser starts blank and the browser agent decides where to go, then works through cookie banners, sign-in prompts, and forms on the page. The transcript shows each step's goal and the actions it took (navigate …, click "Accept", input "Mumbai" → "Where are you going?"), with ✗ and the error when an action fails.

Everything the other forgeqe commands do is also a tool the agent can call, so you can ask for it here instead of leaving the shell:

Ask forToolSame as
"Run SHOP-12 and SHOP-14", "what's in test plan 12?"run_test_cases, find_test_casesforgeqe suite, forgeqe cases
"Write a Playwright test for that run"write_testforgeqe code
"The checkout tests are failing, fix them"heal_testsforgeqe heal
"Run all tests related to login", "run the tests added this sprint", "which tests cover checkout?"find_tests, run_tests, list_sprints(shell and forgeqe run only)
"Check the smoke cases once", "monitor them every 10 minutes", "is anything failing?", "stop monitoring", "send a test alert"monitorforgeqe monitor, --once, --test-alerts
"Sign in to shop", "which logins do I have?", "forget the shop session"loginsforgeqe login, forgeqe logins, forgeqe logins clear
"What flows does page memory know?", "forget what you learned about shop.test"page_memoryforgeqe memory
"What can you do?", "can you do API testing?", "do you support DORA?"capabilitiesforgeqe features
"Which profiles do I have?", "which MCP servers will you use?", "how do email alerts work?", "install the engine", "scaffold a config in ./demo"forgeqe_setupforgeqe models, forgeqe mcp list, forgeqe docs, forgeqe setup, forgeqe init

Only browser runs of the app under test become tests. When the agent uses the browser for a task, such as checking whether a story is linked to a test case in Azure DevOps or Jira, that run is marked as a task and no test is written for it, even if write_test is called on it. write_test and heal_tests run the same loops as the commands: ForgeQE runs the test command itself and a test only counts as passing when ForgeQE saw it pass. Their results, and each monitor round, appear as a card in the transcript. When a step needs you, such as signing in by hand in the browser window, a Your turn prompt shows what to do, and ForgeQE waits until you pick Done, continue (save the session) or Cancel.

The slash commands control the session and show a few things directly (/help lists them):

CommandAction
/stopStop what is running (or press Esc)
/rerun [last|run]Run a passed run again as recorded, without ForgeQE's agent (the last one, a run folder, or its goal)
/headed on|offShow the browser window (live profiles)
/theme light|dark|autoColours for a light or a dark terminal. ForgeQE asks the terminal which it is when it starts and follows it when it changes (auto); a terminal that does not say is drawn dark. FORGEQE_THEME=light (or dark) sets it at start; /theme alone says which is in use
/details [on|off]Show everything the agents do while they work: the browser agent's reasoning, page memory, engine logs, every file the coding agent reads (off by default: step results, files written, test results, problems). --verbose or FORGEQE_VERBOSE=1 starts with it on, for start, run, suite, code, and heal too
/modelPick the model from the profiles in llms.json (or click the model ▾ in the status line; Tab switches to the next one)
/profile <name>Switch LLM profile (or press Tab); the agent restarts with it
/env [name]The test environments in env.yaml and their apps, or switch to one
/logs [shots]Save this session's log and runs in one archive to share (shots: with screenshots)
/featuresWhat ForgeQE can do, and what each capability still needs here
/runsThe Runs page: every run with its result, steps and what page memory replayed; the week as a chart; click a run (or ↑↓) to see how it ended and every step it took: by page memory or the model, the page, what it did, how long it took, and the step that failed with its error. enter opens its report, f its folder, r runs it again
/reportsThe Reports page: every report ForgeQE wrote here (suites, pipelines, /report), newest first, each with open ↗ to view it in the browser and a short summary of the chosen one. / searches by title, kind and summary (esc clears). A single run's report is on the Runs page. f opens its folder, b builds a new report
/jobsThe Jobs screen: every scheduled job with its next run, last result and recent runs; the week's runs as a chart; the chosen job's runs and output. ↑↓ choose, r runs now, p pauses or resumes, s stops, l opens the log, esc goes back
/knowledgeThe Knowledge page: what ForgeQE has kept here as it works (run history, page memory, defect history, the code it wrote and how much is still there, product knowledge, visual baselines, watched pipelines), each with its entries
/skillsThe Skills page: every skill with the agents that use it and where it comes from; enter or open ↗ opens its SKILL.md (/skills <name> shows one in the chat)
/memoryThe Page memory page: each site with its pages, elements and flows; the chosen site's pages and flows
/guardrailsThe Guardrails page: every rule and the file it is in. o opens the file to edit
/doctorThe Doctor page: what ForgeQE needs on this machine, with the fix for what is missing. r also checks the model, the trackers and every service ForgeQE reaches
/settingsThe Settings page: the model, config file, trackers, environments, folders, domain and notifications, each opening where it is changed
/homeBack to the start screen; the session stays
/docsThe user guide
/generatedHow much of this repository's code ForgeQE wrote, and how much is still there
/initSet ForgeQE up in this folder: forgeqe.yaml, llms.json, editable prompts, skills, knowledge
/skills [name]The skills each agent can use, or one skill's instructions
/db [test|tables|refresh|batches|examples|add] [name]The databases set up here (see Databases)
/report, /report export, /report <ask>The team report of every run here and the ones teammates shared; your bundle to share; or ask the agent for a report, e.g. /report open defects in all projects (see Team report)
/auth [system]Sign in to a system in the browser; /auth alone shows what is signed in
/clearClear the transcript
/helpExamples and commands
/exitQuit

start_url in forgeqe.yaml or --url pins a start page for browser runs, such as a staging app. Passed runs become tests in the current folder; forgeqe start --repo ../app puts them in that repo instead, and --no-code turns tests off.

ForgeQE's agent runs on the agent runtime with the active profile's model (or code.model), or, with llm.routing: auto, the model the router picks for the agent area when the session starts (--profile and code.model still win). A github_copilot profile runs it on your Copilot seat. ForgeQE downloads the agent runtime into ~/.forgeqe/tools when it is not installed. When the agent runtime cannot be downloaded, ForgeQE says the agent is off and runs each message as one browser goal.

While a run is going, the bar under the prompt shows a spinner, the tokens used, and the elapsed time, and the transcript ends with what the agent is doing now (Thinking about step 3…, Acting in the browser (step 3)…). Each step starts with + Thought: 4.2s, the time the model took to decide. When the run ends, a summary card closes the transcript:

text
┃  ✓ Task complete                                   10 steps · 1m 42s · 65.3K tokens
┃
┃  Done — I searched Booking.com for hotels in Mumbai for tomorrow. I verified the results
┃  page shows destination "Mumbai" with dates "Tue 29 Sept" to "Wed 30 Sept" …
┃
┃  Ended on  booking.com/searchresults.en-gb.html
┃  Saved     .forgeqe/reports/20260928-172700/  run.json · workflow.yaml · trace.md · report.html

The text is the agent's own result. Failed runs show ✗ Task failed with the reason, and stopped runs show ■ Run stopped. forgeqe run prints the same summary at the end.

The screen

The start screen. Before your first request, the screen is one column under the logo:

text
  playwright-csharp-pom-framework  ·  .NET  ·  Azure DevOps

  ┃ Describe what you want to test…
  ✦ Refine ctrl+r   ⌕ Search ctrl+p                          ? shortcuts  ctrl+c close

  Start with
    ▸ Run test cases            Run test plan 12 suite 34 from Azure DevOps…   alt+1
    ▸ Check release readiness   Are we ready to release?                       alt+2
  Recent runs and reports
    ▤ Report  QI-Payments pipeline status report           18 min ago   open
    ✓ Run     Search hotels in Mumbai on booking.com       2 days ago   open  rerun
  Setup  ▰▰▰▱▱  3 of 5 · Domain and Notifications are not set up   ▸ how to set up
● Tip  Press Tab to switch LLM profile        azure/gpt-5.4 (default) ▾  ·  ForgeQE
  • The line under the logo is what ForgeQE works with here: the folder, what it is built with (from its files, such as a .sln or playwright.config.ts), the systems that are set up. The model and its profile are in the footer, the status line on every screen; click the model ▾ (or type /model) to pick another, or press Tab for the next one.
  • The prompt starts five rows high and grows with a long request, up to nearly half the screen, before it scrolls; the sections below give way, last first, to make room.
  • Start with offers up to four things to ask, from the capabilities that work in this setup, each with an example in the words of your setup. Click one, or press Alt-1 to Alt-4, to put it in the prompt to adjust and send.
  • In a folder without forgeqe.yaml, or without a live model, the line under the logo says so and Get started takes its place: Set up ForgeQE in this folder (runs /init), Add a model, and Sign in to Azure DevOps or Jira (runs /auth). Start ForgeQE in your test project's folder to work with its settings.
  • Recent runs (Recent runs and reports when there are reports) lists the newest runs and reports in the reports folder: open opens the report, rerun puts the run's goal in the prompt.
  • Setup shows how much is set up and what is missing (see the setup checklist below); it goes away once everything is set up.
  • The tip in the footer changes every few seconds. Press ? on an empty prompt for every key; Esc closes the list.

The conversation. Once you send a request, the screen is one column that reads top to bottom, as in Claude Code:

text
> get the current release details from all projects

⏺ Azure DevOps · pipelines build (action list, project QI-Payments, top 1)
⏺ ForgeQE · query_api (azure_devops, vsrm.dev.azure.com/…/_apis/release/releases)
  ⎿ No classic releases came back, so I will confirm on the Releases page.

⏺ Browser: Open Autothon › Pipelines › Releases
  ⎿ Step 1 · Open the Autothon project
    ✓ navigate https://dev.azure.com/devcgazuredevops/Autothon

⏺ No classic releases exist in any of the seven projects.
  …

⠹ Acting in the browser (step 3)… (24s · step 3/40 · 57.4K tokens · esc to interrupt)
╭──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────╮
│ > Ask ForgeQE… (/ for commands, @ to reference a run…)   │
╰──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────╯
 ✦ Refine ctrl+r   ⌕ Search ctrl+p   ▦ Panels ctrl+o              ctrl+c close
 ~/playwright-csharp-pom-framework   azure/gpt-5.4 (default) ▾  ·  57.4K tokens  ·  ForgeQE
  • > is what you asked. ⏺ starts each reply, tool call, browser run, test case, and result; calls in a row sit together, with the arguments dimmed. What came of a call is indented under it after ⎿: the agent's notes, each browser step and its actions (✓ or ✗), and a result's details.
  • While a request runs, one line above the input says what is happening, for how long, how far a browser run or suite has got, and with how many tokens. The footer is the status line, and the one place for the folder, the model and its profile, and what the session used.
  • PgUp and PgDn scroll the conversation from the prompt (the mouse wheel works too); PgDn back at the end follows new lines again.
  • A long request you sent (a refined one, a pasted spec) shows its first lines and … 27 more lines, sent in full. Prices are not shown in the conversation; reports and forgeqe models have them.
  • A result that made nothing (a run that only looked something up) adds no card.

The panels. ▦ Panels (Ctrl-O) splits the screen in half on a terminal 120 columns wide or more: the Activities column on the right, and the conversation with the files, context, and tokens panels on the left. On a narrower terminal the panels take the conversation's place. Ctrl-O again, or ✕ Close, closes them.

The Activities column shows what the agents do as they do it:

  • the status line: who is working, a progress bar for the plan or a suite's cases, for how long, and ■ Stop; under it, the whole width, what is happening now (Agent: Azure DevOps API · QI-Payments/_apis/build/builds…);
  • Plan: the agent's own task list as soon as it writes one, then each browser step or suite case: ○ pending, a spinner while in progress, ✓ done, ✗ failed and why. ForgeQE's agent writes a plan for any request of three steps or more and updates it as it goes;
  • Activity: Now (the task in progress) and Next (the next task), then every tool call, browser step, and note. A call shows a spinner the moment it starts and becomes ✓ or ✗ in the same row when it ends, so a long API query or test run is never invisible. Reads and searches that the conversation leaves out at the brief detail level are listed here. A call takes one row and a note up to three; focus the panel to scroll through everything or filter it. Each request starts under a line with its time and what you asked (── 14:02 can you generate a defect report… ──) and ends with how it went (✓ Done in 1m 12s · 16 activities, or ✗ Failed, ■ Stopped, ? Waiting for your answer), so one request's activities are never read as another's; earlier requests stay above, to scroll back to. The Plan shows the current request only.

Over the conversation: Files (each file read ◉, written ✚, or edited ✎, newest first), Context (how full the model's context window is, and the skills, product knowledge, domain, and MCP servers in it), and Tokens (total, input, output, cached). When that half is under 80 columns they fold into one line: Files 4 (1 changed) · Context 26% · Skills 2 · 52.9K tokens.

Buttons. Under the input: ✦ Refine (Ctrl-R; it fills in once there is text to refine), ⌕ Search (Ctrl-P, the command palette), and ▦ Panels (Ctrl-O); while a request runs, ■ Stop (Esc) takes Refine's place. With the panels open, ▦ Focus panels (Shift-Tab) and ✕ Close (Ctrl-O) are in their status line. On a narrow terminal, ◧ Details (Ctrl-O) and ⇄ Profile (Tab) are under the input. Each is clickable.

Replies. The agent's answers lead with the answer, then short headed sections; lists keep their bullets in a column, so a wrapped line stays under its text instead of running back to the margin.

Moving between panels. With the panels open, Shift-Tab (or a click) moves the keys from the prompt to Plan, Activity, then Files; the focused panel's border lights up and shows its keys. Esc, or typing, goes back to the prompt.

PanelKeys
Plan↑/↓ select a step, Enter puts the whole step, with why it failed, in the chat
Activity↑/↓, PgUp/PgDn scroll; a all, e errors, s steps (browser steps and reasoning), t tools (tool calls and files); the filter buttons are clickable too
Files↑/↓ select, Enter (or a click) opens the file

Links. Report folders, report files, and web addresses in the chat are links: Cmd-click (Ctrl-click on Windows and Linux) opens them in terminals that support links, such as VS Code, iTerm2, Windows Terminal, and recent GNOME Terminal. A path that no longer exists stays plain text.

Setup checklist. Until everything is set up, the start screen shows how many of the five things most features rest on are set up: Model (a live model profile), Coding agent, Work items (Azure DevOps, Jira, TestRail, Zephyr, qTest, or Xray), Domain, and Notifications. In a terminal 110 columns or wider it is the sidebar's Get set up: each finished item with what it found, and the next one as a card with ▸ Set up (its steps in the chat; for Work items, it runs /auth) and skip. A skipped item is kept in .forgeqe/setup.json and not asked about again. Narrower, it is one Setup line with ▸ how to set up and skip. The checks are the same ones /features uses, so the two always agree.

Sidebar and rail. The sidebar has three short groups. Work: Home, This session (once you have asked something), Runs, Reports and Jobs. Knowledge: Knowledge (history and what ForgeQE has kept), Page memory, Test cases, Guardrails and Skills. System: Settings and Doctor. Each opens a page in the chat's place, laid out for what it shows, with its keys at the foot: tables to move through with ↑↓, and open ↗ links that open a report or a skill in the browser. Test cases lives in your tracker, so its page offers the requests that fetch it and puts the one you choose in the composer to read before sending, beside the suite files in the repository. esc goes back. Models, sign-ins and environments are a command away (/model, /auth, /env; /help lists them all), and ? help and docs ↗ are at the top right of the start screen. Beside the chat the sidebar is a column with its labels from 194 columns, and a rail of its icons below that, so the start screen is always one click away: Home (or /home) shows it with the session still open, and ▸ Back to the session or any request returns to it.

Needs you and Today. Once there are runs, the start screen shows what needs you (runs that failed this week and have not passed since, and jobs whose last run failed, each with open and rerun) and what ran today. The greeting says how many runs there were today and how many failed. The model is in the composer beside ⏎ Send: click it to change it. The status line shows the branch, the tracker, and when the next scheduled job runs.

Command palette. Ctrl-P opens a palette of every action, command, profile, and example request; type a few letters to find one ("rr" finds Refine my request, "auth" finds the sign-in commands), ↑/↓ to choose, Enter to run it. Example requests are put in the prompt for you to edit, never sent straight away.

/ and @ in the prompt. Typing / lists the commands that match; typing @ suggests references: recent runs (last run, then each by its folder), files in the repository by name or path (@Login finds pages/Login.ts), and, after @ and a number such as @1761, the Azure DevOps work item with its title (read-only). Tab completes, ↑/↓ choose, Esc closes the list.

Refine. Ctrl-R (or Refine my request in the palette) turns what you typed into a task specification for ForgeQE's agent, laid out as a system prompt and written in ASD-STE100 Simplified Technical English, before it is sent. It has these parts, in this order: Role (who the agent acts as), Objective (one instruction), Context (the facts, with names), Steps (only when they add something), Rules (your limits and the setup's), Output format (what comes back and how it is laid out), and Summary (what you get, in one sentence, read last):

text
Role: A release analyst who reads Azure DevOps deployments.

Objective: Get what is deployed now in each environment of each Azure DevOps project.

Context:
- System: Azure DevOps organization devcgazuredevops.
- Projects: Autothon, CQube.AI, BlackBox_AI, QI-Payments, QI-MobileBanking, Atlas.Ai, QI-Claims.

Output format: One section for each project, with a line for each environment: the deployed build, its date, and its status.

Summary: Show the deployed build in each environment, and why each deployment failed.

Refine is one model session of its own, separate from the conversation: a few model steps (usually two) and read-only tool calls, typically 15K–25K tokens and 20–30 seconds. The panel title shows what it cost and how much of the request keeps the ASD-STE100 rules (✦ Refined request · STE 100% · 20.8K tokens · 21s), and its tokens count in the session total in the footer.

A read-only agent writes it. It starts knowing every capability and whether it works in this setup, and the domain; it checks names and ids in the systems that are set up (work items, test plans, pipelines, sprints, recent runs). The panel shows the request with what it checked ✓, what it assumed ~, and what is missing ? (for example "Which story? Give the work item id"); a capability that is not set up shows as missing too. What is missing comes first, and the title counts it (✦ Refined request · 2 to answer). A refined request longer than the screen scrolls inside the panel with ↑/↓ and PgUp/PgDn (the border says so); the title and the Use refined, Edit, and Keep mine buttons stay on screen. ←/→ or Tab picks one, Enter does it; e puts the request in the prompt to edit, Esc keeps yours. Refine never runs anything, writes files, or changes a work item; it never invents an id, URL, project, or file.

Next steps. When a request finishes, up to three suggestions appear above the prompt: from what it produced (Open the report, Write a test for this run, Why did it fail?, Rerun the cases that failed) and from the agent's answer, which suggests the follow-ups you are likely to want (Write a release report, Show the failed CD logs). Click one or press Alt-1 to Alt-3; suggestions that start work are put in the prompt first, to adjust and send.

KeyAction
EnterSend the message or command
TabNext LLM profile from llms.json; the conversation carries over to the new model. While / or @ suggestions show, completes the selected one
Shift-TabMove between the chat and the Plan, Activity, and Files panels
PgUp / PgDnScroll the conversation
Ctrl-PCommand palette
Ctrl-RRefine the request in the prompt
Ctrl-OOpen or close the panels (beside the conversation, or in its place on a narrow terminal)
Alt-1 to Alt-3Take a suggested next step
Alt-1 to Alt-4On the start screen: put a Start with action in the prompt
?On an empty prompt: every key, in one list
EscAsk to stop the run: Stop run, Stop and close ForgeQE, or Keep running. In a panel, back to the prompt
Ctrl-CAsk to close ForgeQE: Close or Cancel. A second Ctrl-C closes straight away

In the button prompt, ←/→ or Tab move between buttons, Enter confirms, Esc cancels, and a mouse click works too. esc stop and ctrl+c close in the bar under the prompt are also clickable. Stop and close waits until the browser has shut down and the run's files are saved, then closes ForgeQE.

The web app

forgeqe web is the shell in your browser: a chat on Home with the plan, each agent's steps and handoffs, the live activity, and the results (tests, reports, diffs) as they happen; pages for recording, regression packs, API tests, quality, data, runs, reports and schedules; and every setting as a form, with keys kept in your credential store. It listens on 127.0.0.1 only and needs the token in the address it opens and prints.

bash
forgeqe web                  # a free port, opened in your browser
forgeqe web --port 4711      # a fixed port
forgeqe web --no-open        # only print the address

The first page is Settings › Set up: the project folder (an existing clone, or a new folder), ForgeQE in it (the same as forgeqe init), what this computer needs, and a model. See docs/WEB_APP.md for every page.

Without the shell

bash
forgeqe run "Search hotels in Mumbai on booking.com" --profile azure-gpt4o --plain
forgeqe run "Run the cases in smoke.yaml and don't post results" --plain

forgeqe run sends the request to the same agent and prints its tool work and its final reply. Exit codes: 0 done, 1 failed, 2 stopped (Ctrl-C), 3 the agent needs more information; run again with the details.

--direct skips the agent and runs the text as one browser goal, which is also what happens without the agent runtime:

bash
forgeqe run --direct "Search hotels in Mumbai on booking.com" --profile azure-gpt4o --plain --out ./.forgeqe/reports/demo

Exit codes for a direct run: 0 complete, 1 failed, 2 stopped. For test cases in a pipeline, use forgeqe suite (section 7), which runs the same way every time.

Each run gets its own folder, named by its run id (the start time), under .forgeqe/reports/ (export.out_dir). forgeqe init adds .forgeqe/reports/ to .gitignore, and so does the first agent command in a project set up before that. A live run's folder contains:

  • run.json: result, summary, token counts, visited URLs, the judge's check of the result (when judge: true), and how the browser was set up
  • workflow.yaml: compact steps (forgeqe-v2-compact) with semantic locators
  • trace.md: task, per-step result, failure trace
  • steps.json and steps/: each step with a screenshot
  • report.html: the run's report
  • failure.png: last screenshot, only when the run failed

A live agent for programs (forgeqe chat --jsonl)

A program that needs what the shell has (one live agent per conversation, follow-ups, credentials remembered for the session, Your turn and questions, stop), such as a web UI or an IDE, starts forgeqe chat --jsonl and talks to it in JSON lines. It is the shell's agent and the shell's events, not a screen to scrape. ForgeQE serves no HTTP here: the program that starts the process owns transport, users, and sign-in.

bash
forgeqe chat --jsonl [--repo DIR | --no-code] [--profile NAME] [--url URL] [--headed]
                     [--browser-provider local|docker|browserstack|testmu] [--devices a,b] [--max-steps N]
                     [--replay MODE] [--no-memory] [--carry-over FILE] [--config forgeqe.yaml] [--verbose] [--debug]

The flags mean what they mean for forgeqe run and forgeqe start. --jsonl is required (the only format so far). --carry-over FILE is a JSON list [{"user": "...", "reply": "..."}, ...]: the turns of an earlier conversation whose process has gone (a server closing idle chats). The first message then reaches the agent with those turns before it, as when you switch profiles in the shell. To change profiles, start a new process with --profile and --carry-over. --verbose also writes readable event lines to stderr.

The process opens ForgeQE's agent on the first message and keeps it for every later one; it ends it on close, the end of stdin, or a signal. It works like the shell: background monitors are allowed and stop when it exits, and passed runs become tests unless --no-code. A request that was run before and passed is run again as recorded, without the agent, as in the shell (Tier 0, registry.mode); its turn ends with the result as the reply. When the agent cannot run (no model, no agent runtime), the process writes fatal and exits 1 before it reads anything: it never falls back to a browser goal.

stdout carries only JSON lines: one object per line, UTF-8, ending in \n. Logs, warnings, and anything a library prints go to stderr. Lines can be long (a card, a suite result); do not assume a maximum length. Every line has "v": 1, "seq" (1, 2, 3, … in this process), and "t" (ISO time). Every string is passed through the session's secret redaction, so a password given in a message never comes back out, not even inside a tool result; profile keys and the values of ForgeQE's own secret variables never appear.

Input (stdin), one JSON object per line:

typeFieldsMeaning
messageid (your turn id), textA new user message. Refused with error busy while a turn runs
answerid (from an ask), choice (an option id, or null to cancel)Answers the pending question
continueid (from a wait), ok (true = Done, continue; false = Cancel)Ends the pending wait
stopStops the running turn (Esc or /stop in the shell). Nothing when idle
settingsheaded and/or writeTests (each true or false, nothing else)Changes the chat's choices from now on: whether browser runs are shown (as --headed), and whether passed runs become tests (as --no-code, off). The next browser run uses them; the agent is told of a change with your next message. Answered with settings
closeStops any turn, closes the agent, stops monitors and the containers it started, writes closed, exits 0

A line that is not JSON, has an unknown type, or misses a field gets error bad_input with the reason and the first 200 characters of the line; the process goes on. The end of stdin is the same as close.

Output (stdout):

typeFieldsWhen
readyprotocol (1), version, cwd, writeTests, headed, profile (name and provider, never a key)Once, after the config is loaded and checked, before stdin is read
turn_startidA message was accepted
settingsheaded, writeTestsAfter a settings line: the choices now in force
agentid, event: a CodeEvent (stream, say, start, tool, plan, usage, context, test, info, …)While the turn runs
toolid, event: a ToolEvent (run-start, run-event, run-done, suite-*, card, info, agent, code-*, …)While the turn runs; id is null for a background monitor between turns
askid (ask-N), turn, question (title, detail, lines, options[{id, label}])A tool asks you to choose
waitid (wait-N), turn, promptA tool waits for you to do something by hand
turn_doneid, outcome (done, failed, needs_input, stopped), reply (the visible reply), next (follow-ups), error (when there is one), usage (the agent's usage so far, null before it opened)The turn ended, whatever the way
errorcode (bad_input, busy, unknown_id, internal), message, turn (when one runs)A problem that does not end the process
fatalmessageThe process cannot go on; exit code 1 follows
closedThe last line before a clean exit

CodeEvent and ToolEvent are passed through as they are: their types in src/types.ts and src/mcp/tools.ts are the contract. Rules:

  • Every turn that got turn_start gets exactly one turn_done, also after an error (outcome: "failed" with the error).
  • One ask and one wait can be pending at a time. answer or continue with an id that is not pending gets error unknown_id, and nothing else happens.
  • stop and close answer a pending ask with null and a pending wait with false, then stop the turn, which ends with turn_done stopped.
  • If the agent fails to start on a message, that turn ends failed with the error, and the next message tries again.
  • Exit codes: 0 after closed, 1 after fatal, 2 after SIGINT or SIGTERM (the turn is stopped and the agent closed first).

A complete exchange (→ stdout, ← stdin; t left out):

text
→ {"v":1,"seq":1,"type":"ready","protocol":1,"version":"2.3.11","cwd":"/srv/studio/users/ana/workspace","writeTests":true,"headed":false,"profile":{"name":"copilot","provider":"github_copilot"}}
← {"type":"message","id":"m1","text":"Run SHOP-12 on staging"}
→ {"v":1,"seq":2,"type":"turn_start","id":"m1"}
→ {"v":1,"seq":3,"type":"agent","id":"m1","event":{"type":"code","kind":"stream","id":"p1","text":"I'll run SHOP-12"}}
→ {"v":1,"seq":4,"type":"agent","id":"m1","event":{"type":"code","kind":"start","text":"forgeqe_run_test_cases","tool":"forgeqe_run_test_cases","id":"c1"}}
→ {"v":1,"seq":5,"type":"tool","id":"m1","event":{"type":"run-start","goal":"SHOP-12: Checkout with a saved card"}}
→ {"v":1,"seq":6,"type":"ask","id":"ask-1","turn":"m1","question":{"title":"Which sprint?","options":[{"id":"s41","label":"Sprint 41"},{"id":"s42","label":"Sprint 42"}]}}
← {"type":"answer","id":"ask-1","choice":"s42"}
→ {"v":1,"seq":7,"type":"tool","id":"m1","event":{"type":"card","card":{"status":"passed","title":"SHOP-12","stats":[],"body":"Checkout with a saved card passed in 41 s."}}}
→ {"v":1,"seq":8,"type":"turn_done","id":"m1","outcome":"done","reply":"SHOP-12 passed on staging.","next":[{"label":"Write a test for it","request":"Write a test for SHOP-12"}],"usage":{"input":5200,"output":310}}
← {"type":"close"}
→ {"v":1,"seq":9,"type":"closed"}
04

Browser

Goals carried out in a real browser, and the logins they use

Browser

Run any goal in a real browser

Carries out a plain-language goal in a real browser one step at a time and verifies the outcome, with screenshots, a report, and the steps it took. The browser comes from forgeqe.yaml: local Chrome or Chromium by default, or BrowserStack or TestMu AI/LambdaTest when configured. Page memory replays steps that already worked on a site, so repeat runs are faster and cheaper.

Ask it
  • Go to booking.com and search hotels in Mumbai for next weekend
  • What does ForgeQE know about the checkout page?
  • Add Chrome on Windows 11 and Chrome 128 on Windows 10 as TestMu devices, then run SHOP-12 on both
What it needs
  • RequiredA live model profile in llms.json (an API key or Microsoft Entra ID sign-in)
Limits
  • Web applications only: local Chrome or Chromium, or configured remote web grids such as BrowserStack and TestMu AI/LambdaTest; not native mobile or desktop applications.
Tools

browser_run run_details page_memory browser_devices

In detail

See 4. Without the shell.

Page memory

Live runs build a memory of the pages they visit, next to forgeqe.yaml. With no config file it goes to ~/.forgeqe/memory (under FORGEQE_HOME when that is set), so runs from any folder share one memory.

yaml
memory:
  enabled: true
  dir: ./.forgeqe/memory
  replay: verify # verify | trust | off
text
.forgeqe/memory/
  pages/<host>/<path>.json   every interactive element on the page and its locators
  flows/<goal>-<hash>.json   the steps of a goal that passed, for replay
  scribe-notes.json          what the coding agent learned about each repo, and the tests it wrote there

Each page file lists its elements with every locator ForgeQE can build for them: test id, id, role and name, label, placeholder, alt, title, href, text, CSS, and XPath. Each locator has a Playwright string, a confidence %, and its evidence: how many visits it matched on, how often it went missing, and how often it worked or failed when used. Elements and pages carry first_seen, last_seen, last_used, and last_updated. Paths with ids in them share one file (/hotel/*), and query strings are ignored.

Confidence starts from the locator kind (a test id or role and name ranks above text, CSS, and XPath). It is lower when the locator matches more than one element or looks generated (#ember123, hashed class names). It rises as the locator keeps matching over visits and works when used, and it drops when the locator goes missing or fails.

How memory saves time and tokens:

  • Replay. Running a goal that passed before replays its recorded steps directly in the browser, with no model calls. A different goal replays nothing: the agent gets the steps of earlier runs on the site, and the controls that worked there, as hints, and decides itself which of them (a banner, a sign-in, a search form) apply to its task, so a new task does not re-explore the setup. Each step finds its element by the highest-confidence locator that still matches exactly one element, then by the element's fingerprint, so a renamed button or a changed id still resolves. The transcript marks these steps ↻ and shows the locator used (click "Search" [role 88%]).
  • The site's rulebook. Page memory keeps what worked; the rulebook keeps what did not. When an action fails during a run and the agent then finds one that works, it may end its answer with a line or two starting Lesson: ("On the booking page, type the date in the Check-in field; clicking a day in the calendar does nothing"). ForgeQE keeps a lesson only when that run passed, in rules/<host>.json in page memory, and every later run that starts on that site gets the rules, so it does not spend steps on the same dead end. The lesson lines are taken out of the run's summary. The book works like Scribe's rulebook (under Keeping Scribe's cost down): at most 15 one-line rules, merged when learned again, the least confirmed and oldest dropped first, expired after 180 days unconfirmed, and a rule with a link, a token, a destructive command, or wording that drops instructions is refused. ForgeQE says each new rule (Learned for this site: …). browser.rulebook: false turns it off; with page memory off there is none.
  • Hand-off. With replay: verify (the default) the agent then looks at the page once and confirms the result. replay: trust finishes without the model when every step of this same task replayed. When a step cannot be found, the agent continues from that point instead of starting over. replay: off records memory but never replays.
  • Failed runs. A run that fails keeps the steps that worked before it went wrong (resume.json in its run folder, and in the task's flow). Running the same task again replays them and the agent picks up at the point of failure, told what went wrong last time. When ForgeQE's agent retries a failed goal with different wording, it passes the failed run (resume_from), so the retry starts from the same point instead of step 1.
  • Cut-off runs. After every step, a run saves the steps that worked so far and the page memory it has learned, so nothing is lost if ForgeQE is closed, the machine restarts, or the run is stopped. Running the same task again replays those steps and the agent carries on from there; the folder of the cut-off run also has resume.json, so resume_from works with it too. When the model can't be reached (the connection dropped), the run ends as failed and keeps its steps the same way. A run that finishes removes the saved point.
  • Hints. The agent's prompt includes the steps that worked last time on the same site and the controls those runs used, and tells the agent to do them in one turn instead of exploring the page.
  • Tests. The coding agent gets each element's best locator from memory in handoff.md.

A run that fails keeps the last good flow and counts the failure. A stopped run changes nothing. Password fields are never stored, and a value typed from FORGEQE_ENGINE_DEFAULT_LOGIN_USERNAME or FORGEQE_ENGINE_DEFAULT_LOGIN_PASSWORD is stored as the variable name and read from the environment on replay. Replay also stops before a step whose value cannot be restored, and the agent does it.

bash
forgeqe memory                              # hosts with page, element, and flow counts
forgeqe memory show booking.com             # the host's pages
forgeqe memory show https://app.test/login  # elements, confidence, best locator
forgeqe memory show https://app.test/login --locators --all
forgeqe memory flows --steps
forgeqe memory clear booking.com            # prints what it would delete
forgeqe memory clear booking.com --yes
forgeqe memory clear courier --yes          # a mobile or desktop app's memory
forgeqe memory stats                        # how often memory stood in for the model, per area

In the shell, ask ("what does page memory know about booking.com?") and the agent looks it up. For one run, --replay verify|trust|off overrides the config and --no-memory turns memory off.

Commit .forgeqe/memory to share it with a team or CI, or add .forgeqe/ to .gitignore to keep it local.

Browser

Saved logins for the application under test

Signs the browser agent in with saved credentials (including TOTP) and reuses the session; a user can also sign in by hand once in a browser.

Ask it
  • Sign in to the admin site and keep the session
  • Forget the saved session for shop.example.com
What it needs

Nothing beyond ForgeQE itself.

Tools

logins

In detail

Signing in

yaml
logins:
  shop:
    url: https://staging.shop.test/login      # or domains: [https://staging.shop.test, https://*.shop.test]
    username_env: SHOP_USER
    password_env: SHOP_PASSWORD
    totp_env: SHOP_TOTP_SECRET                # optional: the authenticator secret (base32), for 2FA codes
    # save_session: false                     # sign in on every run

The browser agent is told which placeholders exist (shop_username, shop_password, shop_bu_2fa_code) and types them; the engine swaps in the real values only on the matching domains and masks them in everything the model sees. For TOTP, a fresh code is computed at the moment it is typed. After each step ForgeQE saves the site's cookies and local storage to .forgeqe/sessions/<name>.json (private to your user, with its own .gitignore), and the next run starts signed in.

Page memory records the variable names, not the values, so replays type the current values and fresh codes, and tests the coding agent writes read SHOP_PASSWORD from the environment.

Credentials in a message or test case. Paste a test case that says "log in as svc@corp.test with password …" and you do not need logins: at all. Before the first browser run, ForgeQE's agent remembers the credentials as a login for that site and its sign-in hosts (for Dynamics, the CRM host and login.microsoftonline.com). From then on:

  • goals say "sign in with the saved login dynamics" and never contain the password, so it is not in run.json, trace.md, report.html, or workflow.yaml;
  • the browser agent types the exact value from a placeholder, so quotes or a full stop around the password in the test case cannot end up in the password field;
  • the signed-in session is saved in .forgeqe/sessions/, so follow-up runs ("now open the latest case") start signed in;
  • tests Scribe writes read FORGEQE_<NAME>_USERNAME and FORGEQE_<NAME>_PASSWORD, and ForgeQE sets them when it runs those tests in this session.

ForgeQE keeps the values in memory for the session only: it does not write them to disk or put them in the agent runtime's environment. Your message itself is still part of the conversation, which the agent runtime keeps locally like any chat, and which the model provider receives. Close ForgeQE and the remembered values are gone (the saved session stays until it expires or you clear it). Ask "which logins do I have?" to see them, or "forget the dynamics login" to drop them. For pipelines and later sessions, use logins: with environment variables.

Single sign-on, captchas, and security keys need a person: forgeqe login shop opens a browser on the login page, you sign in, press Enter in the terminal, and the session is saved. forgeqe logins lists logins, which variables are missing, and when each session was saved; forgeqe logins clear shop (or --all) forgets sessions. In the shell, "sign in to shop" does the same: the browser opens, a Your turn prompt waits while you sign in, and Done, continue saves the session. forgeqe run asks you to press Enter instead, and forgeqe mcp cannot wait for a person, so it tells the calling agent to have you run forgeqe login shop. In a pipeline, set the login variables as secrets.

Browser

Record a web test by hand

Opens a browser with the Recorder panel docked in the page while the user does the steps of a web test themself, and saves their clicks, typing, choices and keys in order as a run, with the checks, kept values, test data and notes they add; the coding agent then turns it into a test in the repository's framework.

Ask it
  • I'll show you the checkout steps in the shop: record them
  • Record my steps on the cart page and make a test of it
What it needs
  • OptionalThe coding agent, Scribe: a live model profile (or code.model) and the agent runtime, which forgeqe setup installs
Limits
  • Local Chrome or Chromium only, one recording at a time; drag and drop and steps inside iframes are not recorded yet
Tools

web_record

In detail

Recording a web test by hand

When you would rather show the steps than describe them, record them. ForgeQE opens Chrome on the app's page with the Recorder panel docked in a corner of the page, in the same window: you click, type and choose as usual, and each action is listed in the panel as a numbered step, in order. Drag the panel by its title to another corner, or make it small (a pill that shows the step count); it comes back where you left it on every page.

bash
forgeqe record shop --name "Apply a coupon at checkout"     # a web app from env.yaml
forgeqe record --url https://staging.shop.test/cart         # or any page

In the shell, say "I'll show you the checkout, record it" (or /record shop). What you can add while recording:

In the panelIn the page: Ctrl+Shift+Space, thenWhat it adds
Check, then click an elementAA check: its text, or its value for a field, or checked for a checkbox. Change the kind (text, visible, hidden, value) and mark live data (check the pattern, not this value) in the panel
Variable, then click an elementVKeeps the element's text for later steps: typing the same text later is written as {{name}}
Wait for, then click an elementWThe test waits for the element to show
Test dataDThe last value you typed becomes test data the test reads from its data
note: … in the boxNA note on the last step: "this is the cart page object"
Pause / Undo / Stop, ✕ on a stepP / Z / SPause records nothing until you resume; ✕ removes that step

Type a check in words in the panel's box, or in the terminal: the total shows "$40.00". Quoted text the page shows becomes a check on the element showing it; without quotes, one model call finds the text that shows it on the page. A check the page cannot show yet is kept in your words, and the coding agent writes it. In the terminal, undo, pause, delete 4 and stop work too.

What is recorded: clicks (a double click as one step), the final text of each field you typed in (not each key), choices in drop-downs, Enter and Escape, key chords, pages you open from the address bar, and Back. A click on a menu item that opened when the pointer went over its trigger gets a hover on the trigger first. Password fields are written as an environment variable (the saved login's password_env, or <SITE>_PASSWORD), and text equal to a saved login's value as its variable: what you type in them is never stored. A file you choose for an upload is recorded by its path in the working folder; one outside it, a hidden file (.env, anything under .git), or one named like credentials or keys is listed as not recorded. Drag and drop is listed as not recorded. --signed-in starts with the saved sessions of logins:; without it, record the sign-in too.

Data that changes from run to run. A test that spells out this recording's price, date, or order number fails on the next run, so ForgeQE keeps what holds and makes the rest a pattern, a variable, or a value made fresh:

In the recordingIn the test
A check of a price, date, time, order number, or count ("$14.15", "2,463 found")A check of its pattern: /^\$\d[\d,]*\.\d{2}$/
The same value checked again later (the product's price, then the cart total)The first check keeps it as {{price}}; the later one checks {{price}}
A link or button named with such data ("Combination Pliers $14.15")Found by the part that holds: getByRole("link", { name: /Combination Pliers/ })
A date you typedThe date the same number of days from today: {{$date:+3:%Y-%m-%d}}
A value the app refuses twice (a new account's email)A part new on each run: alice+{{$unique}}@example.com

Prices, dates, times, ids, and long numbers are found by their form; a small number on its own ("1" in the cart) stays exact, as it is usually what the check is about. When you stop, one model call sorts the values the forms cannot tell (a new account's email, "today's deal"); without a model, those stay as recorded. Each change shows in the panel with a tick box: untick it to keep the value as recorded.

Recording a test case. forgeqe record --case SHOP-12 (any source ForgeQE reads cases from: Jira, Azure DevOps, Xray, TestRail, a file) starts on the case's page and shows its steps as a checklist in the panel. Click the step you are on (or Next case step): what you record belongs to it, and a check you add there takes its expected result. Each expected result shows ✓ checked or no check yet; Stop warns once about the ones with no check, and a second Stop stops. The test is named and tagged for the case, and mapped to it once it passes.

When you stop, the run folder holds workflow.yaml and trace.md as for any run. With a repository (--repo, or code.repo), ForgeQE asks whether to turn it into a test. First a planner reads the repository and shows the plan:

text
Test      tests/add-to-cart.spec.ts › adds pliers to the cart
Steps
   2. search: HomePage.searchFor()
   4. open the product: new HomePage.product(name)
   6. add to the cart: new ProductPage.addToCart()
New code
  ProductPage in pages/ProductPage.ts
Checks
  after step 5: expect(product.price).toHaveText(/^\$\d+\.\d{2}$/)
  after step 6: expect(product.cartQuantity).toHaveText("1")
