It asks at most three questions where the repository allows two good answers (a new file, or the existing spec?), then Write it, Change the plan (say what to change, in your words), or Cancel. The coding agent replays the recording in Playwright, writes the test to the plan you agreed in the repository's framework, and asserts each check at the step where you added it. In the shell, "change the plan" comes back to ForgeQE's agent, which asks you what to change. --no-plan (or --yes) skips the plan. Later: forgeqe code <run folder>.

The recorder runs in an isolated world in each page, so the page's own scripts can neither see it nor add steps, and it records only real input: clicks and keys a script fakes are ignored. The panel's own clicks and keys never reach the page and are never recorded, and it stays above the page's dialogs. Recording uses local Chrome or Chromium.

05

Test cases

Run, check, write, and explore manual test cases

Test cases

Run manual test cases in browsers

Reads test cases from Azure DevOps, Jira, Xray, TestRail, Zephyr Scale, qTest, a file, or pasted text, runs each in a real browser with a verdict per step, writes report.html and junit.xml, posts results back, and files or updates bugs for failures.

Ask it
  • Run SHOP-12 and SHOP-14 from Jira, two browsers at once
  • Run test plan 12 suite 34 from Azure DevOps and don't post results
What it needs
  • RequiredA live model profile in llms.json (an API key or Microsoft Entra ID sign-in)
  • OptionalAzure DevOps or Jira or Xray or TestRail or Zephyr Scale or qTest, to read cases from a test management system and post results back (files and pasted text work without one): set up under sources: in forgeqe.yaml, or sign in with /auth
  • OptionalBug filing: sources.bugs in forgeqe.yaml (Jira or Azure DevOps)
Tools

find_test_cases run_test_cases

In detail

Test cases and suites

Test cases you already have can be run two ways. In the shell or with forgeqe run, ask in plain words ("run SHOP-12 and SHOP-14", "run test plan 12 suite 34", "run the cases in smoke.yaml") or paste the cases, and the agent works out the source. forgeqe suite takes explicit references and runs the same way every time, with no agent involved, which is what pipelines call. Each case is one goal: ForgeQE tells the agent to do the steps in order and to check every expected result, and the agent's verdict decides whether the case passed. A case with no steps (plain text, a Jira story) is run from its description.

API steps in a test case

A test case can mix API and UI steps ("add three products with POST /add_to_cart/{id}, then remove the first one in the cart, then pay"). The browser agent makes each API step with its api_request action, from the page, in the browser's own session: the logged-in user's cookies go with the call, and the CSRF token comes from the cookie or the page's form when the site needs one. So what an API step changes (a cart, an order) is what the next UI step sees.

  • The verdict of an API step rests on the status the server answered, not on the agent's account of it.
  • The run records it as an api step in workflow.yaml: method, URL, body, CSRF source, and the status and final URL the server gave.
  • ForgeQE's replay repeats it the same way, and fails that step when the server answers differently (403 where the run got 200), so the replay and the test get past it instead of stalling on an empty cart.
  • Scribe's handoff lists each API step with what was sent and answered, and tells Scribe to make it through the framework's API request context that shares the browser's cookies (Playwright's page.request), asserting the status.
  • Kept for reuse: the calls are saved as an API scenario, one per test case (out/api/case-ado-2287/, updated by each run), which forgeqe api and the API tools run again without a browser. The run's card says where (API 2 calls saved as a reusable API scenario).

URLs the case gives are opened exactly as given: when the model adds a trailing slash (/login/, a 404 on many sites), ForgeQE opens and records the URL from the case.

When a run finds a test case does not fit the application, the agent drafts the steps as they should be, from what the run found, and offers to save them. update_test_case shows the current and the new steps and asks before it changes the Azure DevOps Test Case; the reason goes into the work item's history, where the old steps stay.

When a case fails

The browser run of a test case is the test, so when a case does not pass, ForgeQE works out what kind of failure it was and acts on it. The browser agent reports a cause for each failed step (it saw the page); clear evidence in the run, such as an unreachable site or a 503 page, overrides "I could not operate it".

FailureWhat it meansBugAutomation script
Functional defectEvery step was done and the application behaves wrong (a wrong total, no confirmation)Filed straight awayWritten for the correct behaviour (the test case's expected results) and marked as an expected failure naming the bug: Playwright test.fail(), pytest xfail(strict=True). It passes while the defect is there and fails once it is fixed, telling you to remove the marker
Test case needs updatingThe test case does not fit the application (a field or page it names is not there)NoNo: update the test case. The report and the comment on the test case say what the application shows instead
Environment problemThe application or something it needs was unavailable (an error page, a 5xx, a network failure, sign-in or test data missing, a bot check)NoNo. The case runs once more straight away; if it still fails, check the environment first
Automation problemA control that is there could not be operated, or the run ran out of stepsNoNot yet: page memory keeps the steps that worked and the next run resumes from them

In Azure DevOps a test plan point is recorded as Blocked, not Failed, for the last three, so the plan's failures are product defects, and the comment on the test case names the kind of failure.

Tests that need attention. A coding job that ends without a passing, approved test does not leave a failing test among your tests. The files it created move to a needs-attention/<case id>/ folder beside the test, with their relative imports fixed, and a README says which test case it is for, why it stopped (the last failure, the reviewer's issues), and how to pick it up; needs-attention/README.md lists them all. The job's edits to files that were committed before it are saved as changes.patch in that folder and taken out of the files; files you had already changed are left as they are. The folder is kept out of test runs and builds: Playwright's config gets testIgnore: ['**/needs-attention/**'], TypeScript files a // @ts-nocheck line, a Python folder a conftest.py that collects nothing, and C#, Java, or Kotlin files a .txt suffix. code.needs_attention: false leaves unfinished tests where they are.

Running a case again. A case that already has a test (in .forgeqe/test-cases.json) does not get a new one: after its browser run, ForgeQE runs the existing test first. When it passes, there is no coding job at all. When it fails, the coding agent updates that test (with the failure and the new verified run) instead of writing one from scratch. A known-defect test whose defect is fixed (the case now passes) is updated to drop its expected-failure marker; a test that passes although the run found a defect gets the defect's assertion and the marker.

Mapping tests to test cases. Each test written for a test case is named <case id> <title> and tagged with the case id and link in the framework's own way (Playwright tags and annotations, a pytest marker, an NUnit property). .forgeqe/test-cases.json in the repository maps every test case to its test, with its status (automated, known_defect with the bug, or needs_attention with the folder); commit it with the tests. For Azure DevOps test cases, a passing test is also set as the work item's Associated Automation (test name, file, type, and a stable id, with Automation status Automated), so the test plan shows the case as automated.

In the shell

Ask for the cases in plain words, for example "run SHOP-12 and SHOP-14, two at a time" or "show me what's in test plan 12 without running it". The transcript shows each case's steps as it runs, and a suite card at the end:

text
┃  ✗ Suite failed                                     1/2 passed · 1m 12s · 48.2K tokens
┃
┃  ✓ SF-1  Search Mumbai  38s
┃  ✗ SF-2  Open a hotel  34s
┃    No hotel link on the results page
┃
┃  Report    .forgeqe/reports/suites/20260928-215438/report.html
┃  JUnit     .forgeqe/reports/suites/20260928-215438/junit.xml

Where cases come from

bash
forgeqe cases cases.yaml             # list them
forgeqe cases cases.yaml --goal      # and the exact goal each one runs

Plain text. A quoted string is one case: forgeqe suite "Log in as the demo user and open Invoices".

Text or Markdown file. Each block between --- lines is a case, its first line the title:

text
Search for hotels in Mumbai and check at least one result is shown
---
Open the Deals page
Check every deal has a price

YAML or JSON. A list, or {cases: [...]}:

yaml
- id: SHOP-12
  title: Valid login
  url: https://staging.shop.test/login   # start page (optional)
  preconditions: [The demo user exists]
  steps:
    - action: Sign in as demo@shop.test
      data: password from DEMO_PASSWORD
      expected: The dashboard greets the user by name
    - Open the Orders page                  # a step can be a plain string
  group: Accounts                           # optional: area for the Coverage page (or suite, area)
  requirements: [SHOP-3, SHOP-4]            # optional: stories it covers (or refs)

CSV. Header row, then a row per case. A row with an empty id (or the id above) adds a step to that case:

csv
id,title,url,action,expected
SHOP-12,Valid login,https://staging.shop.test/login,Sign in as demo,Dashboard shows
,,,Open Orders,Orders list shows

Columns: id (or key), title (or name, summary), url, description, preconditions, tags, group (or suite, area), requirements (or refs), action (or step), data, expected (or expected_result, result). Lists in a cell are separated by ;.

Gherkin. Each Scenario in a .feature file is a case, and each Examples row of a Scenario Outline is its own case. Background steps come first, and Then steps are the expected results of the step before them.

Azure DevOps. Set the organization, and a PAT with Test Management and Work Items (read and write) in ADO_PAT. The MCP server is the whole organization. Leave project unset (or set it to * or all) and ForgeQE's own calls cover every project; set project only to limit those calls to one.

yaml
sources:
  azure_devops:
    org_url: https://dev.azure.com/acme
    # project: Shop          # optional

ado:123,456 reads test case work items: their steps and expected results (HTML turned into text), shared steps inlined, and one case per parameter row with @param values filled in. Work item ids are unique in the organization, so they do not need a project. ado:plan=12 reads every suite in a test plan and ado:plan=12/suite=34 one suite, with each test point's configuration. With no project configured, the plan is looked up in every project. ado:project=Shop/plan=12 names one.

Jira. jira:SHOP-1,SHOP-2 or jira:jql=project = SHOP AND labels = e2e. The summary is the title and the description holds the steps. Jira Cloud (*.atlassian.net) needs JIRA_EMAIL and an API token in JIRA_API_TOKEN; Server and Data Center use a personal access token in JIRA_API_TOKEN.

yaml
sources:
  jira:
    url: https://acme.atlassian.net

Xray. xray:SHOP-1, xray:jql=<query>, or xray:plan=SHOP-100 for a test plan's tests, with Xray's own steps, data, and expected results. Xray Cloud needs an API key pair in XRAY_CLIENT_ID and XRAY_CLIENT_SECRET. Xray Server and Data Center go through the Jira settings above.

TestRail. testrail:C12,C13 reads cases by id, testrail:run=45 the tests of a run (results go back to that run), testrail:plan=7 every run in a plan, and testrail:suite=3 a suite (needs project_id). Separated steps (custom_steps_separated) become steps with expected results; the text template's steps become one step. It needs the user's email in TESTRAIL_EMAIL and an API key in TESTRAIL_API_KEY.

yaml
sources:
  testrail:
    url: https://acme.testrail.io
    project_id: 1          # needed for suites, and to create a run for cases read by id

Zephyr Scale (Cloud). zephyr:SHOP-T1,SHOP-T2, zephyr:cycle=SHOP-R5 (results go back to that cycle), or zephyr:folder=12 (needs project_key). Call-to-test steps are inlined. It needs an API access token in ZEPHYR_API_TOKEN; setting the variable is enough to turn it on.

qTest. qtest:1234 reads test cases by the id in their URL, qtest:cycle=88 and qtest:suite=9 the test runs under a cycle or suite (results go back to those runs). It needs url, project_id, and an API token in QTEST_TOKEN. Result statuses are the automation status names your qTest uses; the defaults are PASS, FAIL, and BLOCKED:

yaml
sources:
  qtest:
    url: https://acme.qtestnet.com
    project_id: 12345
    statuses: { passed: PASS, failed: FAIL, error: BLOCKED }

Several sources can be mixed: forgeqe suite smoke.yaml jira:SHOP-7 testrail:C12.

From requirements. Ask the agent to test a Confluence page, a story, or a spec file ("write and run test cases for the refund rules page in Confluence"). It reads the requirements, writes one case per rule or acceptance criterion with the main negative paths, shows them, and runs them as text cases. It saves them into a test system only when you ask. Confluence needs sources.confluence:

yaml
sources:
  confluence: true                  # the wiki on the Jira Cloud site above, same account
  # or
  confluence:
    url: https://confluence.acme.com   # Cloud: https://acme.atlassian.net/wiki
    token_env: CONFLUENCE_TOKEN        # Server/DC personal access token; Cloud also email_env
    space: QA                          # optional: keep searches in one space

Running

bash
forgeqe suite cases.yaml                       # one case at a time
forgeqe suite ado:plan=12 --parallel 3         # three browsers at once
forgeqe suite cases.yaml --repo ../app         # passed cases get a test in ../app
forgeqe suite cases.yaml --no-code --no-write-back --out .forgeqe/reports/smoke
yaml
suite:
  parallel: 1        # browsers at once
  code: true         # with a repo set, passed cases go to Scribe
  restore_sessions: false   # true: each case starts signed in from the saved logins' sessions
  out_dir: ./.forgeqe/reports/suites

Browser runs happen parallel at a time and share page memory safely. Each passed case goes to the coding agent as soon as its own browser run ends, and its test is written and run before that browser slot starts the next case: explore, hand off, code, next case. Coding jobs run one after another, so two never edit the repo at once. A case with no test says why in the report (it failed, or no model is set up). With --parallel above 1, each line of output starts with the case id. Ctrl-C stops the suite: cases already running finish closing their browsers, and cases that never started are reported as not run.

At the end, ForgeQE prints the result of every case and writes to .forgeqe/reports/suites/<time>/:

  • report.html: a single file with every screenshot embedded; open it from disk, no server needed. Its menu has three groups. This run:

    • Overview: the verdict, totals, step checks, browser steps, tokens, cost, what page memory saved, browser errors, accessibility issues, tests written and bugs filed; what changed since the last run; results over the earlier runs in .forgeqe/reports/suites/; the cases that need attention and why; results by source and by tag; the slowest cases; and what was posted where.
    • Test results: search and filter the cases. For each case: its step table (action, expected, the agent's verdict, what it actually saw), why it failed (the failed step, the agent's last evaluation, failed actions, the screenshot), last good vs now (the same page from the case's last passing run beside this run's, and where their browser steps start to differ), the browser errors per step (console errors, uncaught exceptions, failed and slow requests), page memory (steps replayed, tokens and time saved against the last run where the agent took every step, locators that healed, flows repaired), the browser agent's steps (goal, actions, errors, time and model time, a screenshot each with the element it acted on outlined), a timing waterfall (waiting for the model, acting in the browser, replayed from memory), the accessibility issues of each page, a screenshot gallery, and its trace, workflow and handoff to read in place. Run history links to earlier reports.
    • Failures: each failed case with what the agent reported, the evidence (including browser errors), its last passing screenshot, its bug, and text to copy into a bug report.
    • Defects: failures grouped by what caused them, so one fix clears every case it breaks, most cases broken first, with each defect's root-cause hypothesis when there is one; it downloads as Markdown.
    • Since last run: new failures, fixes, cases still failing, cases at least 5 s and 25% slower or faster, and new or dropped cases, against the previous run in the folder.

    Analysis:

    • Trends and Flaky tests: pass rate, outcomes, duration, tokens and cost per run; cases whose result keeps changing, and cases that started failing. Both need earlier suite runs in the same folder.
    • Time and cost: where the time went (model, browser, page memory), a timeline of the cases on each parallel worker, page memory savings per case, and tokens and cost per case.
    • Coverage: results by requirement (a requirement fails when any case covering it fails), by group, by tag and by source. Groups and requirements come from the source: the area path in Azure DevOps, the section and refs in TestRail, the folder in Zephyr Scale, components (or the parent issue) and "tests" links in Jira, and group / requirements in case files.
    • Accessibility: the WCAG rules of the standard you chose, checked by axe-core on every page the agent visited: the rules that passed, each rule that failed with its success criterion, level, impact, the elements and a link to how to fix it, and any accessibility budget exceeded. Automated checks are a pre-check, not conformance.
    • Visual: every page against its approved baseline: baseline, this run, and the difference side by side, changed pages first, with the share of pixels changed and the visual budget.
    • Performance: each page's Core Web Vitals (LCP, CLS, INP, FCP, TTFB) and load time, coloured good / needs improvement / poor by Google's thresholds, with the budgets each page went over.

    Tokenomics (reports with token usage):

    • Usage: a usage summary, tokens by case, tokens over the run, and the calls that used the most tokens.
    • Breakdown: what the tokens paid for, by agent, job, task, purpose, model, user and machine.
    • Model calls: every request the agents sent to a model.
    • Models: the models behind the calls, and what the listed alternatives would cost.
    • Trends and forecast: model spend on this machine so far, and the next days at the recent pace, against the budget.
    • Calculator: estimate what a suite will cost before you run it, starting from this run's real numbers.
    • Optimise: where the report could have spent less on models, by how much, and what to change.

    Environment opens from the model card at the foot of the menu: model, browser, the model's price, start URL, ForgeQE version, machine, CI build, branch, commit, and the sites visited.

    It exports Markdown and JSON and prints cleanly. Up to 40 MB of screenshots are embedded, failed cases first; beyond that they load from the run folders next to the report.

  • junit.xml: for CI test reporting; a failed case is a <failure>, a crash an <error>, a case not run <skipped>; step verdicts are in system-out

  • suite.json: everything above as data

  • 01-SHOP-12/, 02-…: each case's normal run folder (with its own report.html, steps.json, and steps/NN.jpg screenshots), plus testcase.json

yaml
report:
  screenshots: all     # all | failures (only failed runs keep step screenshots) | off
  accessibility: true  # check each page the agent visits; false skips the checks

Bugs filed for failed cases list the run's console errors and failed requests under "Browser errors".

Exit codes: 0 every case passed (and every test written for them passes), 1 anything failed, 2 stopped.

Results back in the source

Test cases the agent writes from an Azure DevOps or Jira story are saved there with save_test_cases, linked to the story, and the reply lists them as links; set sources.save_cases: false to keep them in the reply only. Replies in the shell render as markdown (headings, lists, tables, links).

With sources.write_back: true (the default), results go back after the suite:

  • Azure DevOps plan: a completed test run for the plan with each test point's outcome and the agent's summary as the comment. Cases read by id get a comment on the work item instead.
  • Jira: a comment on each issue with the result, the summary, where the run ended, and the test file if one was written.
  • Xray: one Test Execution per test plan (linked to the plan), with each test's status, step statuses and actual results, the summary, and the failure screenshot as evidence.
  • TestRail: results in the run the cases came from, or a new run per suite for cases read by id, with step results and the failure screenshot attached.
  • Zephyr Scale: a test execution per case with step results, in the cycle the cases came from or a new cycle per project.
  • qTest: an automation test log per case with step logs, on the test run it came from, or on new test runs in a new test cycle.

Every comment and result includes the step verdicts. If a system rejects the step details (a TestRail project without separated steps, say), ForgeQE posts the result again without them rather than losing it. Stopped cases are never posted. --no-write-back skips it for one suite.

Bugs for failures

yaml
sources:
  bugs:
    system: jira            # or azure_devops
    project: SHOP           # Jira project key; Azure DevOps uses azure_devops.project
    type: Bug               # issue type or work item type
    labels: [forgeqe]      # Jira labels or Azure DevOps tags
    # area_path: Shop\Web   # Azure DevOps
    # on_error: true        # also for cases that could not run
    # group_by: defect      # defect (default): cases failing the same way share a bug; case: a bug per case

When results are posted, each case that failed with a functional defect (below) gets a bug: the title is [ForgeQE] <case id>: <title>, the description has the result, the steps with verdicts, the preconditions, where the run ended, the build link, and the run folder, and the last screenshot is attached. In Jira the bug is linked to the Jira or Xray test ("relates to"); in Azure DevOps it is "tested by" the test case work item. Each bug carries a label or tag that fingerprints the case (forgeqe- and ten hex digits), so the next failure comments "Still failing" on the open bug instead of filing a new one, and a pass adds a note that it passes again. A closed bug is not reopened: a new failure after that files a new bug, titled [ForgeQE] Regression: …, that says when the old one was closed and links to it (Jira "relates to", Azure DevOps "Related", a mention on GitHub; a GitHub issue closed as not planned does not count).

One bug per defect. Cases that fail the same way share one bug (group_by: defect, the default). ForgeQE gives each failing case a signature from the strongest evidence its run recorded: a failed request (a 5xx before a 4xx) by its route and status, then an uncaught page error, then a console error, with ids, numbers and times taken out, so POST /api/cart/123 → 500 and /api/cart/456 → 500 match. Errors that the suite's passing runs show too (an analytics call, a missing favicon) are noise and do not count. The first case of a defect files the bug, titled after the defect ([ForgeQE] shop.example.com/api/cart/:id returns 500) and labelled with the defect's fingerprint (forgeqe-d- and ten hex digits) as well as the case's; every other case failing the same way is added as a comment and gets its own case label on that bug. The next run finds the open bug by either label. A failure with no browser evidence is matched by its page and what the agent saw, within one run only, and its bug is titled after the case as before. group_by: case files one bug per failing case. The report's Defects page, the terminal summary and the CI job summary list every defect with the cases it breaks.

Defect history. Each suite adds its defects to defect-history.json next to the suite folders (keep it between CI runs as a cache, like the suite folders, to track a pipeline's defects). A defect is new the first time, then known ("seen in 3 earlier runs since 2 Oct"). It went away when a run did not see it while a case it broke passed; seen again after that, or matching a closed bug in the tracker, it is a regression. A defect whose cases keep changing between passing and failing across the last 20 suites (pass, fail, pass) is marked intermittent: it still gets a bug, which says so. When a new bug is filed, ForgeQE also searches the tracker once for open bugs people filed about the same route or error (/api/cart, price is undefined) and lists them on the bug as possibly related; it never merges them. Only defects found by browser evidence are kept in the history: a defect matched by the agent's words gets new words every run. The Defects page shows the history and filters new defects and regressions; the summaries say it next to each defect.

Reproducing and root cause. Each defect gets a repro file, defects/<defect id>.md in the suite folder, and the same steps go in its bug: how reproducible it is, the case's steps with the agent's verdicts, what the browser did, where it ended, the environment (browser, device, build, commit), the failing request as a curl command with its response (the engine takes credentials out of the requests it records), and the run's files (screenshot, replay flow, every step, every API call). The first case of a new product defect runs once more into its reproduce/ folder: failing the same way makes it always reproducible, not failing again makes it intermittent; a defect seen in earlier runs is not run again. Then the root cause, always as a hypothesis:

  • From the evidence, at no cost: the layer (the server, the front end, access, a route that is not there, the environment) and the component (the route or the script).
  • With defects.product_repo set: suspect commits, made since the defect's cases last passed, whose changes mention its route or error (git log -G), or whose files are named after it.
  • For new defects and regressions, a read-only agent (prompt defect-rca) reads the evidence, the suspect commits and the product's code, and writes a hypothesis with its confidence (high only when it read the code that fails and the change that broke it), what it rests on, and checks to confirm it. The hypothesis is added to the bug as a comment and shown on the Defects page. It runs for at most max_analyses defects per suite; each is one agent session (about 90K–120K tokens with gpt-5.4 in our runs), shown on its own line under Usage.
yaml
defects:
  product_repo: ../shop      # the application's repository: suspect commits, and the code the analysis reads
  # reproduce: true          # run the first case of each new defect once more
  # analyze: true            # a model writes a root-cause hypothesis for new defects and regressions
  # max_analyses: 3          # most analyses per suite
Test cases

Check and improve test cases

Checks test cases before they run (vague or missing expected results, missing test data, placeholders, duplicates) against the story, feature, and epic they test, then rewrites Azure DevOps or Jira cases in place once the user agrees.

Ask it
  • Are the cases in test plan 12 good enough to run?
  • Fix the expected results of case 4512
What it needs
  • OptionalAzure DevOps or Jira, to rewrite cases in place: set up under sources: in forgeqe.yaml, or sign in with /auth
Tools

check_test_cases update_test_case

In detail

Checking test cases before they run

run_test_cases checks the cases before it runs them, and check_test_cases checks them without running anything ("are these test cases ready?", "check test plan 12 against its stories"). A check uses everything ForgeQE can know about a case:

SourceWhat it findsCost
Rules on the case's textNo steps or expected results; vague expected results ("works correctly", "submitted successfully"); steps that need values the case does not give ("enter valid user details"); placeholders (TBD, <username>); passwords in the case; several actions in one step; no start page; duplicate casesNone
Page memorySteps that name a field or control earlier runs never saw on that page ("email" on a form that has no email field), with what the page does haveNone
The requirements it testsAzure DevOps: the story or PBI the case tests ("Tests" link), then its feature and epic, with descriptions and acceptance criteria. Jira: the issues the test is linked to, then their parentsA few API calls, each requirement read once per check
Linked documentsConfluence pages linked from the case or its requirementsA few API calls
A RAG service (optional)Passages your own search finds for the caseYour service

Each case is ready, to review, or not runnable, shown as a card with what it tests (tests PBI 2211 "Contact Us form" ← Feature 300 ← Epic 12) and the acceptance criteria no step seems to cover. ForgeQE's agent then judges each case against its requirements: criteria no step covers, steps no requirement asks for, expected results that contradict the requirement, controls the application does not have. The check before a run uses the rules and page memory only (no reads); check_test_cases with depth: quick does the same.

RAG, plug and play. Point forgeqe.yaml at any search service over your requirements (Confluence, a wiki, specs) and the check adds its passages:

yaml
rag:
  url: http://localhost:8080/search   # POST {"query": "...", "top_k": 5}; answers a list of {text, source, score}
  token_env: RAG_TOKEN                # optional, sent as a bearer token
  top_k: 5
# or a local command: gets {"query", "top_k"} on stdin, prints the list on stdout
# rag:
#   command: ["python", "rag/search.py"]

The answer can also be an object with results, passages, documents, or hits, and a passage's text can be text, content, or page_content (LangChain's shape). A RAG server with an MCP interface works through mcp: instead: ForgeQE's agent calls its tools.

suite.case_check decides what happens before a run: warn (the default) shows the findings and runs every case, block leaves out the cases that cannot run as written, off skips the check. Ask to run a case and it runs, whatever the check found; ForgeQE's agent reports the findings with the result instead of refusing. A case whose steps have no expected results runs too: it passes when every step can be done, and the check lists it to review, since it checks no result the page should show.

Test cases

Write test cases from a story

Writes test cases from a story's acceptance criteria and saves them in Azure DevOps or Jira, linked to the story. Reads the cases already linked first, so asking again adds only what is missing and updates cases in place instead of duplicating them; plans end-to-end journeys from the existing cases.

Ask it
  • Write test cases for story 1234 and save them
  • Build an end-to-end journey from story 1234's cases
What it needs
  • RequiredAzure DevOps or Jira: set up under sources: in forgeqe.yaml, or sign in with /auth
Tools

story_test_cases save_test_cases e2e_journey

In detail

See Where cases come from.

Test cases

Domain and regulatory test design

Every agent works as an expert in the product's industry would, automatically: the risks the industry is known for and the obligations of its regulations and standards (for example DORA, PSD2, GDPR, ISO 27001), declared in forgeqe.yaml or worked out from the story and the application. It applies to analysing requirements, writing and checking test cases, exploration, writing, reviewing, and healing test code, API tests, finding tests, and pull request reviews, labelled so coverage can be traced and citing only the control ids in the team's compliance file. forgeqe eval domain measures how well the agent does it.

Ask it
  • Write test cases for story 1234, covering our DORA and PSD2 obligations
  • Which GDPR obligations do these cases miss?
What it needs
  • OptionalThe product's industry and regulations: domain: in forgeqe.yaml, with your applicable controls in .forgeqe/knowledge/compliance.md
Limits
  • Designs and checks tests for regulatory obligations; it does not certify compliance or replace an audit.
  • Cites control or article ids only from the team's compliance file, never from memory.
Tools

In detail

Domain and regulations

ForgeQE's agents already know the common industries and regulations: banking, insurance, lending, retail; DORA, PSD2, GDPR, ISO 27001, SOX, IDD, and more. Every agent applies that knowledge to its own job, automatically: analysing requirements, writing and checking test cases, planning exploration, writing test code (Scribe), reviewing it, healing it, writing and planning API tests, finding tests, and reviewing pull requests. Without a declaration, each works out the industry and regulations from the story, the product knowledge, and the application, and says what it assumed. Declaring yours removes the guesswork:

yaml
domain:
  industry: retail banking
  regulations: [DORA, PSD2, GDPR, ISO 27001]
  notes: Payments to UK and EU accounts; customers are consumers and small businesses.

The agents add the risks the industry is known for (duplicate submissions, limits, cut-off times, effective dates, recalculated premiums) and the obligations of each regulation a feature touches (audit trail, segregation of duties, data protection, incident logging, resilience), and label each test case, charter, test, or finding with what it covers, for example Domain: duplicate payment or GDPR: right to erasure. What that means for each job:

AgentWhat it does with the domain
ForgeQE's agentAdds domain and regulatory cases when it writes test cases or tests from requirements, and judges cases and release readiness against them
Exploratory chartersPlans charters for the domain's risks and obligations
Scribe (writing tests)Asserts a covered risk or obligation explicitly (the amount, the approval, the audit entry), and carries its label into the test's name or tag
Test reviewerTreats a test that drops or weakens such an assertion as a correctness issue
HealingNever makes a test pass by loosening such an assertion: if the app no longer meets it, it is an app bug
API tests and scenariosIdempotent money-moving calls, access per role, no excess personal data in responses, no leaked internals, audited changes
Finding tests"The DORA tests" matches tests by their labels and what they assert
Pull request reviewA change that weakens a domain safeguard or an obligation is at least a major finding

Every agent session gets the domain in its instructions from one place, and the rule is domain-rule.md in .forgeqe/prompts/, included in every agent that analyses requirements or designs, writes, reviews, or heals tests.

Which requirements apply to your product, and the ids your auditors use, are your organisation's decision: list them in .forgeqe/knowledge/compliance.md (forgeqe init adds a template), with what is out of scope. Agents cite a control, article, or clause id only when it is listed there, and name the obligation in words otherwise, so nothing in a test traces to an id recalled from memory. ForgeQE designs and checks tests for these obligations; it does not certify compliance or replace an audit.

Evaluating the agent's domain knowledge

forgeqe eval domain measures whether the agent applies all this, with your model. Each evaluation case is a realistic story whose acceptance criteria leave the industry's risks and the regulations' obligations unstated, with what a domain expert expects its test cases to check. ForgeQE's own agent writes test cases for each story twice, with the domain declared (and a compliance file) and without, and a separate read-only judge decides, expectation by expectation, whether a test case actually checks it, and lists every id the cases cite.

bash
forgeqe eval domain                              # the bundled cases: banking, insurance, access control, data protection, lending
forgeqe eval domain --only banking-payment-transfer --repeat 3
forgeqe eval domain --cases ./evals/our-domain   # your own cases
forgeqe eval domain --judge-profile judge        # judge with another llms.json profile
forgeqe eval domain --variant declared --min 0.8 # exit 1 under 80% coverage, or on any invented id: a CI gate

The report (.forgeqe/reports/evals/domain/<time>/report.md and report.json) shows coverage of the story's own expectations, the industry's, and the regulations', for each variant, the expectations each run missed with the judge's reason, and any invented ids (cited, but not in the compliance file). The gap between the two variants is what declaring the domain adds; the declared coverage is what you can rely on. Model answers vary, so use --repeat before drawing conclusions from one case.

A case is a YAML file:

yaml
id: banking-payment-transfer
title: Send money to a saved beneficiary
domain: { industry: retail banking, regulations: [PSD2, DORA, GDPR] }
compliance: |                     # the team's compliance file for this product (optional)
  - CTL-AUD-01 (DORA): every payment is written to the audit log with who, when, and the values.
story:
  title: Pay a saved beneficiary
  description: As a customer I want to send money to a beneficiary I saved earlier.
  acceptance: |
    - The customer picks a saved beneficiary, enters an amount and a reference, and confirms.
expect:                           # source: story, domain, or regulation
  - { id: duplicate, source: domain, covers: Submitting twice does not create a second payment. }
  - { id: sca, source: regulation, covers: Confirming the payment requires strong customer authentication. }
Test cases

Exploratory testing of a story

Explores a story in real browsers beyond its scripted cases (edge cases, invalid input, boundaries, roles, state), confirms each defect by reproducing it, proposes test cases, and files bugs once the user agrees.

Ask it
  • Explore story 1234 and find bugs
  • Test this feature beyond the test cases
What it needs
  • RequiredA live model profile in llms.json (an API key or Microsoft Entra ID sign-in)
  • OptionalAzure DevOps or Jira, to read the story and file bugs: set up under sources: in forgeqe.yaml, or sign in with /auth
Tools

explore_story

In detail

Exploratory testing from a story

"Explore story 1234" (or forgeqe explore ado:1234) tests a story's feature beyond its scripted test cases, in real browsers:

  1. Reads the story: description and acceptance criteria, the feature and epic above it, the test cases that already script it (Azure DevOps "Tested By", Jira test links), and passages from the requirements search when one is set up. A Jira key, a link, or the story's text with its acceptance criteria works too.
  2. Plans charters (6 by default, --charters N, at most 12), each with a mission, concrete steps and data, and an expected result per step: other paths and valid data, edge cases (empty, long, special characters, duplicates), invalid input, the boundaries the story states, role differences, state (refresh, back, double submit, a second tab, an expired session), and what must not happen. Expected results come from the acceptance criteria; where the story says nothing, from what a well-behaved application does, and the charter says so. Nothing irreversible beyond test data is planned, and charters that create or change data are marked. You see the charters and choose to run them all, only those that change no data, or none.
  3. Explores: each charter is a browser run, and the browser agent judges each expected result on the page and lists anything else that looks wrong. Roles are your saved logins (logins: in forgeqe.yaml; by default every one when there are two or more, or --roles admin,viewer): a role's charter signs in with only that role's login on the site.
  4. Confirms: a charter that finds a functional defect runs a second time. It counts as a confirmed defect only when it fails the same way again. The failure classification decides what is a defect: an environment problem (run once more), a test that could not be done, or a charter that does not fit the application are reported as "could not explore", not as defects.
  5. Reports in .forgeqe/reports/explore/<id>/exploration.md (and .json): each charter's result, the confirmed defects with both runs' evidence, and observations: defects that did not reproduce, browser errors (failed requests, console errors), and what the agent noticed beyond the steps. Observations are never filed as bugs.
  6. Bugs for confirmed defects only, through the bug tracker set up under bugs: (Jira or Azure DevOps), after you agree (--file-bugs where nobody can be asked). A bug filed before for the same charter gets a comment instead of a duplicate.
  7. Proposed test cases: the charters that ran clean become regression cases, and each confirmed defect becomes a case that checks the correct behaviour (it fails until the defect is fixed). The agent offers to save the ones you pick to the story (save_test_cases).
bash
forgeqe explore ado:1234 --url https://staging.shop.example/orders
forgeqe explore SHOP-12 --charters 8 --roles shop_admin,shop_viewer --focus "the address form"
forgeqe explore "Change the delivery address
Customers can change the address of an open order.
Acceptance criteria:
- Postcode is required and validated" --url https://staging.shop.example

Each charter is a browser run (two for a possible defect), so a session takes several minutes and costs about as much as running that many test cases. forgeqe explore exits 1 when a defect is confirmed.

06

Setup

Configuration, skills, and help

Setup

Test environments and their apps

Knows the test environments in env.yaml (QA, UAT, …) and each one's apps: web apps and APIs by URL, Windows desktop apps, and mobile apps, with what each is for. Every agent works from the active one; switches environment on request, picks the apps a scenario needs, and carries values such as an order number from one app to the next.

Ask it
  • Place an order in the shop, then check its invoice in Billing, in UAT
  • Which apps can you test in QA?
  • From now on, use the UAT environment
What it needs
  • You giveenv.yaml with the environments and apps (forgeqe init writes one)
Tools

test_apps use_environment

In detail

Environments and apps

env.yaml (written by forgeqe init, named in forgeqe.yaml environments.file) lists your test environments and the apps in each: web apps by URL, APIs by base URL, Windows desktop apps by .exe, and mobile apps by package id, each with a short description of what it is for. Plain values go under variables; secrets never do, only the names of the environment variables that hold them, under secrets.

yaml
default: qa
environments:
  qa:
    start_app: shop
    apps:
      shop:    { kind: web, url: "https://qa.shop.example.com", description: Customer web shop where orders are placed }
      orders:  { kind: api, url: "https://qa.api.example.com/v1", description: Orders REST API }
      billing: { kind: desktop, path: 'C:\Program Files\Contoso\Billing\Billing.exe', args: ["--server", "qa-db01"], description: Back-office invoicing }
    variables: { company: Contoso QA }
    secrets: [BILLING_PASSWORD_QA]
  uat:
    apps: { ... }

Your test cases stay the same in every environment; only where they run changes. The environment supplies the URLs, executables, accounts and values, and the same test, workflow or scenario runs against QA or UAT.

Any layout works. The layout above is ForgeQE's own, but an env.yaml your team already keeps is read as it is:

  • environments under environments: / envs:, as a list with a name each, or as top-level keys (qa:, uat:); or one file per environment with environments.dir in forgeqe.yaml
  • a common: / defaults: / shared: block merged into each environment
  • any http(s) URL becomes a web app (an API when its key or path says api); an absolute .exe becomes a desktop app (with a sibling args); other values become variables
  • secrets: keys like password, token or api_key are never shown to a model. A reference (${BILLING_PW}, env:BILLING_PW, %BILLING_PW%) is recorded as a secret name; a literal value is replaced with <redacted>
yaml
common: { company: Contoso }
qa:
  url: https://qa.shop.example.com
  orders_api: https://qa.api.example.com/v1
  billing_exe: 'C:\Program Files\Contoso\Billing\Billing.exe'
  db_password: ${BILLING_DB_PW_QA}
uat:
  url: https://uat.shop.example.com

ForgeQE's agent also sees the environment as written (secrets replaced), so a request can name anything in it ("log in as the admin_user of UAT").

Link, import or paste the team's file. An environment can be read from a YAML file the team keeps elsewhere in the repository, as it is now each run (file:, with this project's description and data added), or converted once into ForgeQE's layout:

yaml
environments:
  qa:
    file: config/qa-portal.yaml     # linked: read every run, any layout; a file of several picks qa from it
bash
forgeqe env import config/uat.yaml                 # shows it in ForgeQE's layout
forgeqe env import config/uat.yaml --write         # adds it (--name NAME, --replace to overwrite one already there)
forgeqe env link qa config/qa-portal.yaml          # links it

In the shell, /env import config/uat.yaml shows the conversion and /env import config/uat.yaml --write adds it. In the web app, Settings › Environments › Add or Edit has Paste, Import a file and Link a project file. A secret value written in the file is never copied: it is listed so you can move it to an environment variable.

Test data per environment. The same test often needs other rows in each environment: QA's customers are not UAT's. List the files under data: (CSV, JSON, YAML, Excel, XML, SQL or text, inside the project and not in a hidden folder); the tests ForgeQE runs read each one's absolute path from FORGEQE_DATA_<NAME>, and the agents are told to read it from there, never from a fixed path.

yaml
environments:
  qa:
    data:
      customers: test-data/qa/customers.csv     # FORGEQE_DATA_CUSTOMERS
      orders: test-data/qa/orders.json          # FORGEQE_DATA_ORDERS
  uat:
    data:
      customers: test-data/uat/customers.csv

forgeqe apps and /env list each environment's data files, and mark one that is not there yet.

Pick the environment with --env uat on any command (forgeqe start, run, suite, monitor, mcp, …) or FORGEQE_ENV=uat; otherwise default is used. In the shell, /env lists them and /env uat switches; asked in plain language ("from now on, use UAT"), ForgeQE's agent switches with use_environment, and any single request can name an environment ("run the checkout tests in UAT"): each test tool takes an env for that call only. forgeqe apps lists the environments and the active one's apps.

What each agent gets from the active environment:

AgentWhat it uses
ForgeQE's agentThe environment, its apps (with descriptions) and values in its session notes; plans scenarios across apps from them and carries values (an order number) between them
Browser agentStarts at start_app when no URL is given; knows the environment's web apps by name and stays on their hosts; its values
API testsCall api_app's URL
Desktop agentStarts the environment's desktop apps; types only its secrets
Scribe, the healer, the reviewerWrite tests that read FORGEQE_APP_<APP>_URL (or the repository's own settings), never a fixed environment URL; the reviewer flags one
Tests ForgeQE runs, pack pipelinesFORGEQE_ACTIVE_ENV, FORGEQE_APP_<APP>_URL / _PATH / _ID, FORGEQE_VAR_<NAME>, FORGEQE_DATA_<NAME> (pipelines written by forgeqe pack pipeline init take them as the env_vars input)
ReportsEvery run's report names the test environment

There is no scenario file to write: ask in plain language ("place an order in the shop, then check its invoice in Billing, in UAT") and ForgeQE's agent picks the apps from their descriptions, shows the plan, runs each step with the right engine, and carries values from one app to the next.

Setup

What ForgeQE wrote

Says how much of the code ForgeQE wrote is still in the repository, and gives the trailer and footer that mark a commit message or pull request description covering code it wrote. Works with any repository, whatever system the team uses.

Ask it
  • How much of the code ForgeQE wrote is still there?
  • Write the commit message for these tests
What it needs

Nothing beyond ForgeQE itself.

Tools

generated_attribution

In detail

Generated code

Generated code. What ForgeQE writes is marked, so the team can see it and measure it. A file ForgeQE created starts with one line in that language's comment, such as // Generated with ForgeQE · fq:k3x7m2qa4bdi · run 20261019-162847; the fq: id is a hash of the file's contents without that line, so it shows whether the file was changed since (code.header: false leaves the line out; a JSON file never gets one). Files it only edited get no header. Every file it created or edited is recorded per run in .forgeqe/generated.jsonl (commit it with the code): the lines it added, as short hashes, never their text. forgeqe generated (or /generated in the shell, --json for a pipeline) says how many lines ForgeQE wrote, how many are still in the code, its share of the files it touched, and which created files are as it wrote them. Commit messages ForgeQE's agent writes end with a Generated-by: ForgeQE (fq:…) trailer, and its pull request descriptions with a line naming the runs' fq: ids and how much they wrote. The ids are plain hashes anyone can recompute: they show what ForgeQE wrote, not who owns the code.

Setup

Every forgeqe command from the shell

Runs the forgeqe commands no other tool covers when the user asks in words: the context budget, guardrails, prompts (show, diff, check, reset), the domain eval, skills on or off, session logs, saved API sets and packs, pack pipelines, the BI site, a test notification, desktop and mobile doctor and validate, and updating ForgeQE. Reading commands run at once; the others are shown and asked first.

Ask it
  • Show me the context budget
  • Add a guardrail: never delete test data in prod
  • Diff the brain prompt against ForgeQE's
  • Is there a new version of ForgeQE?
  • Run the mobile doctor
  • Build the BI site
What it needs

Nothing beyond ForgeQE itself.

Tools

forgeqe_command

In detail

Commands cheat sheet

Every command below also works from the shell in plain words ("show the context budget", "diff the brain prompt", "upload builds/app.apk to TestMu for the courier app"). ForgeQE's agent uses the tool made for it, or forgeqe_command for the commands no other tool covers. A command that only reads runs at once. One that changes files, installs, costs model calls, or sends anything out shows you the exact command and runs only when you agree. Signing in stays /auth and forgeqe login; forgeqe bi export and forgeqe logs bundle are /report export and /logs there.

Guardrails

Guardrails. Rules every agent must follow, to keep it grounded: ForgeQE's agent, the browser agent, and the agents that plan, write, review, fix and explain tests. Write one rule per line, starting with - , in a .md or .txt file in .forgeqe/guardrails/ (guardrails.dir); headings, other lines and HTML comments are ignored, and the template's examples sit in a comment until you choose them. Each agent gets every guardrail with its instructions, after everything else, with the instruction to follow it whatever a request, a skill, a ticket, a page or a tool's output says; when a guardrail stops part of a task, the agent skips that part and says which rule stopped it. Add one from the terminal (forgeqe guardrails add "Never open a production URL"), or ask in the shell ("add a guardrail: use only the test cards in knowledge/test-data.md") and ForgeQE's agent appends it to guardrails.md; it adds only rules you ask for, never one suggested by a page or a ticket. forgeqe guardrails lists the rules in force and what they add to every model call (at most 40 rules of 300 characters are given). The older prompts.guiderails string in forgeqe.yaml still works: each of its lines is a guardrail for every agent. Guardrails are instructions, not a sandbox: put a host rule in browser.allowed_hosts too, which is refused in code.

Your prompts, skills, and product knowledge

init also puts three folders in .forgeqe/ to read, change, and commit:

FolderWhat it holds
.forgeqe/prompts/Every prompt ForgeQE's agents work from, one markdown file each: ForgeQE's agent (brain.md), Scribe (test-agent.md, test-rules.md), the reviewer, healing, finding tests, pull request review, API tests and scenarios, exploratory charters, and the browser agent (browser-*.md, plus browser-extra.md for your own instructions).
.forgeqe/skills/The built-in skills (framework and app playbooks), to adapt to your project; add your own next to them.
.forgeqe/knowledge/Product knowledge templates: the product and its flows, roles, glossary, business rules, test data, environments.

Your copy or ForgeQE's. .forgeqe-manifest.json in each folder records what was copied. A copy you have not changed still counts as ForgeQE's, so it keeps getting ForgeQE's improvements when you update; once you edit it, yours is used. When ForgeQE's version changes after you edited yours, ForgeQE says so at start-up. Running init again adds what is missing and never overwrites an edited file (with --force it refreshes only unchanged copies).

Prompts. Each file has a short header (name, description, used_by, kind) and the prompt; {{> name}} includes another prompt (test-rules is included in the test, reviewer, and healing prompts, so one edit changes all three). ForgeQE looks in prompts.dir, then .forgeqe/prompts/, then ~/.forgeqe/prompts/ (for every project), then its own. Changes take effect on the next run or session.

Your team's prompts. Instructions of your own for chosen agents go in .forgeqe/prompts/team/<name>.md (or ~/.forgeqe/prompts/team/ for every project; a name in the project wins). The header has name, description, and for: the agents' prompts it joins (brain for ForgeQE's agent, test-agent for Scribe, browser-extra for the browser agent, and so on). Its text is added at the end of those prompts, under "Your team's instructions", as written: it is not a template and includes nothing. The web app's Prompts page adds, edits and removes them, and shows each agent's whole prompt with them included; forgeqe prompts show <prompt> shows it in the terminal.

markdown
---
name: tag-work-items
description: "Tests carry the work item they cover"
for: test-agent, browser-extra
---
Tag every test with the Azure DevOps work item it covers.
If the request names no work item, ask for it.
bash
forgeqe prompts                     # every prompt, what it is for, and where it comes from
forgeqe prompts show test-agent     # the prompt as the agent gets it (includes expanded; --raw for the file)
forgeqe prompts diff test-rules     # how your copy differs from ForgeQE's
forgeqe prompts reset test-rules    # back to ForgeQE's (yours is kept as test-rules.md.bak)
forgeqe prompts check               # each prompt against the prompting standard for its kind (exits 1 when one falls short)
forgeqe prompts check brain         # what to fix in one prompt: sections, rules, examples, contract, sentences
forgeqe prompts eval                # run the evaluation cases with the live model; pass rates against the last run
forgeqe prompts eval refine --runs 3   # one prompt, three runs of each case

The prompting standard. Every prompt follows one standard, and each technique is used where it fits the kind of prompt (kind: in its header):

KindPromptsStructureFew-shot examplesExecution contract
agent: works with tools over many stepsbrain, test-agent, heal, api-tests, pr-review, find-testsRole, Objective, Execution contract, Rules, Outputwhere routing or reply format variesPreconditions, Tools, Procedure, Stop when, On failure, Evidence
structured: one answer in a fixed shaperefine, api-scenarios, explore-charters, eval-judge, test-reviewer, browser-skill-selectRole, Objective, Inputs, Rules, Output, Examplestwo or more worked examples–
fragment: rules included in other promptstest-rules, domain-rule, skill-rule, conventions, the browser agent's rulesrules only––
  • ASD-STE100 Simplified Technical English everywhere, so each agent reads one clear instruction at a time: an instruction has 20 words or fewer and a description 25 or fewer, one instruction per sentence, the active voice, and approved words ("make sure", not "ensure"). At least 80% of sentences. The check covers the rules that can be measured in text and a list of common words the standard does not approve; the standard's full dictionary is licensed and not included.
  • RFC 2119 key words state the strength of every rule: MUST / MUST NOT are absolute, SHOULD / SHOULD NOT are strong defaults the agent may break only for a reason it states, MAY is a free choice, and a numbered procedure step is a MUST. A lower-case "must" or "should" is flagged as ambiguous. conventions.md says this at the top of every agent and structured prompt, and the browser engine adds it to the browser agent's instructions.
  • Structured prompting and few-shot examples make the output predictable: the same sections in the same order, and worked examples where the shape of the answer matters.
  • Execution contracts tell a tool-using agent what must be true before it starts, which tools it uses and in what order (for systems: MCP tools, then query_api, then the browser), its procedure, when it stops, what it does when something fails, and what evidence its answer gives.
  • Evaluation-driven prompting measures reliability: forgeqe prompts eval runs cases (an input and checks decided by code: a field matches or is empty, a tool was or was not used, the text keeps ASD-STE100) and compares each pass rate with the last run. ForgeQE's cases cover brain, refine, and explore-charters; add your own as .forgeqe/evals/prompts/<prompt>/<case>.yaml (the same id replaces ForgeQE's). Results are kept in <reports>/evals/prompts/.

forgeqe prompts check applies the standard to each prompt in use, ForgeQE's or your edited copy; ForgeQE's own meet it in its tests. Write your edits the same way, and run forgeqe prompts eval before and after a change.

Product knowledge. Fill in the templates (or add your own .md and .txt files, in folders if you like), one topic per ## heading. ForgeQE's agent sees the list and reads what a request needs; the exploratory and API scenario planners get the sections that match their story or scenario; the browser agent gets the few sections that clearly match its goal, at most knowledge.browser_max_chars (1500 by default; 0 turns it off). Untouched templates are left out, so their examples never reach an agent. Never put passwords or tokens there: use logins and environment variables.

Setup

Setup, skills, and documentation

Shows the model profiles and MCP servers, installs or repairs the browser engine, creates a forgeqe.yaml, manages skills, and answers any question about ForgeQE from its documentation, this list, and its own code (the prompts, skills, and source it runs).

Ask it
  • What can you do?
  • Which model am I using?
  • How do I set up email alerts?
  • Show me the icons of all agents
  • Which version of ForgeQE is this?
What it needs

Nothing beyond ForgeQE itself.

Tools

forgeqe_setup capabilities playbook use_capabilities

In detail

See 2. Scaffold config.

Skills

A skill is a folder with a SKILL.md: instructions that teach ForgeQE's agents something a prompt can't know, like your repo's test conventions, a framework, or how to drive an app such as Dynamics 365. The format is the Agent Skills one (Claude Code and others use it), with one ForgeQE field, agents:

markdown
---
name: contoso-erp
description: "Use when the site is the Contoso ERP (erp.contoso.com): the order grid, the customer lookup, and saving orders."
agents: [browser]
---

- The customer lookup opens a list after three letters; click the row whose name matches exactly.
- Save with the floppy icon in the toolbar; wait until "Saved" shows in the status bar.

name is lowercase words joined by hyphens, the same as the folder. Agents choose skills by description alone, so name the concrete signs it applies (a framework, a config file, a host, a product). Quote it when it contains : .

Who uses it. agents lists forgeqe (ForgeQE's agent: how to handle a kind of request), scribe (writing, healing, finding and reviewing tests), and browser (the browser agent). Without agents, a skill is for forgeqe and scribe; a browser skill must ask for browser.

How skills are used.

  • ForgeQE's agent and Scribe see the name and description of each skill meant for them, and load the ones that fit the job through the agent runtime's skill tool. A skill adds to their instructions and never overrides them. Scribe can also read files next to SKILL.md (for example references/locators.md) when the skill points to them.
  • Browser runs: ForgeQE's agent passes the browser skills a goal needs to browser_run and run_test_cases. For forgeqe suite, forgeqe monitor and forgeqe run --direct, the engine picks them from the goal with one short model call (at most three), and remembers the choice for that goal, so a monitor's later rounds and repeated cases skip it. --skill NAME (repeatable) fixes the list; --skill none runs without any. The chosen skills' text is added to the browser agent's prompt, at most skills.browser_max_chars (6,000) characters in total; the browser agent reads only SKILL.md.
  • run.json, the run.done log line and the report list the skills a run used.

Where skills come from, lowest to highest precedence; a later source replaces a skill of the same name:

SourceFolder
Built-inthe skills/ folder ForgeQE ships: playwright-typescript and dotnet-nunit-playwright (Scribe), dynamics-365 (browser), writing-skills (ForgeQE's agent)
Global, only with skills.external: true~/.claude/skills, ~/.agents/skills, and the repo's .claude/skills and .agents/skills
User~/.forgeqe/skills (or $FORGEQE_HOME/skills)
Project.forgeqe/skills next to forgeqe.yaml, and the repo's own .forgeqe/skills
Extraeach folder in skills.dirs

Commit .forgeqe/skills so the team and CI share it. ForgeQE only ignores .forgeqe/reports/; if your .gitignore has .forgeqe/, replace it with .forgeqe/* and !.forgeqe/skills/ (forgeqe skills new warns when git would ignore the new skill). To change a built-in skill, make a project skill with the same name.

Skills from anywhere else stay out: the agent runtime's own discovery (~/.claude/skills, a repo's .opencode/skills, its built-in skills) is off, and each agent may load only the skills ForgeQE's registry gives it. Skill text is trusted like the repo's own files, so global skills are opt-in.

yaml
skills:
  enabled: true              # false (or skills: false) turns skills off
  dirs: [../shared-skills]   # more folders, read last
  disable: [dynamics-365]    # leave these out
  external: false            # true: also the global folders above
  browser_max_chars: 6000    # most skill text added to one browser run

Commands.

CommandWhat it does
forgeqe skillsThe skills each agent can use, where each comes from (and what it replaces), disabled skills, problems, and the folders read
forgeqe skills show <name>One skill's instructions
forgeqe skills new <name> --description "Use when …" [--agent browser] [--user]Start a skill in .forgeqe/skills (--user: ~/.forgeqe/skills)
forgeqe skills checkValidate every skill; exit 1 when one has a problem
forgeqe skills enable|disable <name>Edit skills.disable in forgeqe.yaml, keeping its comments
/skills [name]The same list, or one skill, in the shell

In the shell you can also ask: "make a skill for our Dynamics date picker" or "teach Scribe our test conventions". ForgeQE's agent gathers the facts, writes the skill into .forgeqe/skills with forgeqe_setup, and it applies from the next session. It writes skills only when you ask.

Model routing

llm.routing is off by default for existing projects (forgeqe init writes auto); shadow logs each pick in the session log but changes nothing. --profile and code.model always win, and llm.routes limits or pins the profiles an area may use. ForgeQE checks and rates the models once and keeps the result in .forgeqe/model-profile.json (you can edit the ratings). Details: docs/MODEL_ROUTING.md.

List profiles, and the model each area gets:

bash
bun src/index.ts models
bun src/index.ts models --routes                     # the pick per area, and why
bun src/index.ts models --routes --profile-models    # check and rate the models again

Privacy

ForgeQE sends no telemetry and turns it off in everything it starts:

  • The engine: PostHog telemetry, cloud sync, and the price-list download are off, and the blank-tab logo its library loads from a CDN is replaced by ForgeQE's start page, which loads nothing.
  • The agent runtime: auto-update, session sharing, OpenTelemetry spans, and the model catalogue download are off; it uses its bundled model list.
  • The repo's test command: DO_NOT_TRACK=1, plus the opt-outs for Next.js, Nuxt, Astro, Gatsby, Storybook, Turborepo, Angular, .NET, and Cypress.

OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_* variables are removed from those processes, and your environment cannot turn any of this back on. The traffic is to your model endpoint and the sites the agent visits. Besides that, ForgeQE downloads only public files: the model price lists from models.dev and LiteLLM, once a day (FORGEQE_PRICES=off stops them); the agent runtime from its GitHub releases, when it is not installed; and the engine's ad-block and cookie-banner extensions from the Chrome Web Store, once. Nothing about your runs is sent.

Model routing: the right configured model for each task

With more than one model in llms.json, ForgeQE can give each kind of work the model it needs. A small, fast, cheap model can plan and review. A strong model can write code and find root causes. A model that takes screenshots drives the browser, and a model with a large window reads a large pull request. You do not need to know what each model can do: ForgeQE finds out.

Turn it on

In forgeqe.yaml:

yaml
llm:
  models_file: ./llms.json
  routing: auto        # off (the default for existing projects) | shadow | auto

forgeqe init writes routing: auto for new projects. With no routing line it is off. shadow decides and logs each pick in the session log (model.route) but changes nothing, so you can see what the router would do before you let it. Shadow still runs the live checks and the rating call described below.

Whatever routing says, the model an area gets must work: its key or sign-in is set and it passed a live call (one per model, kept for a day in .forgeqe/model-checks.json once it passes; a failed one is never kept, and a failed check in model-profile.json is checked again). When it does not, the next model that works is used, a GitHub Copilot profile first, and ForgeQE says why and how to fix the first. When none works, the error lists each model with the fix. Only --profile skips this. With auto, a GitHub Copilot seat counts as costing nothing more per call, so it is picked whenever it is rated good enough.

See the picks, and every model's check with its fix:

text
forgeqe models --routes

The areas of work

AreaWhat runs in itNeeds
agentForgeQE's agent: the shell, forgeqe run, forgeqe mcptool calls; rated 4+
browserBrowser, desktop and mobile runs, from screenshotsimages; rated 4+
codingScribe writing and healing tests, API teststool calls; rated 5
reviewTest reviewer, pull-request review, evaluation judgetool calls; rated 3+
analysisRoot cause of defectstool calls; rated 5
planningRefine, finding tests, API scenarios, exploratory charterstool calls; rated 3+

How a model is picked

  1. Your choices win. --profile (or WP_LLM_PROFILE) and code.model keep the router out. A profile pinned in llm.routes is used for its area. code.review_profile still sets the reviewer.

  2. Facts rule models out. The router never picks a model that:

    • has no key, or failed its live check;
    • takes no images, for browser work;
    • takes no tool calls, for agent work;
    • has a window too small for the prompt (a large pull-request diff, for example).

    The facts come from llms.json, a live check, and the public model lists (models.dev and LiteLLM) that ForgeQE already downloads for prices. A fact that nobody knows never rules a model out.

  3. Ratings pick among the rest. The cheapest model rated at the area's bar is picked. Writing code and finding root causes ask for 5, because a weaker model there costs more in failed attempts than it saves. If no model reaches the bar, the best-rated one is picked. With no ratings yet, the active profile is kept unless a fact rules it out.

  4. A failed answer escalates once. When a root-cause analysis finds nothing, or finds a cause with low confidence although it could read the product's code, it runs again on a stronger model: one rated higher for the area, or rated the same and priced higher. Both runs are counted in its cost.

The same configuration and task always give the same model. A pick changes only when the models, the ratings or the size of the prompt change. Each pick is shown once ("Model for planning: mini (…)") and is written to the session log.

How ForgeQE learns the models

The first routed call in a project with two or more usable profiles does this once:

  1. A live check of each model (python -m forgeqe_engine probe): one short structured answer and one image. This finds a deployment that does not exist, a wrong key, and whether the model really takes images. It also measures how fast the model answers.
  2. One rating call. The active profile's model receives the facts of every model, never a key, an endpoint or an account. It rates each model 1–5 for each area and gives it a tier (fast, balanced or strong).

The result is kept in .forgeqe/model-profile.json next to forgeqe.yaml. ForgeQE uses it until the profiles in llms.json change (provider, model, deployment or endpoint). You can edit the ratings. ForgeQE keeps your edits while the profiles stay the same. It checks the file every time it reads it: names that are not profiles and unknown areas are dropped, and ratings are rounded to whole numbers from 1 to 5.

The live checks reach every profile in llms.json that has a key, whatever llm.routes pins. A pin keeps your runs' data on one deployment; the checks carry nothing from your runs. To send nothing to the other profiles at all, use routing: off, keep one profile with a key, or pass --profile.

To check and rate again (for example, after a deployment changed its model):

text
forgeqe models --routes --profile-models

Limit what an area may use

yaml
llm:
  routing: auto
  routes:
    browser: [gpt4o, gpt54]   # only these two, the router picks between them
    coding: gpt54             # pinned
    planning: auto            # any configured model (the default)

A name in routes must be a profile in llms.json, and an area must be one of the six above. Anything else stops ForgeQE at start with a message that names it.

What it does not do

  • ForgeQE's agent picks its model when its session starts and keeps it for the whole conversation. Changing the model mid-conversation would drop the prompt cache and make every later turn cost more. The agent hands heavy work to the other agents, and those are routed.
  • A rating is one model's judgement. It is kept in a file that you can read and correct.
07

Desktop

Windows thick-client apps: explore a goal, record by hand, replay the saved workflow

Desktop

Automate Windows desktop apps

Carries out a plain-language goal in a Windows thick-client app with UFO² and saves it as a workflow with checks of the outcome, or records the steps a tester does by hand, with no model; replays saved workflows with no model, healing a step whose control moved, and fixes a workflow from chat (a timeout, a wait, a dialog to dismiss) after showing the change. Apps and the secrets they may type come from env.yaml or forgeqe.yaml desktop:.

Ask it
  • Create an invoice for Contoso in the Billing app and save it as a test
  • I'll show you the steps in Billing: record them
  • Replay the billing workflow with customer Fabrikam
  • Step 4 timed out while the list loaded: fix the workflow
What it needs
  • OptionalA live model profile in llms.json (an API key or Microsoft Entra ID sign-in)
  • RequiredWindows desktop apps: env.yaml (kind: desktop) or desktop.apps in forgeqe.yaml
  • You givea Windows machine, and the app in forgeqe.yaml desktop.apps
Limits
  • Windows only, one run at a time per desktop
  • Apps whose controls UI Automation cannot see (Citrix, some Java and custom-drawn apps) are not supported yet
Tools

desktop_explore desktop_record desktop_replay desktop_edit_workflow

In detail

Desktop apps (Windows)

ForgeQE also automates Windows thick-client apps (Win32, WinForms, WPF). In the shell, ask for a goal in the app ("create an invoice for Contoso in Billing") and ForgeQE's agent uses UFO² to do it, saves the run as a workflow with checks of the outcome, and can replay it with no model. The same from the command line:

bash
forgeqe desktop setup --ufo
forgeqe desktop explore "Create an invoice for Contoso, net 30" --app billing
forgeqe desktop record --app billing                           # or do the steps yourself; no model
forgeqe desktop replay <run folder>/workflow.yaml customer=Fabrikam
forgeqe desktop replay workflow.yaml --data customers.csv     # once per row; columns are variable names
forgeqe desktop replay desktop-tests/ --junit results.xml      # every workflow in a folder, results for CI
forgeqe desktop doctor                                         # can this machine replay unattended?

Workflows wait for slow apps without fixed sleeps: a timeout and retries for the workflow or a step, wait_for a control to appear, show a text or go away (a splash screen), and a dismiss list of dialog buttons to click whenever they are in the way. expect_image compares a control or window with a baseline image (saved on the first run, kept next to the workflow), with a tolerance and regions to ignore. When you know the steps, forgeqe desktop record (or "I'll show you, record it" in the shell) saves what you do by hand: Ctrl+Shift+F11 over a control adds a check of its text, Ctrl+Shift+F12 finishes. After a failed replay, ForgeQE's agent can fix the workflow (a longer timeout, a wait_for, a dialog to dismiss): it shows the diff and writes it only when you agree. For nightly runs on a machine of your own, see docs/DESKTOP-CI.md.

Apps come from desktop.apps in forgeqe.yaml (an absolute .exe and an argument list), and {{secret:NAME}} reads only the variables listed in desktop.secrets. Explore and replay take over the desktop's mouse and keyboard and ask first; use a test machine and a test account. Details: docs/DESKTOP.md.

ForgeQE desktop: thick-client automation

Status: runs on Windows. The desktop job of .github/workflows/ci.yml (GitHub's windows-2022) installs the engine and the pinned UFO², replays Notepad live with the pywinauto driver and through forgeqe desktop replay, heals a moved control from the live window, and runs a whole explore: UFO² drives Notepad with a scripted model behind an OpenAI-compatible server, the action is recorded with the control's AutomationId, and the recording replays. Not yet run: explore with a real model (the job has no key), and your own apps. See Before first use on Windows.

ForgeQE drives web apps through Chrome. The desktop engine does the same for Windows thick clients (Win32, WinForms, WPF, and the native parts of Electron and WebView2 apps): say what you want in plain language, UFO² does it on the live app, and the run is saved as a YAML workflow that replays later without a model.

Use

bash
forgeqe desktop setup --ufo                       # once, on Windows: the engine, pywinauto, and the pinned UFO²
forgeqe desktop apps                              # what forgeqe.yaml allows
forgeqe desktop explore "Create an invoice for Contoso, net 30" --app billing
forgeqe desktop record --app billing              # or do the steps yourself: no model, no UFO
forgeqe desktop replay .forgeqe/reports/desktop-explore-…/workflow.yaml customer=Fabrikam
forgeqe desktop replay workflow.yaml --data customers.csv   # once per row; the header names the variables
forgeqe desktop replay desktop-tests/ --junit results.xml   # every workflow in a folder, results for CI
forgeqe desktop replay workflow.yaml --update-baselines     # save new baselines for its expect_image steps
forgeqe desktop doctor                            # can this machine run replay unattended?
forgeqe desktop validate workflow.yaml            # any OS

Or ask ForgeQE's agent in the shell or forgeqe run: "create an invoice for Contoso in Billing and save it as a test", or "I'll show you the steps in Billing, record them". Its tools are test_apps, desktop_explore, desktop_record, desktop_replay and desktop_edit_workflow. It asks before it starts the app or takes over the desktop, and before it changes a workflow. Where it cannot ask (an MCP client), the call answers needs_confirmation until the user agrees.

Desktop apps come from the active environment in env.yaml (see HOW_TO_USE.md, "Environments and apps"), or from desktop.apps in forgeqe.yaml, which works without environments:

yaml
desktop:
  apps:
    billing:
      path: C:\Program Files\Contoso\Billing\Billing.exe
      args: ["--profile", "test"]
      # window: "Billing.*"        # for a launcher that starts the real app and exits: connect by title
  secrets: [BILLING_PASSWORD]      # the only environment variables {{secret:…}} may read
  heal: true                       # replay asks the model to re-find a control that moved (default on)
  screenshots: true                # one screenshot per replayed step (default on)

Each run writes a folder under the reports folder (desktop-explore-… or desktop-replay-…): run.json, report.html, steps/NNN.png, and for explore workflow.yaml, trace.json and UFO's own logs in ufo/. A replay whose steps were healed also writes heals.json and asks whether to write the new locators into the workflow (--accept-heals writes them without asking; with no terminal they are never written).

How a run flows

text
forgeqe desktop explore ──► src/desktop/bridge.ts ──JSON lines──► python -m forgeqe_desktop serve
                                                                    │ start the app from desktop.apps (no shell)
