                                                                    │ UFO² in-process: model = llms.json profile,
                                                                    │ UI tools only, every action traced with its
                                                                    ▼ control's UIA properties
                                                       record.py ──► workflow.yaml
forgeqe desktop replay ───► bridge ──► serve (replay.py on pywinauto, no model)
                                          │ a control is not found
                                          ▼
                                       heal.py: the model picks one of the window's real controls, by number
                                          ▼
                                       proposed locator → heals.json, written into the workflow only when accepted

One model config: llms.json → UFO

UFO picks its model client by API_TYPE through ufo.llm.base.BaseService.get_service, which every model call goes through (ufo/llm/llm_call.py). The adapter adds the type forgeqe:

  • write_ufo_config writes UFO's config/ufo/agents.yaml from its template, with API_TYPE: forgeqe, the model's name, and no endpoint and no key, for HOST_AGENT, APP_AGENT, BACKUP_AGENT and EVALUATION_AGENT.
  • ufo_bridge.install makes get_service("forgeqe", …) return ForgeQEService, which calls the profile through the openai SDK (models.py): azure (key or auth: entra), openai, openai_compatible (key or Entra), and ollama through its OpenAI-compatible /v1. Keys, Azure endpoints and Entra use the browser engine's own helpers (forgeqe_engine.llm), so a profile means the same thing in both. Keys stay in environment variables.
  • Each call's time and tokens go to ForgeQE's usage ledger as the "desktop agent" (counts only, never the prompt).

Explore sends screenshots, so its profile must take images: "vision": true or false on the profile says so; without it, ollama profiles are refused and others accepted. Healing sends a screenshot only to a profile that takes images.

The request parameters suit the model by themselves: a reasoning model (o1, o3, o4, gpt-5, by the profile's model or deployment name, or "reasoning": true on the profile) gets max_completion_tokens and no temperature from the first call, and when the API refuses a parameter (max_tokens, temperature, frequency_penalty, response_format, …) the call is sent again without it, and later calls to that model leave it out (py/forgeqe_engine/model_params.py). The browser agent does the same.

Workflow YAML (forgeqe-desktop-v1)

yaml
format: forgeqe-desktop-v1
name: Create an invoice
app: billing                 # a name from forgeqe.yaml desktop.apps, never a path or a command
variables:
  customer: Contoso
steps:
  - op: launch
  - op: click
    target: {automation_id: btnNewInvoice, control_type: Button, name: New invoice}
  - op: type
    target: {automation_id: txtCustomer, control_type: Edit}
    text: "{{customer}}"
  - op: type
    target: {automation_id: txtPassword, control_type: Edit}
    text: "{{secret:BILLING_PASSWORD}}"
  - op: select
    target: {automation_id: cmbTerms, control_type: ComboBox}
    value: Net 30
  - op: keys
    keys: ctrl+s
  - op: expect
    target: {automation_id: lblStatus}
    text: Saved
  - op: close
  • Ops: launch, click, double_click, type, select, keys, expect, expect_image, wait (at most 60 s), wait_for, close.
  • Locators use UIA properties, never screen coordinates: automation_id, name, control_type, class_name, index (when several match), and path (the ancestors' control_type and name). A target needs an automation_id or a name. Replay looks in the launched app's own windows only.
  • Variables: {{name}} from the workflow's variables or name=value on the command line; {{secret:NAME}} from the environment, only for names in desktop.secrets. A secret ForgeQE itself uses (a model key, a tracker token) is refused there. Values are substituted once and never expanded again. Targets take {{name}} too (a CSV row's {{customer}} as the control to click), never a secret.
  • Keys: ctrl, shift and alt with one named key or character (ctrl+s, alt+f4, enter, f5). The Windows and context-menu keys are refused: Win+R followed by typing runs any command.
  • Typing is literal: pywinauto's key syntax (^, %, +, ~, {ENTER}) in the text is escaped.

Recording by hand

When you know the steps, do them yourself instead of describing a goal: no model and no UFO are involved, so it costs nothing and does exactly what you did.

bash
forgeqe desktop record --app billing --name "Create an invoice"

ForgeQE starts the app and watches the mouse and keyboard (low-level Windows hooks), asking UI Automation which control each click and key went to. Do the steps, then:

  • Ctrl+Shift+F11 with the mouse over a control adds a check: an expect step with the text it shows now (a status message, a saved total). Add one at the end, so the replay tests the outcome, not only the steps.
  • Ctrl+Shift+F12, or closing the app, finishes. The console shows each step as it is recorded.

What becomes a step:

You doStep
Click a control (two quick clicks: double_click)click with its AutomationId, name and control type
Pick an item in a drop-downselect on the drop-down, with the item's name
Type into a field (corrections and pastes included)one type with the field's text when you move on, read from the field
A key chord outside a text field (ctrl+s, enter, f5)keys
Ctrl+Shift+F11 over a controlexpect with its text
  • Passwords: a password field is never read; its step types {{secret:NAME}} (the one name in desktop.secrets, or <APP>_PASSWORD, which the result tells you to set). Text equal to a desktop.secrets value is written as its placeholder. The typed characters themselves are never kept, only the field's text when you move on.
  • Only the app: clicks and keys outside the app's own process are not recorded; nor are the Windows and menu keys, or controls with neither an AutomationId nor a name. The result lists what was left out.
  • The run folder has workflow.yaml, run.json and report.html, like explore. Replay it with forgeqe desktop replay.

Fixing a workflow from chat

When a replayed step fails because the app was slow or a dialog was in the way, ForgeQE's agent fixes the workflow with desktop_edit_workflow: it proposes the change (a longer timeout or retries on the step, a wait_for before it, a dialog button in dismiss, a healed locator, a corrected text), shows you the diff, and writes the file only when you agree. For example, after "step 4 timed out while the invoice list loaded":

text
Change invoice.yaml?
• new wait_for step before step 4
• step 4: retries 2
    - op: click
  +   target: {automation_id: btnPost}
  +   retries: 2
  • Each change has a fixed shape (set, unset, set_step, add_dismiss, insert_step, remove_step, set_variable); step numbers are the replay report's. Anything else is refused.
  • The changed workflow is checked by the engine's own validator before it is shown; one that would not run is not offered. Comments and layout are kept.
  • The old text is kept in the reports folder (desktop-edits/), and a file that changed since the edit was proposed is not overwritten.
  • The workflow must be a .yaml inside the working folder or the reports folder.

Waits, retries and dialogs

Real apps are slow in places: a splash screen, a form that loads its data, a "Tip of the day" or "Save changes?" dialog. A workflow says how to wait for them, without fixed wait steps:

yaml
timeout: 20              # seconds a step waits for its control (default 10, at most 300)
retries: 1               # tries a failed action again, after a short pause (default 0, at most 5)
dismiss:                 # clicked whenever one of them is in the way
  - {name: OK, control_type: Button, automation_id: btnTipClose}
  - {name: "Don't Save", control_type: Button}
steps:
  - op: launch
    timeout: 90          # a slow start: how long to wait for the app's window
  - op: wait_for         # the splash screen goes away
    target: {automation_id: splashForm}
    gone: true
    timeout: 120
  - op: wait_for         # the form has loaded its data
    target: {automation_id: lblStatus}
    text: Ready
  - op: click
    target: {automation_id: btnPost}
    timeout: 60          # per step, over the workflow's timeout
    retries: 3
  • Each step waits for its control up to its timeout. click, type and select also wait until the control is enabled and visible (a form that is still loading greys its buttons out).
  • wait_for waits until a control is there, shows a text, or with gone: true has gone.
  • retries applies to click, double_click, type, select, keys, expect and expect_image. Between tries, replay pauses (1 s, 2 s, … up to 5 s) and closes any dismiss dialog. An error in the workflow itself (a missing variable) is not retried.
  • dismiss lists at most 10 targets, checked like any target. When a control is not found, or something waits to go away, replay clicks the dismiss controls that are on screen and looks again, up to 3 times per lookup. The report and the console name every dialog closed, and how many tries a step took.

Visual checks (expect_image)

expect compares text. When what matters is drawn (a chart, a signature, a colour, a layout), expect_image compares pixels with a baseline image:

yaml
  - op: expect_image
    target: {automation_id: chartRevenue}   # without a target: the app's top window
    image: revenue-chart.png                # a file name in <workflow name>.baselines/ next to the workflow
    tolerance: 0.01                         # share of pixels that may differ (default 0.01, i.e. 1%)
    ignore: [[0, 0, 120, 24]]               # regions [x, y, width, height] that change every run (a date, a clock)
  • The first run with no baseline saves what it captured and passes; the report says so. Commit the baselines folder with the workflow. --update-baselines replaces them after an intended change.
  • A pixel counts as different when a colour channel differs by more than 32 of 255, so anti-aliasing and ClearType do not fail a check. A different size (another screen scale or window size) fails with both sizes named.
  • The report shows the baseline, what was captured, and the differences in red, with the share of pixels that differ (images/NNN-baseline|actual|diff.png in the run folder).
  • Image names are file names only (letters, digits, _, -, ending .png). A path, .., or a link that leads out of the baselines folder is refused.
  • Run visual checks on one machine with a fixed resolution and scale (see DESKTOP-CI.md).

Unattended runs

Replay needs a signed-in, unlocked Windows session. Before it starts the app, it checks the session and fails at once with the reason if it is a Windows service (session 0), a locked screen, or a disconnected Remote Desktop session. forgeqe desktop doctor runs the same check. To run the suite every night on a machine of your own, see DESKTOP-CI.md: a runner set up in the user's session (ci/desktop-runner/prepare-runner.ps1), a nightly workflow (ci/desktop-runner/desktop-nightly.example.yml), and --junit results.

Updating UFO²

UFO² is pinned to one commit. Moving it is checked before it is pinned:

  1. The ufo-update job of .github/workflows/ci.yml runs weekly, or by hand (Run workflow, job ufo-update) with a commit. It sets up that UFO on Windows (FORGEQE_UFO_COMMIT, which takes a full 40-character commit id only), runs the adapter tests against its real code and a scripted explore of Notepad, and says in the job summary whether it passed.
  2. When it passed: bun scripts/ufo-bump.ts <commit> writes the new pin into src/desktop/setup.ts, py/forgeqe_desktop/ufo_runner.py and this page. Run the tests and push; the Windows CI job tests the pin again.

When it fails, the failing step names what changed in UFO (usually the action executor or the config layout that the adapter wraps). Keep the pin until the adapter is updated for it.

Checks of the outcome

A recording of actions only proves the steps run. When UFO has finished, and before the app closes, explore shows the model the goal and the app's controls with the text each shows now, and the model picks up to three whose text proves the goal (a status message, a saved value), by number, with the part of the text to expect. A check is kept only when that text really is in that control, so the model can choose among what is on screen but never invent it; text holding a desktop.secrets value is never offered. The checks are saved as expect steps at the end of the workflow, and the report says how many there are. With none, the report and the agent say so: add expect steps by hand.

Recording

UFO's own step log names the control and a per-step id, not its AutomationId, so the adapter wraps UFO's ActionExecutor.execute and records each action with the UIA properties of the control it acted on. record.py turns that trace into steps: click_input → click/double_click, set_edit_text → type, keyboard_input → keys (one allowed chord) or type, select → select. Coordinate clicks, scrolls and anything else are listed as not recorded in the report. A typed value equal to a desktop.secrets value is written as its placeholder; typed text that only looks like {{…}} is kept literal, so a model told to type {{secret:X}} cannot make replay type the secret.

Security

The YAML, the trace, the app's screen text and the model's replies can all be written by someone else. The rules in the repository's CLAUDE.md apply; for this engine:

  • No command in the workflow. app: names a desktop.apps entry: an absolute .exe (a .bat or .cmd would run through cmd.exe, which reparses its arguments) and an argument list, started with subprocess.Popen([path, *args]).
  • UFO gets UI tools only. Each explore writes UFO's mcp.yaml with UICollector, HostUIExecutor and AppUIExecutor. UFO's default CommandLineExecutor, which runs shell commands, and its Word, Excel, PowerPoint and PDF executors are left out. The adapter starts the app, so UFO never launches a program.
  • Keys: during explore the adapter refuses UFO's Windows and menu keys too, and the report lists what it refused.
  • No one at the console: UFO's safe guard asks before sensitive actions; explore answers no, since nobody is there.
  • Environment: the engine process gets system settings, the profile's key variable (or Entra's) and desktop.secrets, nothing else (desktopEnvNames).
  • Explore controls the real mouse and keyboard. It asks before starting (--yes to skip; required with no terminal). Use a test machine or VM and a test account.
  • Agent tools: a workflow path the agent gives must be a .yaml inside the working folder or the reports folder (real path, so a link out is refused); variables are names and text only; heals are reported, never written.
  • Healing: the model picks one of the window's real controls by number; control text is passed as data, and any other answer heals nothing. Heals are written into the workflow only when accepted.
  • Report: every value is escaped; images are only the engine's own steps/NNN.png and images/NNN-baseline|actual|diff.png.
  • Baselines: expect_image names are file names, and their real path must be inside the workflow's baselines folder, so a workflow cannot read or overwrite another file.
  • Unattended machines: prepare-runner.ps1 never handles a password; automatic sign-in uses Sysinternals Autologon, which stores it encrypted.
  • Install: UFO is cloned and checked out at the pinned commit (or FORGEQE_UFO_COMMIT, a full commit id only), and the checkout's HEAD is verified; replay's packages are pinned to exact versions.

Before first use on Windows

Verified in CI: setup with and without --ufo, replay, healing, the CLI, and UFO's own code with the adapter (its config, its model calls routed to the profile, the action trace, UI tools only). Still to check, on a Windows test machine with your apps:

  1. UIA coverage: Inspect.exe shows the controls that matter with stable AutomationIds. Custom grids, Java (needs Java Access Bridge), Delphi, SAP GUI and Citrix (vision only) are where UIA falls short.
  2. Explore with your model: one goal with an Azure profile and one with auth: entra; trace.json has the controls' AutomationIds, and ufo/ has UFO's logs.
  3. Replay: the recorded workflow passes 10 runs out of 10; a renamed control is healed.
  4. Cost and time of one explore, from forgeqe usage.

Found by the Windows runs and fixed: UFO's pins need Python 3.10 (pandas 1.4.3); UFO prints emoji panels to stdout, which is the engine's event stream and crashed on the cp1252 console (now sent to stderr, characters replaced); an error in UFO's own evaluation lost the recorded actions (the trace is now kept and saved).

Next steps

  1. The checks above.
  2. forgeqe suite and test cases from Azure DevOps, Jira and Xray for desktop workflows, with results written back.
  3. Scribe: export a workflow as pywinauto code (or C# with FlaUI) in the user's repo.
  4. App adapters where UIA falls short: Java Access Bridge, SAP GUI scripting, vision-only for Citrix.
  5. A pipeline template for a self-hosted Windows runner signed in to a desktop session.

Running desktop workflows unattended (nightly CI)

Desktop replay clicks and types into real windows. It needs what a person at the machine would have: a signed-in Windows session, unlocked, with a screen. GitHub's hosted windows-2022 runners have that, and ForgeQE's own CI runs Notepad there. Your own apps usually need a machine of your own, with the app installed, its test database, and a network route to its servers. This page sets one up as a GitHub Actions self-hosted runner. The same rules apply to Azure DevOps agents and Jenkins nodes.

What breaks a desktop run, and how ForgeQE tells you

SituationEffectFix
The runner is a Windows serviceSession 0 has no desktop; every control lookup times outRun the runner in the user's session (below)
The screen is lockedWindows sends no input to appsNo inactivity lock, no screen-saver lock
A Remote Desktop session was disconnectedThe session is lockedLeave Remote Desktop with tscon (below), or don't use it on the test machine
The machine slept or the screen turned offInput and screenshots stopPower plan: never sleep, never turn off the screen
After a restart nobody signs inNo session at allSysinternals Autologon

Before each replay or explore, the engine checks the session (py/forgeqe_desktop/session.py). On a session it cannot drive, the run fails at once with the reason, instead of timing out step by step. forgeqe desktop doctor runs the same check from a shell or a job:

text
engine   ready (C:\Users\qa\.forgeqe\desktop\.venv\Scripts\python.exe)
explore  not set up (forgeqe desktop setup --ufo)
session  1, screen 1920x1080
desktop  logged in and unlocked

It exits with 1 when the desktop cannot be driven, so a job stops before the suite runs.

Set up the test machine

Use a test machine or VM, and a test account: the runner's jobs control its mouse and keyboard.

  1. Sign in as the account the tests will run as.

  2. Install the runner (GitHub: Settings → Actions → Runners → New self-hosted runner). Give it the label forgeqe-desktop. When config.cmd asks whether to run as a service, answer no.

  3. Run ci/desktop-runner/prepare-runner.ps1 in an elevated PowerShell (-WhatIf first to see the changes):

    powershell
    .\prepare-runner.ps1 -RunnerDir C:\actions-runner -WhatIf
.\prepare-runner.ps1 -RunnerDir C:\actions-runner

    It stops sleep, screen-off and the screen-saver lock. It turns the inactivity lock off and registers a logon task that starts run.cmd in this account's session. It warns you if the runner is also installed as a service. It never handles a password.

  4. Sign in automatically after a restart with Sysinternals Autologon, run as the test account. It stores the password encrypted by Windows. Never put the password in a script, a registry value you write yourself, or the repository.

  5. Fix the screen size. A visual check (expect_image) compares pixels, so the resolution and scale (100%, 125%) must match the machine where its baselines were saved. Set them once and leave them.

  6. Restart and check that the runner shows as idle on GitHub. Then run forgeqe desktop doctor from a job.

Leaving Remote Desktop without locking the session

Closing a Remote Desktop window disconnects the session, and Windows locks it. To leave it running instead, hand the session back to the machine's console. In an elevated prompt inside the Remote Desktop session:

bat
for /f "skip=1 tokens=3" %s in ('query user %USERNAME%') do tscon %s /dest:console

On a VM, the hypervisor's console (Hyper-V, VMware, VirtualBox) needs no such step.

The nightly job

ci/desktop-runner/desktop-nightly.example.yml is a ready workflow. Copy it into .github/workflows/ in the repository that holds your desktop workflows. It:

  • runs at night on [self-hosted, windows, forgeqe-desktop], one run at a time;
  • installs a pinned ForgeQE and runs forgeqe desktop setup and forgeqe desktop doctor;
  • replays every workflow in a folder: forgeqe desktop replay desktop-tests/ --junit results/desktop.xml;
  • keeps the reports, screenshots and image diffs as an artifact.

--junit writes one test case per workflow (and per CSV row with --data), which CI servers show as test results. runs.json in the output folder has the same, with the path of each report.

Secrets the workflows type ({{secret:NAME}}) come from the CI's secret store as environment variables, and must be listed in desktop.secrets or the environment's secrets in env.yaml. Pick the environment with FORGEQE_ENV or --env.

Healing calls the model. In a nightly run, either give the job the model's key so moved controls are healed and reported in heals.json (never written into the workflow without --accept-heals), or pass --no-heal so a moved control fails the run.

08

Mobile

Android and iOS apps through Appium or Maestro: explore a goal, replay, run the team's flows

Mobile

Automate Android and iOS apps

Carries out a plain-language goal in an Android or iOS app through Appium and saves it as a mobile workflow with checks of the outcome; replays workflows with no model on Appium (healing a moved control) or Maestro; runs the team's own Maestro flows after checking them against an allowlist; uploads an app build to TestMu AI and saves its lt:// id as the app's build. Apps come from env.yaml (kind: mobile) or forgeqe.yaml mobile:.

Ask it
  • Sign in to the courier app and accept the first delivery, and save it as a test
  • Replay the courier login on Maestro
  • Run our Maestro flows in .maestro/
  • Upload builds/courier.apk to TestMu for the courier app
What it needs
  • OptionalA live model profile in llms.json (an API key or Microsoft Entra ID sign-in)
  • RequiredMobile apps: env.yaml (kind: mobile) or mobile.apps in forgeqe.yaml
  • You givean Appium server with a device or emulator (or a device cloud), or Maestro installed; the app in env.yaml or forgeqe.yaml mobile.apps
Limits
  • Explore runs on Appium only
  • Maestro replays do not heal moved controls or compare images (expect_image)
  • On TestMu AI, Maestro (through HyperExecute) refuses workflows that type secrets; replay those there with --driver appium
Tools

mobile_explore mobile_replay mobile_maestro mobile_upload

In detail

Mobile apps (Android, iOS)

ForgeQE drives Android and iOS apps two ways: through Appium, step by step (it can explore a goal with the model, and heals a control that moved), and through Maestro (fast replays, and your team's own Maestro flows). In the shell, ask for a goal in the app ("sign in to the courier app and accept the first delivery") and ForgeQE's agent explores it on your device or emulator and saves a workflow with checks of the outcome. The same from the command line:

bash
forgeqe mobile doctor                                         # does the Appium server answer, is maestro installed?
forgeqe mobile explore "Sign in and accept the first delivery" --app courier
forgeqe mobile replay <run folder>/workflow.yaml              # on Appium (mobile.driver), healing moved controls
forgeqe mobile replay workflow.yaml --driver maestro          # the same workflow as a Maestro flow
forgeqe mobile replay mobile-tests/ --junit results.xml       # every workflow in a folder, results for CI
forgeqe mobile maestro .maestro/                              # your team's Maestro flows, checked first
forgeqe mobile flow workflow.yaml > login.flow.yaml           # a workflow as a Maestro flow, to keep

Apps come from env.yaml (kind: mobile, platform: android or ios, app_id, and optionally activity and an app build to install) or mobile.apps in forgeqe.yaml. mobile.appium names the Appium server (default http://127.0.0.1:4723, or a device cloud's hub with its user and key in environment variables), and {{secret:NAME}} reads only mobile.secrets (and the environment's secrets). A flow kept with forgeqe mobile flow reads each secret as ${MAESTRO_<NAME>}, never its value, so set MAESTRO_<NAME> when you run it outside ForgeQE; a workflow that types a secret not listed there is refused. Team Maestro flows run only with UI commands: scripts, JavaScript in ${…}, Maestro's AI commands and file writes are refused. Details: docs/MOBILE.md.

On TestMu AI (formerly LambdaTest)

Both drivers run on TestMu AI's devices. Upload the app build once, then use the lt:// id it prints as the app's app (in env.yaml or mobile.apps; ForgeQE adds it to Appium's LT:Options itself):

bash
forgeqe mobile upload courier.apk                             # prints lt://APP…, for TestMu's real devices
forgeqe mobile upload courier.apk --virtual                   # for TestMu's emulators and simulators instead
forgeqe mobile upload courier.apk --name courier-nightly      # the name TestMu shows for the build

The user name and access key come from LT_USERNAME and LT_ACCESS_KEY (or the variables username_env and access_key_env name), for example in a .env file in the project folder. They go only to the mobile engine, never to a flow or a model, and a user or key in the hub URL is refused.

yaml
mobile:
  apps:
    courier: { platform: android, app_id: com.contoso.courier, app: "lt://APP1016…" }
  appium:                                   # Appium on TestMu's mobile hub
    url: https://mobile-hub.lambdatest.com/wd/hub
    username_env: LT_USERNAME
    access_key_env: LT_ACCESS_KEY
    capabilities:
      "LT:Options": { w3c: true, isRealMobile: true, deviceName: "Pixel 6", platformVersion: "14" }
  maestro:
    testmu:                                 # Maestro on TestMu, through HyperExecute
      cli: ./hyperexecute                   # TestMu's hyperexecute CLI
      devices: ["Pixel 6-14"]               # name-version, or a pattern such as ".*-.*"
      real_device: true                     # false: TestMu's emulators
  • Appium needs nothing else: it talks to the hub with the user and key from the two variables.
  • Maestro runs through HyperExecute. mobile.maestro.testmu names TestMu's hyperexecute CLI (cli), the devices to run on (devices), and real devices or emulators (real_device). ForgeQE writes a HyperExecute project in the run folder (the flows in their folder layout, so runFlow still works, the app, fixed setup and run scripts, and hyperexecute.yaml) and starts the CLI. Team flows are checked against the allowlist first, as always. The access key is removed from the CLI's output and from every file in the run folder; the run prints the HyperExecute job link.
  • Secrets: a workflow that types a secret is refused on Maestro on TestMu (HyperExecute would need the value in its own vault). Replay it on TestMu with --driver appium instead. Maestro on TestMu runs Android apps; run iOS apps there through Appium.

In the shell, ask in words: "upload builds/courier.apk to TestMu for the courier app". After you agree, ForgeQE uploads it and writes the lt:// id into that app's app: in forgeqe.yaml (an app from env.yaml gets the id to set there). The build must be inside the working folder; hidden folders and files named like credentials are refused.

samples/mobile-testmu is a ready project with TestMu's Wikipedia sample app: copy .env.example to .env, upload the app with forgeqe mobile upload sample.apk, put the id in its forgeqe.yaml, download the hyperexecute CLI into the folder, then run forgeqe mobile replay wiki.yaml --driver appium, --driver maestro, and forgeqe mobile maestro .maestro. Details: docs/MOBILE.md.

ForgeQE mobile: Android and iOS apps

Status: the engine and its tests run on any OS, against a fake Appium server and a stand-in for Maestro. The CI job The android job of .github/workflows/ci.yml runs it on an Android emulator: Appium explores and replays the emulator's Settings app, and Maestro replays the same workflow. iOS runs the same code with the XCUITest driver, but no CI job tests it yet (it needs a macOS runner with a simulator).

ForgeQE drives mobile apps two ways, and one workflow format runs on both:

AppiumMaestro
Runsstep by step, through an Appium server (yours or a device cloud's)the whole workflow as one Maestro flow
Explore a goal with the modelyesno
Heal a control that movedyes, one model call, reported for reviewno
Visual checks (expect_image)yesno
Your team's own flowsn/aforgeqe mobile maestro, checked against an allowlist
Needsappium with UiAutomator2 (Android) or XCUITest (iOS), a device or emulatormaestro and a device or emulator

Use

bash
forgeqe mobile doctor                                   # does the Appium server answer, is maestro installed?
forgeqe mobile apps                                     # the mobile apps of the active environment
forgeqe mobile explore "Sign in as the courier and accept the first delivery" --app courier
forgeqe mobile replay .forgeqe/reports/mobile-explore-…/workflow.yaml
forgeqe mobile replay workflow.yaml --driver maestro
forgeqe mobile replay mobile-tests/ --data couriers.csv --junit results.xml
forgeqe mobile maestro .maestro/                        # the team's flows
forgeqe mobile flow workflow.yaml                       # print a workflow as a Maestro flow
forgeqe mobile validate workflow.yaml

Or ask ForgeQE's agent in the shell or forgeqe run: "sign in to the courier app and save it as a test", "replay the courier login on Maestro", "run our Maestro flows". Its tools are mobile_explore, mobile_replay and mobile_maestro. It asks before each one starts, and desktop_edit_workflow fixes a mobile workflow after a failed replay, the same way as a desktop one.

Apps and settings

Mobile apps come from the active environment in env.yaml, or from mobile.apps in forgeqe.yaml:

yaml
# env.yaml
environments:
  qa:
    apps:
      courier:
        kind: mobile
        platform: android               # or ios
        app_id: com.contoso.courier     # Android package or iOS bundle id
        activity: .MainActivity         # Android only, optional
        app: /builds/courier-qa.apk     # optional: install this build (.apk/.aab/.ipa/.app/.zip, https URL, bs://…)
        description: Driver app that confirms delivery
    secrets: [COURIER_PASSWORD_QA]
yaml
# forgeqe.yaml
mobile:
  driver: appium                        # appium | maestro: the default for replays
  appium:
    url: http://127.0.0.1:4723
    capabilities:                       # added to every session
      appium:udid: emulator-5554
    # username_env: BROWSERSTACK_USERNAME
    # access_key_env: BROWSERSTACK_ACCESS_KEY
  maestro:
    command: [maestro]
    # device: emulator-5554
  secrets: [COURIER_PASSWORD]
  heal: true
  screenshots: true

Each run writes a folder under the reports folder (mobile-explore-…, mobile-replay-…, mobile-maestro-…) with run.json, report.html, step screenshots, and for Maestro runs the flow, maestro.log and Maestro's JUnit report.

Workflow YAML (forgeqe-mobile-v1)

yaml
format: forgeqe-mobile-v1
name: Courier signs in
app: courier
timeout: 20
dismiss:
  - {name: Allow, control_type: android.widget.Button}   # permission dialogs, when they are in the way
steps:
  - op: launch
  - op: type
    target: {automation_id: username}
    text: "{{courier}}"
  - op: type
    target: {automation_id: password}
    text: "{{secret:COURIER_PASSWORD}}"
  - op: tap
    target: {name: Sign in}
  - op: wait_for
    target: {automation_id: progress}
    gone: true
  - op: scroll_to
    target: {name: Deliveries}
  - op: expect
    target: {automation_id: status}
    text: Welcome
  • Ops: launch, tap, double_tap, long_press, type, expect, expect_image, swipe (direction up, down, left or right, optionally on a target), scroll_to (scrolls until the target is on screen), back, hide_keyboard, keys (enter, back, tab, delete, search), wait, wait_for, close. Timeouts, retries and dismiss work as in desktop workflows. Explore saves a tap on a system dialog (another package's control, such as Android's notification prompt) under dismiss, not as a step, so the workflow still runs where the dialog never shows; on Maestro, each dismiss target is tapped after a launch if it is there.
  • Targets use the desktop keys, mapped per platform: automation_id is the Android resource-id (with or without the package:id/ prefix) or the iOS accessibility identifier; name is the Android text or content-desc, or the iOS label; control_type is the Android class (android.widget.Button) or the iOS type (XCUIElementTypeButton); index picks one of several matches.

Explore

The model sees the goal, its steps so far, the numbered controls on screen with the text each shows, and a screenshot when the profile takes images. It answers with one action on a control by its number (tap, long press, type), a swipe or scroll, back, or done. So it can only act on a control that is on screen. When it says done, it picks checks of the outcome from the controls' current text, kept only when that text is really there, as on the desktop. The app's text is passed as data.

Maestro

ForgeQE workflows on Maestro. --driver maestro writes the workflow as a Maestro flow (flow.yaml in the run folder) and runs maestro test. Maestro's debug output gives each workflow step its result. Variables are filled in first. Values are matched literally, so Maestro's regular expressions never see them as patterns. A secret becomes ${MAESTRO_NAME}, which Maestro reads from the environment ForgeQE gives it, so a secret's value never lands in the flow file or on a command line. A value holding ${…} is refused, because Maestro would run it as JavaScript.

Your team's flows. forgeqe mobile maestro <files or folders> checks each flow, and every flow it runs, before Maestro gets it:

  • Only UI commands pass: launchApp, tapOn, inputText, assertVisible, scrollUntilVisible, swipe, extendedWaitUntil, runFlow, repeat, retry and the like.
  • These are refused:
    • runScript, evalScript and assertTrue, which run JavaScript.
    • Maestro's AI commands, which send screenshots to a service.
    • takeScreenshot, recordings and addMedia, which read or write files.
  • ${…} may only name a variable (${USERNAME}), never an expression.
  • when: {true: …} is refused, because it is JavaScript.
  • openLink takes http(s) or the app's own deep links, never javascript:, file: or intent:.
  • runFlow files must be .yaml inside the working folder; links out of it are refused too. Nesting stops at 5 levels.

A refused flow does not run, and the error names the command.

TestMu AI (formerly LambdaTest)

Both drivers run on TestMu's devices. Upload the app once, then use the lt:// id it returns as the app's app:

bash
forgeqe mobile upload courier.apk          # → lt://APP…  (--virtual for TestMu's emulators)

From the shell, ask in words ("upload builds/courier.apk to TestMu for courier"): mobile_upload asks first, uploads the build (it must be inside the working folder), and saves the lt:// id as that app's app: in forgeqe.yaml.

The user and key come from LT_USERNAME and LT_ACCESS_KEY (or the variables the config names), for example in a .env file in the project folder. samples/mobile-testmu/ is a ready project with TestMu's Wikipedia sample app.

yaml
mobile:
  apps:
    courier: { platform: android, app_id: com.contoso.courier, app: "lt://APP1016…" }
  appium:                                   # Appium on TestMu's mobile hub
    url: https://mobile-hub.lambdatest.com/wd/hub
    username_env: LT_USERNAME
    access_key_env: LT_ACCESS_KEY
    capabilities:
      "LT:Options": { w3c: true, isRealMobile: true, deviceName: "Pixel 6", platformVersion: "14", build: nightly }   # app: added from the app's own
  maestro:
    testmu:                                 # Maestro on TestMu, through HyperExecute
      cli: /opt/hyperexecute                # TestMu's hyperexecute CLI
      devices: ["Pixel 6-14"]               # name-version, or a pattern such as ".*-.*"
      real_device: true                     # false: TestMu's emulators
      # username_env: LT_USERNAME           # the defaults
      # access_key_env: LT_ACCESS_KEY
  • Appium needs nothing else: the Appium driver talks to the hub with the user and key from the two variables.
  • Maestro runs through HyperExecute. ForgeQE writes a HyperExecute project in the run folder and starts the CLI. The project holds the flows in their folder layout (so runFlow still works), the app (an lt:// id is passed as the device's appId; a build file is uploaded with the project), a fixed setup script (Android platform tools 35.0.2 and Maestro 2.6.1 from its release, both checked against their SHA-256) and a fixed run script, and hyperexecute.yaml. Team flows are checked against the allowlist first, as always. Flow file names may only use letters, digits and _ . - / (and one leading dot, for .maestro/), because HyperExecute's shell reads them. After the job, ForgeQE downloads its artifacts (Maestro's command results and JUnit reports), so each step gets its own result in the report.
  • Secrets on Maestro on TestMu: a workflow that types a secret is refused there for now (HyperExecute would need the value in its own vault). Replay it on TestMu with --driver appium.
  • The access key is passed to the CLI as an argument. It is removed from the CLI's output and from every file in the run folder. Neither the user name nor the key is passed to the flows' environment.
  • The testmu job of .github/workflows/ci.yml (Run workflow, job testmu) runs all three on TestMu (Appium, Maestro with a workflow, a team flow) with TestMu's Wikipedia sample app, once the repository has the secrets LT_USERNAME and LT_ACCESS_KEY.

Security

  • No commands from the workflow or the model. app: names a configured app (a platform and an id). Appium is spoken to over HTTP, and maestro is started with an argument list, never a shell.
  • Locators go into XPath as quoted literals, so a target cannot change the query.
  • Secrets: {{secret:NAME}} reads only mobile.secrets and the environment's secrets, and ForgeQE's own keys are refused there. Explore types a secret from its placeholder, so the model never sees the value.
  • The engine's environment holds system settings, the profile's key, the listed secrets, the cloud's user and key, and JAVA_HOME / ANDROID_HOME, nothing else.
  • Cloud credentials come from the variables mobile.appium.username_env and access_key_env name; a user or key in the URL is refused. TLS is verified as usual.
  • App builds are absolute paths to an app file, https URLs, or a device cloud's own upload ids (bs://, lt://, storage:filename=); file:// and other schemes are refused.
09

Operations

Monitoring and CI pipelines

Operations

Services, mocks, workspace and browser in Docker or Podman

Starts the services a test run needs (a database, a queue, the API under test) in Docker or Podman, as forgeqe.yaml declares them, and waits until each is ready; stops them, and shows their status and logs. Starts mocks of an API on WireMock, from the calls a browser run recorded or from an OpenAPI description, and can point an API app of the environment at the mock for the session. With containers.workspace, the coding agents' shell commands (build, restore, run) and every test ForgeQE runs go to a container of the project's image; with browser.provider docker, the browser agent, the replay and the coding agent's live browser use Chromium in a container.

Ask it
  • Start the database and the queue in Docker
  • Start the services from our docker-compose file
  • Mock the payments API from the last run
  • Create a mock service from openapi.yaml and point order-api at it
  • Show the logs of the db container
What it needs
  • RequiredDocker or Podman on PATH; the services to start in containers.services in forgeqe.yaml (mocks need none)
Limits
  • Starts only services declared in forgeqe.yaml containers.services; it never runs an image, flag or mount from a request
  • No privileged containers, host network, or access to the container runtime's socket; ports on 127.0.0.1 only; mounts read-only from inside the project
  • Mocks answer REST calls (WireMock); GraphQL, SOAP and gRPC are not mocked
  • Reads docker-compose files only for their services' image, ports, environment, bind mounts, command and depends_on; refuses build, privileged, network_mode, cap_add, devices and env_file; leaves named volumes out
  • The workspace mounts the repository with its credential files (.env, .npmrc, keys) covered; the agents and the model key stay on this machine; ForgeQE's own agent keeps this machine's shell
  • A browser in a container reaches this machine's apps as host.docker.internal, not localhost; it is headless, or visible on a virtual display, never on this screen
Tools

services mock_service

In detail

Containers and mock services

When Docker or Podman is installed, ForgeQE can start the services a test run needs, and mock an API the tests should not call. Ask in the shell: "start the database and the queue", "mock the payments API from the last run", "make a mock from openapi.yaml and point order-api at it", "show the db logs". Full reference: docs/CONTAINERS.md.

The project declares its services in forgeqe.yaml. ForgeQE starts only these, and builds each command itself:

yaml
containers:
  # runtime: podman                     # default: docker, else podman, on PATH
  services:
    db:
      image: postgres:16.4              # an exact version or a digest; never latest
      ports: [5432]                     # or "15432:5432" (this machine:container); always 127.0.0.1
      env: { POSTGRES_DB: shop, POSTGRES_USER: shop }
      secrets: [POSTGRES_PASSWORD]      # names of environment variables (.env works), passed by name
      volumes: ["./db/init:/docker-entrypoint-initdb.d"]   # read-only, inside the project
      ready: { tcp: 5432 }              # or { http: /health, port: 8080 }
      timeout: 90
bash
forgeqe services                 # declared services, what runs, whether Docker answers
forgeqe services up db           # start and wait until ready; keeps running after the command
forgeqe services logs db --lines 50
forgeqe services down            # stop this project's containers, mocks included
  • Mocks: mock_service builds WireMock stubs. From a run, they answer what the app really got. From an OpenAPI or Swagger description, or a contract set, they answer with its examples, or values made from its schemas. The mock runs on a free port of 127.0.0.1 (wiremock/wiremock:3.9.1, or containers.mock.image), and its stubs are in .forgeqe/mocks/<name>/mappings. With for_app, an API app of the active environment points at the mock for the rest of the session, so API tests and the tests ForgeQE runs (FORGEQE_APP_<APP>_URL) use it.
  • Session: what the agent starts (services or mocks) stops when the shell, forgeqe run, or forgeqe mcp ends. forgeqe services up keeps its services running until forgeqe services down.
  • Asked first: services up and mock_service ask before they start anything.

Guard rails:

  • Fixed run settings: every container gets no new privileges, a process limit, ports on 127.0.0.1, read-only mounts, and labels with the project, so ForgeQE stops only its own containers.
  • Refused service settings: privileged, network, cap_add, devices, user, and anything else that is not a service setting. Also refused: a port bound to another address, an image without an exact version, and a secret that is one of ForgeQE's own keys.
  • Mounts: inside the project only. A mount that is or holds a hidden file (.env, .git), a file named like a key, or a link is refused, because a container can send what it reads anywhere.
  • The docker, podman and compose programs only see the variables they need to find their daemon, plus the service's listed secrets.
  • Not in the agents' shell: docker, podman, nerdctl and compose commands are refused for ForgeQE's agent and Scribe. Containers start only through these tools.

Compose files. containers.compose: docker-compose.yml reads the file's services into the same list, checked by the same rules (forgeqe.yaml's own declaration of a service wins):

  • Translated: image, ports, environment, bind mounts, command and depends_on. A ${NAME} value becomes a secret passed by name.
  • Left out: named volumes. Mounts made read-only are listed in the notes.
  • Ignored: container_name, restart, healthcheck and networks.
  • Refused: build, privileged, network_mode, cap_add, devices, pid and env_file.

x-forgeqe: { ready: … } sets the ready check. Without it, a service is ready when its first port takes connections. The project's containers share their own network, so services reach each other by name (db:5432).

Docker as the workspace and the browser. Two settings move the rest of the work into containers:

yaml
browser:
  provider: docker                 # the browser agent, the replay and Scribe's live browser use Chromium in a container
containers:
  workspace:                       # Scribe's shell commands and every test ForgeQE runs use this image
    image: mcr.microsoft.com/playwright/dotnet:v1.63.0-noble
    env: { NEXUS_ENVIRONMENT: CI }
  browser:                         # optional
    headless: false                # visible on a virtual display inside the container (some sites refuse headless)
  • Workspace: Scribe builds, restores, runs and fixes tests in a Linux container of the project's image, with the repository at the same path. Each test run gets its own container from that image, and HOME is a volume of the project's, so package caches outlive the session. The agents and the model key stay on this machine, and ForgeQE's own agent keeps this machine's shell. .env, keys and other credential files in the repository are covered inside the containers.
  • Browser: each browser run gets a fresh container, reached through a gateway that wants a random token. A declared service is reached by its name; an app on this machine is host.docker.internal, not localhost. --browser-provider docker picks it for one run.
  • FORGEQE_HOST_RUN=1 runs Scribe's commands and the tests on this machine for a session.

Tests in a container. With containers.tests, run_tests runs the repository's tests in that image instead of on this machine, after starting the services it lists:

yaml
containers:
  tests:
    image: mcr.microsoft.com/playwright:v1.48.2-jammy
    services: [db]
    secrets: [TEST_DB_PASSWORD]
  • What runs: the command you confirmed, inside the container, in the repository (mounted at its own path, so the result files land where ForgeQE reads them).
  • User: your user.
  • Variables: only the environment's FORGEQE_* variables, the saved logins' variables, and the listed env and secrets.
  • To run on this machine instead: ask to run the tests on this machine (in_container: false).
  • Machines: Linux and macOS.

Testcontainers. When a test needs a real database or queue, or the repository already uses Testcontainers, Scribe's testcontainers skill writes the test to start it from code. It reuses the repository's fixtures, pins images, waits for readiness, and uses the mapped port. It never uses privileged mode, the host network, or the Docker socket.

ForgeQE containers: services and mocks in Docker or Podman

Status: the tests run against a fake runtime on any OS. They check the commands ForgeQE builds, the checks on config, compose files, mounts and secrets, the mock stubs, and tests in a container. tests/containers.live.test.ts runs on real Docker (and is skipped without it):

  • Postgres from forgeqe.yaml and from a compose file.
  • A WireMock mock that answers a recorded run's calls.
  • A tests container that reaches the database by name and writes its results into the repository as this machine's user.

The containers job of .github/workflows/ci.yml runs both on GitHub's Docker. There, a skipped live test fails the job.

ForgeQE uses Docker or Podman, when one is installed, for five jobs:

  • Services a test run needs: a database, a queue, a cache, or the API under test. The project declares them in forgeqe.yaml, or in a docker-compose file that forgeqe.yaml names.
  • Mocks of an API: WireMock stubs made from the calls a browser run recorded, or from an OpenAPI description, so the tests can run without the real service.
  • The repository's tests in a container: run_tests runs the command the user confirmed in an image, instead of on this machine, after starting the services the tests need.
  • A workspace for the coding agents: with containers.workspace, Scribe's shell commands (build, restore, run, git) and every test ForgeQE runs for it go to a container of the project's image. The agents stay on this machine.
  • A browser in a container: with browser.provider: docker, the browser agent, the replay and Scribe's live browser drive Chromium in a container of Microsoft's Playwright image.

Scribe also has a testcontainers skill, for tests that start their own services from code (below).

ForgeQE's agent uses them through two tools, services and mock_service. The CLI has forgeqe services. When neither Docker nor Podman is on PATH, the agent isn't offered these tools.

Use

bash
forgeqe services                     # the declared services, what runs, whether the runtime answers
forgeqe services up                  # start every declared service and wait until each is ready
forgeqe services up db queue         # just these
forgeqe services logs db --lines 50  # last lines, secrets removed
forgeqe services down                # stop and remove this project's containers (mocks included)

In the shell or forgeqe run, you can ask, for example:

  • "start the database"
  • "is the queue up?"
  • "mock the payments API from the last run"
  • "make a mock service from openapi.yaml and point order-api at it"
  • "stop the services"

The agent asks before it starts anything.

Declaring services

yaml
# forgeqe.yaml
containers:
  runtime: docker                       # docker | podman; default: docker, else podman, on PATH
  services:
    db:
      description: Orders database for the API tests
      image: postgres:16.4              # an exact version, or name@sha256:<digest>
      ports: [5432]                     # 127.0.0.1:5432 -> 5432; "15432:5432" maps another port of this machine
      env:                              # plain values only
        POSTGRES_DB: shop
        POSTGRES_USER: shop
      secrets: [POSTGRES_PASSWORD]      # environment variable names; their values come from the environment or .env
      volumes:
        - ./db/init:/docker-entrypoint-initdb.d    # read-only; a folder or file inside the project
      command: ["-c", "log_statement=all"]         # arguments for the image's entrypoint
      ready: { tcp: 5432 }              # or { http: /health, port: 8080 }; without it: the container runs
      timeout: 90                       # seconds to wait until ready (default 60)
    queue:
      image: rabbitmq:3.13.7-management
      ports: [5672, 15672]
      ready: { http: /, port: 15672 }
  mock:
    image: wiremock/wiremock:3.9.1      # the default
SettingRule
Service nameLower-case letters, digits and -, starting with a letter. Names starting with mock- are kept for mocks.
imageMust have an exact tag or a digest. An untagged image or latest is refused, because it changes under the same name.
portsAlways bound to 127.0.0.1. An address such as 0.0.0.0:5432:5432 is refused.
envPlain values on one line. Secrets go under secrets.
secretsVariable names. They are passed to the runtime by name (--env NAME), so the value is never on a command line. One of ForgeQE's own keys (a model key, a tracker token) is refused.
volumesAlways read-only. Must be inside the folder of forgeqe.yaml, and can't be the whole folder.
commandA list of arguments, never one command string.
depends_onServices to start first.
Anything elseRefused with an error, not ignored. That includes privileged, network, cap_add, devices, user, pid and the socket.

Every service, whether from forgeqe.yaml or a compose file, can list depends_on. up starts a service after the ones it depends on; a circle is refused.

Compose files

yaml
containers:
  compose: docker-compose.yml          # or a list; inside the project

ForgeQE reads each service of the file and translates it into a forgeqe.yaml service. The same rules then check it, and a service declared in forgeqe.yaml wins over the compose file's service of the same name. Compose itself never runs.

ComposeForgeQE
imageRequired; an exact version or a digest. build is refused: build and tag the image first.
ports"5432", "15432:5432", "127.0.0.1:15432:5432", or the long form; always bound to 127.0.0.1. Any other address, and UDP, are refused.
environmentPlain values are passed as they are. KEY: ${KEY}, KEY alone in the list form, and KEY: ${KEY:-default} pass the variable by name as a secret. ${OTHER} under another name, and values that mix text and ${…}, are refused.
volumesBind mounts (./db/init:/docker-entrypoint-initdb.d) are mounted read-only, and :rw is noted. Named and anonymous volumes are left out, so the container keeps no data between runs. A path outside the project is refused when the service starts.
commandA list, or a string split into arguments (compose uses no shell, and neither does ForgeQE).
depends_onList or map form: the start order. condition is not used; ForgeQE waits until each service is ready.
x-forgeqeready, timeout, description, as in forgeqe.yaml. Without ready, a service with ports is ready when its first port takes connections.
container_name, restart, labels, healthcheck, networks, expose, deploy, profiles, …Ignored: ForgeQE names, labels and networks the containers itself.
privileged, network_mode, cap_add, devices, pid, ipc, user, security_opt, volumes_from, env_file, entrypoint, extends, …Refused, and the error names the setting.

What was left out (a named volume, a mount made read-only) is listed in notes.

The project network

Each project's containers join their own network, forgeqe-<project>, under their service name. So the API container reaches the database as db:5432, and the tests container reaches both. No other container joins the network, and no container is on the host's network. forgeqe services down with no names also removes the network.

Tests in a container

yaml
containers:
  tests:
    image: mcr.microsoft.com/playwright:v1.48.2-jammy   # an exact version; the tests' toolchain
    services: [db, order-api]          # started first (when not running yet); the tests reach them by name
    env: { DB_HOST: db }
    secrets: [TEST_DB_PASSWORD]

With containers.tests set, run_tests runs the tests in that image unless it is called with in_container: false. Its confirmation says where the tests will run. The container:

  • Runs the command the user confirmed with the image's own shell, sh -c "<command>", inside the container, never on this machine.
  • Mounts the repository read-write at its own path and works in it, so results land where ForgeQE reads them. On this machine, the tests already have the whole disk. A folder outside the repository that the results go to is mounted the same way.
  • Runs as this machine's user (--user uid:gid), so files it writes are yours. It also gets --security-opt no-new-privileges, --pids-limit 4096 and --rm, and it is removed by name when the run ends or is stopped.
  • Gets only these variables: CI=1, NO_COLOR, HOME=/tmp, the environment's FORGEQE_ACTIVE_ENV, FORGEQE_APP_* and FORGEQE_VAR_*, the saved logins' variables, and containers.tests env and secrets. Secrets are passed by name. ForgeQE's own keys are never passed.
  • Has secret values removed from its output, as on this machine.

This needs a Linux or macOS machine, because the repository is mounted at its own path.

The workspace

yaml
containers:
  workspace:
    image: mcr.microsoft.com/playwright/dotnet:v1.63.0-noble   # an exact version; the project's toolchain
    services: [db]                       # started first; reached by name
    env: { NEXUS_ENVIRONMENT: CI, DOTNET_ROLL_FORWARD: Major }
    secrets: [TEST_DB_PASSWORD]          # by name; values from the environment or .env

With containers.workspace set:

  • Scribe's shell runs in the container. The agent runtime runs each command as $SHELL -c <command>. ForgeQE sets SHELL to a script that runs the same command with docker exec … sh -c <command>, in the same folder, in the workspace container. A folder outside the repository is refused, since the container has only the repository. The agent is told its commands run in a Linux container of that image.
  • Every test ForgeQE runs goes to the same image: the coding loop's runs, heal, suite's existing tests and run_tests. Each run gets its own container (docker run --rm), so stopping a run removes it. Browsers the test starts are found with docker top, as ForgeQE finds them on this machine.
  • What stays on this machine: the agent runtime, the model key, ForgeQE's other secrets, the file tools (read, edit, write; the repository is the same folder in both places), and ForgeQE's own agent, which works with you on this machine and keeps its shell here. FORGEQE_HOST_RUN=1 runs everything on this machine for a session.
  • The container:
    • one per ForgeQE process and project, forgeqe-<project>-workspace-<id>, removed when ForgeQE ends;
    • the repository mounted read-write at its own path; credential files in it (.env, .env.* except templates, .npmrc, .netrc, *.pem, *.key, id_* keys) covered with an empty read-only file, and .ssh, .aws, .azure, .docker, .kube and .forgeqe/sessions with an empty folder;
    • HOME on the project's volume forgeqe-<project>-home, so NuGet, npm and pip caches outlive the session. Docker creates the volume for root, so ForgeQE runs chown in it once, with a fixed command;
    • this machine's user (--user uid:gid), no-new-privileges, --pids-limit 4096, the project network;
    • only CI=1, HOME, the env and secrets listed, and, in each command, the environment's FORGEQE_APP_* and FORGEQE_VAR_* and the saved logins' variables, by name.
  • containers.tests still names the image run_tests uses when it is set. Without it, run_tests uses the workspace's image.

This needs a Linux or macOS machine, as tests in a container do.

The browser in a container

yaml
browser:
  provider: docker        # or per run: --browser-provider docker
containers:
  browser:                # optional
    image: mcr.microsoft.com/playwright:v1.63.0-noble   # default; needs Node.js and /ms-playwright
    headless: false       # default: browser.headless; a visible browser runs on a virtual display (Xvfb)
  • One container per use: each browser run (a suite runs suite.parallel at once) gets its own, and a coding job gets one that its replay and live browser share. Each is removed when its run or job ends.
  • Connection: Chromium listens on 127.0.0.1 inside the container. CDP has no authentication of its own, so a small gateway in front of it (BROWSER_SERVER in src/containers/browser.ts, run with the image's Node.js) accepts a connection only with a random 48-character token in its path, compared in constant time, and refuses a browser Origin that is not local. The port is published on 127.0.0.1 only. The token reaches the engine, the replay and the live browser's MCP server through the environment, never a file or a command line.
  • Visible or headless: a visible browser runs on Xvfb inside the container. Sites that turn headless browsers away see an ordinary one, but you do not see it on your screen. Runs keep screenshots and traces as usual.
  • Reaching the app: the container joins the project network, so a declared service is reached by its name (http://order-api:8080). An app this machine serves is http://host.docker.internal:<port>; localhost is the container itself, and ForgeQE warns when a run starts there.
  • Replay: one pass, in a new browser context. The second pass (without Playwright's automation switches) needs a launch, which a running browser does not have.
  • A run explored in a container is replayed in one even when the provider is changed back later (run.json).

Testcontainers (Scribe)

When a test needs a real service, or the repository already uses Testcontainers (Java, .NET, Node, Python, Go), Scribe loads the testcontainers skill. It reuses the repository's containers and fixtures, pins images, waits for readiness, uses the mapped port, and sets the app's own configuration from it. It never uses privileged mode, the host network, the Docker socket, or a docker command from a test. These containers belong to the tests in the repository: the tests start and stop them, wherever they run.

Mocks

mock_service builds WireMock stubs and runs WireMock on a free port of 127.0.0.1:

  • From a run (the default: the latest run, or run_dir):
    • One stub per method, path and query of the app's own API calls, answering with the last status, content type and body the app got.
    • Other sites' calls are left out unless third_party is set.
    • Failed calls are left out.
    • Answers that were cut when recorded are listed under skipped.
  • From a description (spec: OpenAPI 3 or Swagger 2, a link or a file):
    • One stub per REST operation.
    • Each stub answers with the operation's first success status and a body from the schema's examples, or values made from its types.
    • A path parameter (/orders/{id}) matches any one path segment.
    • Instead of a description, you can give a contract set from api_contracts (set_id).
  • GraphQL, SOAP and gRPC are listed under skipped, not mocked.

A spec given as a link is fetched by ForgeQE itself, with no credentials, as api_contracts does. The stubs are written to .forgeqe/mocks/<name>/mappings/. You can read and edit them there. The folder is mounted read-only at /home/wiremock/mappings. WireMock runs with --enable-stub-cors, so a web app on another local port can call it.

With for_app: <api app>, the active environment's API app points at the mock for the rest of the session. That covers the API tools, and the tests ForgeQE runs, which read FORGEQE_APP_<APP>_URL. env.yaml itself is not changed.

Lifetime

  • Services and mocks the agent starts are removed when the shell, forgeqe run, or forgeqe mcp ends, as are the workspace and browser containers (forgeqe.kind workspace and browser). The home volume stays.
  • forgeqe services up keeps its services running after the command ends, until forgeqe services down.
  • Container names are forgeqe-<project>-<service>. <project> is a hash of the project folder, so two projects never share a container. Starting a service replaces its earlier container. Removing a container also removes the anonymous volumes its image declares (a database's data folder), so nothing is left on the machine. A container is never reused with old settings.
  • Labels: each container carries forgeqe=1, forgeqe.project, forgeqe.service and forgeqe.kind. down and status look only at this project's containers.

Security

ForgeQE's agent reads text other people control: tickets, web pages, repositories. A container command built from that text could mount the whole disk or run privileged, so:

  • No commands from the model. The agent names services that forgeqe.yaml declares, and ForgeQE builds the argument array (docker run …). It never uses a shell. Images, flags and mounts never come from a request.
  • The same restrictions for every container: --security-opt no-new-privileges, --pids-limit 1024, ports on 127.0.0.1, and read-only mounts. There is no setting to turn these off.
  • Mounts are checked like uploads (except a mock's stubs folder, which ForgeQE writes itself and mounts read-only). The path is resolved with realpath and must be inside the project. A mount that is or holds a hidden file or folder (.env, .git, .npmrc), a file named like a key (*.pem, id_rsa, llms.json) or a link is refused. A container can send what it reads anywhere.
  • Environment:
    • The docker and podman programs see only the system settings and the variables they need to find their daemon (DOCKER_HOST, DOCKER_CONTEXT, CONTAINER_HOST, …), plus the service's listed secrets.
    • A container gets only its env and secrets.
    • Logs and errors have secret values replaced with ***.
  • Not in the agents' shell: docker, podman, nerdctl, docker-compose and podman-compose are denied in the shell of ForgeQE's agent and of Scribe. So the only way to start a container is these tools.
  • Asked first: services up and mock_service ask the user before starting, as run_tests does.
Operations

Scheduled monitoring with alerts

Runs checks on a schedule and alerts Teams or email when one keeps failing, and again when it recovers.

Ask it
  • Check the login flow every 15 minutes and alert Teams
What it needs
  • RequiredA live model profile in llms.json (an API key or Microsoft Entra ID sign-in)
  • OptionalAlert channels: monitor.alerts.teams or monitor.alerts.email in forgeqe.yaml
Tools

monitor

In detail

Monitoring

forgeqe monitor runs a set of checks on a schedule and alerts Microsoft Teams and email when one breaks. A check is any test case forgeqe suite takes (section 7): a YAML or Gherkin file, a folder, Jira keys or JQL, an Azure DevOps plan, TestRail, and so on.

yaml
monitor:
  checks: [checks/smoke.yaml]
  every: 15m                  # 30s, 15m, 1h, 1h30m; at least 10s
  replay: verify              # verify | trust | off
  parallel: 1
  fail_after: 1               # failing rounds in a row before the first alert
  remind_every: 2h            # repeat while it keeps failing; default: never
  keep: 100                   # round folders kept
  write_back: false           # post each round's results to the source system
  # out_dir: ./.forgeqe/reports/monitor
  # report_url: https://reports.example.com/monitor
  alerts:
    teams:
      webhook_env: TEAMS_WEBHOOK_URL
    email:
      host: smtp.office365.com  # or SMTP_HOST
      port: 587                 # STARTTLS; 465 is TLS from the start (secure: true)
      username_env: SMTP_USERNAME
      password_env: SMTP_PASSWORD
      # from: ForgeQE <qa-bot@example.com>
      to: [qa-team@example.com, oncall@example.com]
bash
forgeqe monitor --test-alerts                  # send a sample alert through every channel
forgeqe monitor                                # monitor.checks every monitor.every, until Ctrl+C
forgeqe monitor checks/checkout.yaml --every 5m
forgeqe monitor --once                         # one round, then exit (cron, scheduled pipelines)

Each round runs the checks like a suite, without writing tests, and replays the flows from page memory (section 5). A flow that passed before costs one model call to confirm the expected results (replay: verify), or none (replay: trust, which trusts the replay without checking). A new or changed flow falls back to the full agent. Rounds start every apart; a round that overruns is followed by the next one straight away.

Alerts go out when something changes, not every round:

  • A check that fails fail_after rounds in a row gets an alert. With fail_after: 2 a single flaky failure is never reported.
  • While it keeps failing, nothing more is sent, unless remind_every is set.
  • When it passes again, a recovery alert says how long it was down.
  • A round that cannot run at all (a test system is down, a source is empty, the engine will not start) alerts as the check "ForgeQE monitor", and the other checks keep their state.

All changes in one round go out as one message. Teams gets an Adaptive Card with each check, why it failed and at which step, a link to the test case, and an "Open report" button. Email gets the same as text and HTML, with the failing checks' last screenshots attached (up to five).

For Teams, create an incoming webhook on the channel, or a Workflows flow with the trigger "When a Teams webhook request is received" and the action "Post card in a chat or channel"; both take the card as it is. Put the URL in TEAMS_WEBHOOK_URL. For email, any SMTP server works: Office 365 (smtp.office365.com:587, with SMTP AUTH allowed for the mailbox), Gmail with an app password, SendGrid, or a relay on your network (no username needed). The webhook URL and the SMTP password stay in the environment; if a send fails, the error never contains them.

The terminal shows one block per round:

text
Monitoring checks/smoke.yaml every 15m · replay verify · alerts: Teams, email to qa-team@example.com · Ctrl+C stops
[14:15] ✗ 4/5 passing · 38s · 6.2K tokens
    ✗ SHOP-12 Checkout: failing since 14:15
        The Pay button is missing on the payment page
    ↑ Alert sent: Teams, email
    Report  .forgeqe/reports/monitor/20260929-141500/report.html
    Next round at 14:30
[14:30] ✗ 4/5 passing · 35s · 5.9K tokens
    ✗ SHOP-12 Checkout: failing since 14:15 (2 rounds, already alerted)
    Report  .forgeqe/reports/monitor/20260929-143000/report.html
    Next round at 14:45

Files. Each round is a suite folder in .forgeqe/reports/monitor/<time>/ (report.html, junit.xml, suite.json, the runs, and alerts.json when it alerted), so the report's trends and flaky tests cover the rounds. Folders beyond keep are deleted. state.json in .forgeqe/reports/monitor/ remembers which checks are failing, since when, and when they were last alerted; delete it to start fresh. Set report_url to where you publish .forgeqe/reports/monitor/ and alerts link <report_url>/<round>/report.html; without it they name the local file.

Scheduling. Run forgeqe monitor in a long-lived session (tmux, a service, a container), or forgeqe monitor --once from cron or a scheduled pipeline. --once exits 0 when every check passed, 1 when any failed, and 2 when stopped. In a pipeline, cache .forgeqe/reports/monitor/state.json between runs; without it every run starts fresh, and a failure alerts every time. Only one monitor can use an out_dir at a time, so an overlapping cron run exits instead of racing.

You can also ask ForgeQE's agent. In the shell, "check the smoke cases once" runs one round and shows it as a card, and "monitor the smoke cases every 10 minutes" starts a monitor in the background: you keep working, each round appears as a card, "is anything failing?" shows the state, and "stop monitoring" ends it. A background monitor lives as long as the shell (or forgeqe mcp) and stops when it closes; forgeqe run refuses to start one, because it would stop when the command exits. While one is running, a one-off round on the same out_dir is refused rather than run twice. For monitoring that keeps going after you close the shell, "monitor the smoke cases every 10 minutes and alert the QA channel in Teams, always on" makes the agent set up monitor: in forgeqe.yaml, send a test alert, and tell you how to keep forgeqe monitor running (or add a scheduled pipeline).

Operations

Background jobs on a schedule

Runs ForgeQE commands (suites, monitor rounds, regression packs, healing, pull-request tests, browser goals) on a schedule or once in the jobs service, apart from the conversation and after it closes, and watches an existing GitHub Actions or Azure DevOps pipeline (read-only). A failed pipeline run or pack run is investigated: the healing agent works on each failure on a local branch of its own (a fix for review, or an app bug), never pushing. Says how each job goes when asked, shows runs as they end, and sends failures, recoveries and findings to Teams or email.

Ask it
  • Run the release-2.3 pack every weekday at 2am in the background
  • Watch the pipeline in the background and investigate failures
  • How are my jobs doing?
What it needs
  • OptionalA notification channel: notify.channels in forgeqe.yaml, each a Teams Workflows webhook (in an environment variable) or email
Tools

jobs

In detail

The jobs service: scheduled and long jobs apart from the shell

forgeqe jobs runs ForgeQE commands on a schedule, or once, in a service of their own, so they keep going while you work on something else in the shell, or with the shell closed. Each run is its own forgeqe process with its own output and session log; you ask how a job is going at any time, and its failures and recoveries go to Teams.

bash
forgeqe jobs add nightly-pack --cron "0 2 * * 1-5" -- pack run release-2.3   # weekdays at 02:00, this machine's time
forgeqe jobs add smoke --every 30m -- suite checks/smoke.yaml
forgeqe jobs add heal-login --now -- heal --focus login                     # once, now
forgeqe jobs                       # every job: running or how its last run ended, and when it runs next
forgeqe jobs status nightly-pack   # its last runs, and the last lines of the latest
forgeqe jobs logs nightly-pack --tail 100
forgeqe jobs run nightly-pack      # run it now as well
forgeqe jobs stop nightly-pack     # stop the run that is going
forgeqe jobs disable|enable|remove nightly-pack
forgeqe jobs shutdown              # stop the service; scheduled jobs wait until it starts again
  • What a job runs. One ForgeQE command: suite, monitor, run, heal, pr-tests, pack run, pack triage or pipeline watch, with its arguments after --. Nobody is there to answer a question while a job runs, so a run job is a browser run of the goal (--direct), and a pack run job carries --yes: adding the job is your approval, and the command you agree to shows it. It runs with the project's own forgeqe.yaml, model profile and approvals: a job cannot set --config, --profile or --yes, and nothing else is started (no shell, no other program). A monitor job runs one round each time it is due (--once), on the job's schedule; it sends its own monitor.alerts, so its job is quiet unless you add it with notices on.
  • When. --every 15m (at least 1m), --cron "0 2 * * 1-5" (minute hour day month weekday, local time), or neither: it runs when asked, and add starts it at once. --now also runs a scheduled job straight away. A run missed while the service was down starts once when it is back. When several are due, the one that has waited longest goes first.
  • How many. jobs.parallel in forgeqe.yaml (default 2) runs at once over all jobs, and never two runs of one job. A run past --timeout (default 6h, at most 24h) is stopped and marked timed out.
  • The service. forgeqe jobs add and forgeqe jobs run start it when it is not running, as a process of its own that outlives the command and the shell; forgeqe jobs serve runs it in the foreground (for a container or a system service). It is one per project, on this machine, until you stop it or the machine restarts. It listens on 127.0.0.1 only, with a random token kept in your own ForgeQE folder (~/.forgeqe/jobs/, readable by your user only, never in the project, which others may read), and refuses requests from web pages. A second service for the same project is refused. .forgeqe/jobs/ and .forgeqe/pipelines/ get their own .gitignore, so runs and logs are never committed.
  • Files. .forgeqe/jobs/<job>/job.json and, for each of its last 50 runs, runs/<run>/run.json and output.log; .forgeqe/jobs/events.jsonl lists what happened; .forgeqe/jobs/service.log is the service's own output. A run that was going when the service stopped is marked interrupted.
  • Watching a CI pipeline. forgeqe jobs add ci --every 10m -- pipeline watch --github org/app --workflow ci.yml --branch main (or --ado-project Shop --pipeline 12) looks at the pipeline's latest completed run each time, read-only: it starts, re-runs and changes nothing. A new failed run is triaged like a pack's (the JUnit or TRX files in its artifacts on GitHub, the Test Runs API on Azure DevOps; without results, the job and step that failed), in .forgeqe/reports/pipelines/. The job fails while the latest run is failed, so the team hears once when the pipeline breaks and once when it is green again; a cancelled run changes nothing. forgeqe pipeline watch … on its own does one look. GitHub needs the gh CLI signed in; Azure DevOps the same sign-in as the rest of ForgeQE.
  • Investigating failures. When a pipeline watch or pack run job fails, the failures that healing can decide (an element not found, an expected value that does not hold; not the environment, flaky tests or broken test code, and nothing while the environment is down) are investigated, at most 3 a run, jobs.parallel at once. Each goes to the healing agent in a git worktree of its own, on a new local branch forgeqe/investigate/<test>-<time>-<n>, from the commit the failing run tested when this clone has it (fetched when it can be, else HEAD, which the finding says). The agent re-runs the flow in the browser: when the flow works it heals the test, and the fix is committed on that branch for you to review; when the browser fails at the same step it is an app bug, and no branch is kept. Your checkout, its branch and its files are never touched; nothing is pushed or merged; the worktree is removed at the end. node_modules, .venv and venv from your checkout are linked into the worktree so the tests can run, and never committed. The failure is told at once (job_failed), the findings when they are in (job_investigated); forgeqe jobs status lists them, .forgeqe/jobs/<job>/runs/<run>/investigations.json and investigate.log have the detail, and forgeqe investigate <run folder> runs an investigation by hand. --no-investigate turns it off for a job.
  • From the shell. Ask ForgeQE's agent: "run the release-2.3 pack every weekday at 2am in the background" makes it propose the job, and ForgeQE asks you to agree to its exact command and schedule before adding it (Add the job / Don't add it). You keep working; when a job's run ends, a line says so in the conversation ("Job nightly-pack failed: …"), and "how are my jobs?" or "show the end of the nightly-pack log" get answers from the service. The agent adds a job only when you ask for one, and never one that runs anything but the commands above.
  • Notices. With notify: channels set up (section 17), a job that fails sends job_failed once (not every run while it keeps failing), job_recovered when it passes again, and a job that runs only when asked sends job_done when it ends. --no-notify keeps a job quiet.
yaml
jobs:
  parallel: 2

On company machines. Nothing here needs administrator rights:

WhatNeeds
The jobs serviceRuns as you, in your project and your ForgeQE folder. It listens on 127.0.0.1 only, so no firewall rule is needed. Endpoint security may see a background bun process: it is ForgeQE's own (in its install folder), which IT can allow. Jobs pause while the machine sleeps or is off and a missed run starts once when it is back; for always-on jobs, run forgeqe jobs serve on a VM or build agent under its service manager (systemd, a Windows Task Scheduler task "at log on", which needs no administrator, or a container).
