pipeline watch on GitHubThe gh CLI signed in (gh auth login) with read access to the repository's Actions. A fine-grained token needs Actions: read and Contents: read; with SAML single sign-on, authorize the token for the organization. GitHub Enterprise Server: --github host/owner/repo and gh auth login --hostname host.
pipeline watch on Azure DevOpsThe Azure DevOps sign-in ForgeQE already uses (forgeqe auth login azure_devops, or a token in ADO_PAT) with Build: read and Test Management: read. Azure DevOps Server works through its collection URL.
Investigating failuresgit, read access to fetch the failing commit, the tests' dependencies installed in your checkout, and a model: a key-based profile, or GitHub Copilot where the organization's policy allows Copilot CLI and the SDK. Branches are local; nothing is pushed.
Teams noticesA Workflows webhook ("post to a channel when a webhook request is received"), or an incoming webhook where the tenant still allows them; where neither is allowed, email.
Proxy and inspected HTTPSThe same as the rest of ForgeQE (section "Company networks"); gh uses HTTPS_PROXY. forgeqe doctor checks the machine and the network.

On Windows, the dependency folders are linked into worktrees as junctions (no administrator or developer mode needed), a stopped run ends at once rather than being asked to finish, and the token file is protected by your user profile's permissions.

Operations

Teams and email notifications

Tells the team in Microsoft Teams channels or by email when a suite fails or passes, when a release readiness verdict is in, and when the domain evaluation falls below its gate, with the failing cases, their bugs, and links to the report and the CI build. Each channel picks its events; the agent also posts a summary to a channel when asked.

Ask it
  • Post the results to the QA channel
  • Tell the release channel the verdict is NO-GO
What it needs
  • RequiredA notification channel: notify.channels in forgeqe.yaml, each a Teams Workflows webhook (in an environment variable) or email
Limits
  • Automatic notifications come from runs nobody is watching (forgeqe suite, forgeqe run, pipelines, the MCP server), not from the interactive shell, where the agent posts only when asked.
Tools

notify

In detail

Notifications

ForgeQE tells the team in Microsoft Teams channels, or by email, when something they should know happens. Each channel picks its events:

yaml
notify:
  report_url: https://reports.example.com/forgeqe   # where .forgeqe/reports is published, so cards link to each report
  channels:
    qa:
      teams: { webhook_env: TEAMS_WEBHOOK_URL }
      on: [suite_failed, eval_failed]
    release:
      teams: { webhook_env: TEAMS_RELEASE_WEBHOOK }
      on: [release]
    leads:
      email: { host: smtp.office365.com, port: 587, username_env: SMTP_USERNAME, password_env: SMTP_PASSWORD, to: [qa-leads@example.com] }
      on: [suite_failed, release]
EventWhenThe card shows
suite_failedA suite ends with a case that did not pass, or a test that did notEach failing case (up to 10) with its kind of failure, the test case and bug links, tests written, and the report and build
suite_passedEvery case passedThe count, how long it took, where it ran
releaseA release readiness verdictGO, GO WITH RISKS, or NO-GO, the counts, every blocker and risk, and the wiki page when published
eval_failedforgeqe eval domain --min falls below its gateCoverage for each variant and invented ids
job_failedA job of the jobs service fails or runs out of time, after passing or for the first time (not again while it keeps failing)The job, its command and schedule, how long it ran, its last lines of output, and where its output is
job_recoveredA job passes again after failingThe same, for the run that passed
job_doneA job that runs only when asked endsThe same, whether it passed or failed
job_investigatedThe investigation of a job's failed run is doneEach failure: a test out of date with its fix on a branch, an app bug, or not decided, with the agent's reasons

A channel without on gets suite_failed, release, eval_failed, job_failed, job_recovered, and job_investigated. Email adds the failure screenshots. A stopped suite is not announced. Monitoring keeps its own monitor.alerts, with the same cards.

Events are sent from runs nobody is watching: forgeqe suite in a pipeline or on a schedule, forgeqe run, and the MCP server. In the shell, where people try things out, nothing is sent on its own; ask instead ("post the results to the QA channel", "tell the release channel the verdict"), and ForgeQE's agent posts a card with notify.

Links: with report_url, cards open the report where the reports folder is published. In Azure Pipelines and GitHub Actions they also open the build, where the report is an artifact. Without either, the card names the report's path.

Setting up a Teams channel:

  1. In the channel, open Workflows and choose Post to a channel when a webhook request is received, then copy the URL it gives you. Microsoft is retiring the older Office 365 "Incoming Webhook" connectors; Workflows webhooks take the same cards.
  2. The URL is the secret (anyone with it can post): keep it in the environment, the system's credential store (forgeqe auth login TEAMS_WEBHOOK_URL), or a pipeline secret, never in forgeqe.yaml.
  3. Create the workflow with a shared or service account: a workflow belongs to whoever made it and stops when they leave. Your tenant must allow the Workflows app.
  4. forgeqe notify test [channel] sends a test card to check it arrives.
Operations

CI pipelines

Writes a GitHub Actions or Azure Pipelines workflow that runs ForgeQE, stores the secrets it needs, pushes a branch, and makes the pipeline green.

Ask it
  • Add a GitHub Actions workflow that runs tests/cases.yaml on every PR, and make it green
What it needs
  • Requiredgit on PATH
  • Requiredgh on PATH, for GitHub Actions; or az on PATH, for Azure Pipelines
Tools

push_branch set_ci_secret

In detail

Pipelines

ForgeQE's agent writes, runs, and fixes GitHub Actions and Azure Pipelines. Run it in the repo (or pass --repo) and ask:

bash
forgeqe run "Add a GitHub Actions workflow that runs the smoke cases in cases.yaml on every pull request"
forgeqe run "Set up an Azure DevOps pipeline that runs test plan 12 every night and posts results back"
forgeqe run "The ForgeQE workflow is failing, fix it"

What it does:

  1. Works out the platform from the git remote (github.com, or dev.azure.com / visualstudio.com) and reads the repo's existing CI, so it can add a job to it or write a new file.
  2. Writes the pipeline so it installs Bun and uv, runs npm install -g @capagents/forgeqe and forgeqe setup, then forgeqe suite <source...> --no-code --plain --out <dir>, and publishes junit.xml and the report folder. On GitHub the suite adds a results table to the job summary.
  3. Validates it (actionlint when installed, otherwise by parsing the YAML) and commits it.
  4. Pushes to a new branch named forgeqe/ci-<topic> with ForgeQE's push_branch tool. That tool refuses the default branch, main, and master, and never forces; git push itself is blocked for the agent. Merging stays with you.
  5. Stores the keys the pipeline needs (the model's key from llms.json, and ADO_PAT, JIRA_EMAIL and JIRA_API_TOKEN, or XRAY_CLIENT_ID and XRAY_CLIENT_SECRET) with ForgeQE's set_ci_secret tool. The agent only names the variable; ForgeQE passes the value from its own environment to gh secret set (on stdin) or az pipelines variable (through the environment), and never shows it. A variable that is not set in ForgeQE's environment is reported, not guessed.
  6. Triggers the run with gh workflow run or az pipelines run, watches it, reads the logs of failed steps, fixes the pipeline (or the code), pushes again, and repeats until the run passes or code.max_attempts runs have failed. It reports the run link and the result.

It needs gh logged in for GitHub, or az with the azure-devops extension logged in for Azure DevOps. Without them it still writes, validates, and commits the pipeline, then tells you the commands to run. Set llm.active to a live profile in the committed forgeqe.yaml. With a github_copilot profile the pipeline needs a GitHub token for Copilot in COPILOT_GITHUB_TOKEN (see In pipelines).

10

Data

Databases: their schema, and SQL from plain language, checked before it runs

Data

Ask a database in plain language

Connects to the project's databases (PostgreSQL, MySQL and MariaDB, SQL Server and Azure SQL, Oracle, Snowflake, Redshift, CockroachDB, SQLite, DuckDB, and MongoDB) from a connection string or host and user, signing in with a password, Microsoft Entra ID or AWS IAM. Reads the schema through DB-GPT's connectors and turns questions into SQL that is checked before it runs: reads in a read-only session that is rolled back, writes only on a database marked writable, after you confirm them.

Ask it
  • Which customers in India have paid orders? Ask the orders database
  • Show me the tables in the shop database
  • Write the SQL for revenue per country last month and run it
  • Check the connection to the reporting database
What it needs
  • RequiredA database under databases: in forgeqe.yaml or env.yaml: a connection string in an environment variable (url_env), or host, database, user and password_env; auth: azure or aws_iam for token sign-in
Limits
  • One statement at a time: SELECT (with CTEs and set operations), SHOW, DESCRIBE and EXPLAIN SELECT anywhere; INSERT, and UPDATE or DELETE with a WHERE clause, only on databases marked writable and after you confirm
  • Never DDL (CREATE, ALTER, DROP, TRUNCATE), procedures, or functions that read files, sleep or reach other servers
  • MongoDB is queried with find, aggregate, count and distinct (JSON operations); its test data is not made yet; other non-SQL stores are not supported
Tools

databases database_schema run_sql

In detail

Databases

ForgeQE's agent can read your databases and answer questions from them in plain language: "which customers in India have paid orders?", "show me the tables in the shop database", "write the SQL for revenue per country and run it". It reads the schema first (through DB-GPT's connectors), writes SQL in the database's own dialect, runs it, and shows you the rows and the SQL.

Supported: PostgreSQL, MySQL, MariaDB, SQL Server and Azure SQL, Oracle, Snowflake, Redshift, CockroachDB, SQLite, DuckDB, and MongoDB. The first use sets up the data engine (its own Python, in ~/.forgeqe/data) and that database's driver.

MongoDB (engine: mongodb, or url_env with a mongodb:// or mongodb+srv:// string): the agent lists the collections with the fields of a sample of documents, and queries with find, aggregate, count and distinct, written as JSON. Operators that run code on the server or write elsewhere ($where, $function, $accumulator, $out, $merge) and system.* collections are refused; inserts, updates and deletes need writable: true and your confirmation, and an update or delete needs a filter. MongoDB has no read-only session, so connect as a read-only user. Test data is made for the SQL databases only.

Add the databases under databases: in forgeqe.yaml, or under an environment in env.yaml (the active environment's entries win by name). No secret goes in either file: name the environment variables that hold them.

yaml
databases:
  orders:                        # a whole connection string in a variable: URI, JDBC, or ADO.NET as Azure shows it
    url_env: ORDERS_DB_URL
    description: Orders written by the checkout app
  reporting:                     # or the parts
    engine: postgres             # postgres, mysql, mariadb, mssql, oracle, snowflake, redshift, cockroachdb, sqlite, duckdb
    host: reporting.postgres.database.azure.com
    database: reporting
    user: qa_reader
    auth: azure                  # a Microsoft Entra ID token (az login, managed identity, service principal)
  billing:
    engine: mysql
    host: billing.abc123.eu-west-1.rds.amazonaws.com
    database: billing
    user: qa_iam
    auth: aws_iam                # an RDS IAM token from your AWS profile; the region is read from the host
  fixtures:
    engine: postgres
    host: localhost
    port: 5432
    database: test
    user: qa
    password_env: FIXTURES_DB_PASSWORD
    writable: true               # allows confirmed INSERT, and UPDATE or DELETE with a WHERE clause

Save a secret once with /auth ORDERS_DB_URL in the shell (or set it in the environment).

In the shell, /db lists the databases, /db test <name> connects, /db tables <name> [words] shows the tables (or those whose names, comments or columns hold the words), /db refresh <name> reads the schema again, /db batches shows test data batches, and /db examples <name> the remembered queries. /db add sets one up with the agent: it asks how to connect, writes the entry, and has you save the secret with /auth, so no password goes through the chat. The same commands work outside the shell (forgeqe db test orders), and for scripts:

bash
forgeqe db query orders "SELECT status, COUNT(*) FROM orders GROUP BY status" --json   # one checked read
forgeqe db query orders "SELECT ..." --dry-run          # the database checks every name; no rows are read
forgeqe db query orders "SELECT * FROM orders" --max-rows 1000  # rows to read (default 200, at most 5000)
forgeqe db ask "paid revenue per country last month" --db orders --save queries/revenue.sql
                                                        # the agent writes and validates it; stdout is only the SQL

Drafts, files and examples. Ask for a query without running it ("write the SQL for ...") and the agent validates it with a dry run: the database resolves every table and column (and shows its plan where it can) without reading or writing anything. Ask it to keep a query and it saves a .sql file in the project with the question as a comment. When you confirm a result is right, the agent remembers the question and its SQL for that database (.forgeqe/data/<name>/examples.jsonl) and follows it for similar questions: your names for things and your filters.

Other settings: schema (default: the user's), port, path (sqlite, duckdb), timeout_seconds (default 30), ssl (verify, the default for Azure, AWS and GCP hosts; require; disable), ssl_ca (a CA file), region (aws_iam), sample_rows (0 to 5 sample rows per table shown to the model; default 0), mask (more column name patterns to hide), and options (warehouse and role for Snowflake; odbc_driver for SQL Server with auth: azure, which needs Microsoft's ODBC Driver 18).

What ForgeQE does with them:

  • Reads run in a read-only session where the database has one (PostgreSQL, MySQL, Oracle, SQLite, DuckDB), with a statement timeout and a row cap (200 by default), and are rolled back. Use a read-only login all the same; SQL Server and Snowflake have no read-only session, so there the login is the limit.
  • Every statement is checked first. One statement only: SELECT (with CTEs, joins and set operations), SHOW, DESCRIBE, or EXPLAIN of a SELECT. Refused: DDL, procedures, SELECT INTO, FOR UPDATE, MySQL's executable comments, and functions that read files, sleep, or reach other sessions or servers (pg_read_file, LOAD_FILE, xp_cmdshell, OPENROWSET, UTL_HTTP, ...).
  • Writes run only on a database marked writable: true, and only after you confirm the exact statement: one INSERT, or one UPDATE or DELETE with a WHERE clause.
  • Masked values: columns named like passwords, tokens, hashes, card numbers, IBANs and tax ids show as •••.
  • What the model sees: table and column names, types, keys and comments, and the rows of the queries it runs. Passwords and tokens never reach it, its shell, or the tests. ForgeQE's agent cannot run database clients (psql, mysql, sqlcmd, sqlplus, ...) in its shell.
  • The schema is read once and kept in .forgeqe/data/<name>/catalog.json. ForgeQE checks a cheap fingerprint of the schema (its tables, columns and types) every few minutes and reads it again when a migration changed it, or after a week; /db refresh <name> reads it at once. For a large database the agent gets the tables a question names and the tables that join them.
  • vector: true adds DB-GPT's schema index: a summary of every table, embedded by a small model that runs on this machine, which can bring in tables a question describes without naming. Its hits add to the word matching rather than replace it (on a 938-table ERP the words alone found the right tables more often). It installs about 400 MB (Chroma and the ONNX runtime) and downloads an 80 MB model once; building the index of a large schema takes a few minutes, once per schema change.
Data

Test data on demand

Makes realistic test data from a spec that fits the database's schema (types, sizes, NOT NULL, unique keys, CHECK lists, enums), parents before children, the same rows for the same seed. Or takes a masked subset of a real database: the rows you pick and every row they need, with names, emails, phones, addresses and other personal columns replaced the same way every time. Shows a preview, writes CSV, JSON or SQL files for automation, or inserts the rows into a database marked writable after you confirm, as a batch that can be removed again. Specs live in the project, so tests and pipelines make the same data with forgeqe data gen.

Ask it
  • Generate test data: 20 customers with 1 to 3 orders each in the shop database
  • Make test data for the orders tables as CSV files for our automation
  • Insert 50 test users into the fixtures database
  • Copy 100 paid orders from prod into fixtures as a masked subset
  • Remove the test data batch we inserted yesterday
What it needs
  • RequiredA database under databases: in forgeqe.yaml or env.yaml: a connection string in an environment variable (url_env), or host, database, user and password_env; auth: azure or aws_iam for token sign-in
Limits
  • Inserts only into databases marked writable, after you confirm; removes only rows of a batch it inserted
  • A subset reads its source in read-only sessions; its start condition is a filter of one table, and personal columns are found by name (mask: adds others)
  • Simple CHECK constraints (a list of values, bounds) are read; other rules the database enforces may refuse rows, and then nothing is kept
Tools

generate_test_data test_data_batches

In detail

Test data

ForgeQE makes test data that fits your schema: ask "generate 20 customers with 1 to 3 orders each in the shop database", "make the orders tables as CSV for our automation", or "insert 50 test users into fixtures". The agent reads the schema, writes a spec, shows you a preview, and then writes files or inserts the rows.

A spec is YAML you can keep in the project, so tests and pipelines make the same data again:

yaml
database: fixtures          # optional: else --db, or the only database
seed: 42                    # the same seed makes the same rows
locale: en_IN               # Faker's locale for names, addresses, phone numbers
edge_cases: true            # a few rows per table at the limits: longest text, unicode, nulls where allowed
tables:
  customers:
    rows: 20
    columns:
      email: {faker: email, unique: true}
      country: {one_of: [IN, UK, US], weights: [5, 3, 2]}
  orders:
    per: {customers: [1, 3]}          # each customer gets 1 to 3 orders
    columns:
      status: {one_of: [NEW, PAID, SHIPPED]}
      total: {range: [5, 500], decimals: 2}
      placed_at: {date_between: ["-90d", "today"]}
      reference: {sequence: "ORD-{n:05d}"}
  order_items:
    per: {orders: [1, 4]}
    columns:
      product_id: {ref: products.id}  # rows already in the database

Rules: a plain value, faker (a Faker method, with args), one_of (with weights), range (with decimals), date_between (dates, today, or -30d, -6m, +1y), sequence ({n} counts from 1), bothify (# a digit, ? a letter), ref (table.column), null_ratio, and unique. Two rules keep a row consistent: {ref: products.price, via: product_id} takes the price of the very product the row points at, and {template: "{first_name}.{last_name}@shop.test"} builds a value from the row's other columns. Columns without a rule are filled from the schema: their type and size, NOT NULL, unique keys, a CHECK constraint's list of values or bounds, enum types, and their name (email, phone, city, created_at, ...). Keys the database makes (serial, identity, auto-increment) are left to it, and foreign keys point at rows the spec makes or rows already in the database. A schema without declared foreign keys is matched by name (orders.customer_id to customers).

From the command line, for automation and pipelines (no model):

bash
forgeqe data gen test-data/shop.yaml                          # what it makes
forgeqe data gen test-data/shop.yaml --format csv --out test-data/shop   # csv, json or sql (INSERTs in the database's dialect)
forgeqe data gen test-data/shop.yaml --insert --yes --json    # into a writable database; prints the batch id
forgeqe data batches --db fixtures
forgeqe data clean 20261019-173810-9f5b5f --db fixtures --yes # removes exactly that batch, children first
  • Inserts go only into a database marked writable: true, in one transaction: if the database refuses a row, none are kept. In the shell, ForgeQE asks first; on the command line, --yes is needed.
  • Batches: every insert records the keys it made in .forgeqe/data/<database>/batches/, so clean removes those rows and nothing else.
  • Files are written inside the project only, never to a hidden folder.

Test data in test cases and suites. A test case in a YAML or JSON case file can bring its own data: the rows go in before the case runs and come out after it (and after the test ForgeQE writes for it), and the case's text uses their values:

yaml
- id: CHECKOUT-7
  title: A returning customer pays by card
  data: test-data/checkout.yaml          # or {spec: ..., database: fixtures, keep: true}
  steps:
    - action: Log in as {{data.customers.0.email}}
    - action: Open order {{data.orders.0.reference}} and pay it
      expected: The order page shows Paid for {{data.customers.0.name}}

{{data.<table>.<row>.<column>}} takes a value of the rows as inserted, keys the database made included ({{data.customers.email}} is row 0). A whole suite can have data too: forgeqe suite cases.yaml --data test-data/shop.yaml (--data-db NAME, --keep-data to leave it for a look), or data_spec when the agent runs cases. The data goes only into a database marked writable; these hooks do not ask first, because the team wrote them.

In automation scripts. The tests ForgeQE runs, and the tests it writes, read the inserted rows from the JSON file FORGEQE_DATA_FILE names. In a pipeline, make that file with the same spec:

bash
forgeqe data gen test-data/checkout.yaml --insert --yes --json --export test-data/.run/checkout.json > batch.json
FORGEQE_DATA_FILE=test-data/.run/checkout.json npx playwright test
forgeqe data clean "$(jq -r .batch batch.json)" --db fixtures --yes      # in an always() step

Scribe, ForgeQE's coding agent, writes tests that read their data from that file (its test-data skill), and can look at the databases and preview test data through ForgeQE's data tools; it never changes a database itself.

A masked subset of real data. Instead of tables:, a spec can take real rows from another database: the rows you pick, every row they point at (and the rows those point at), and the child tables you name, with personal data replaced:

yaml
database: fixtures                # where the rows go (or --db); leave it out for files
seed: 7                           # the same seed masks the same way
subset:
  from: prod_copy                 # a databases: entry, read in read-only sessions
  start: {table: orders, where: "status = 'PAID' AND created_at >= '2026-01-01'", limit: 100}
  children: [order_items, payments]
mask:                             # more columns to mask: a Faker method, or null to empty them
  customers.notes: paragraph
keep: [customers.country]         # columns whose names look personal but are not
  • Masked by name: names (in tables of people: customers, users, employees, contacts, ...), emails, phones, addresses, post codes, cities, birth dates, national and tax ids, card numbers, IBANs, IP addresses, and passwords, hashes and tokens. Keys are never masked.
  • The same everywhere: a value always becomes the same fake value, in every table and every run, so joins on a masked column still match; a masked value in a unique column stays unique.
  • The start condition is a filter of the start table only: one condition, no UNION, and only tables of the source's schema. Rows are taken in key order, so the same spec takes the same rows.
  • Into a database, keys the target makes are new and every reference follows them; a subset never goes back into its source. As files, keys stay as they were.
Data

Data flows between systems

Checks the data that passes between your systems' databases (the digital channel, CRM, billing, a warehouse), by batch ETL or in real time: each hop's rows arrived, complete, with every mapped column right after its rule, on time for its SLA, without duplicates. Follows one record end to end, says what a change to a table or column reaches downstream, and writes the flows from your mapping documents wherever they are, or suggests them from the schemas.

Ask it
  • Check that customers from the digital database reach the CRM correctly
  • Trace customer 42 from digital through CRM to billing
  • What does a change to digital.customers.email affect downstream?
  • Write the data flows from mapping.xlsx
  • Reconcile yesterday's orders between the ERP and the warehouse
What it needs
  • RequiredA database under databases: in forgeqe.yaml or env.yaml: a connection string in an environment variable (url_env), or host, database, user and password_env; auth: azure or aws_iam for token sign-in
Limits
  • Hops between SQL tables, Kafka topics, and APIs (a target by key, a source by its list; GET or POST only); MongoDB is not checked
  • At most 200,000 source rows a flow per check, compared by key
  • Reads only; flows are written into flows.yaml, and nothing in the databases changes
Tools

data_flows check_flows trace_record read_mapping_file

In detail

Data flows

When your systems pass data to each other (a customer signs up in the digital channel's database, the CRM gets a contact, billing gets an account), ForgeQE checks each hop: ask "check that customers reach the CRM correctly", "trace customer 42 from digital to billing", or "what does a change to digital.customers.email affect downstream?".

The hops are in flows.yaml, beside forgeqe.yaml. ForgeQE writes it from your mapping documents, wherever they are (a spreadsheet in the project, a Confluence page, a Jira or Azure DevOps item, a text file), or suggests flows from the schemas and a sample of keys when there are none; it shows you each flow and saves the ones you confirm. You can write it by hand too:

yaml
flows:
  signup_to_crm:
    from: {db: digital, table: customers, key: id, changed: updated_at}
    to: {db: crm, table: contacts, key: source_customer_id}     # the target column that holds the source key
    mode: realtime          # it moves as it changes (API, queue, CDC)
    sla: 15m                # a source row changed longer ago than this must have arrived
    extra: true             # also report target rows with no source row
    columns:
      email: {to: email_address, rule: lower}
      full_name: {to: full_name, template: "{first_name} {last_name}"}
      country: {to: country_code, map: {India: IN, United Kingdom: GB}}
  orders_to_crm:
    from: {db: digital, table: orders, key: id}
    to: {db: crm, table: crm_orders, key: source_order_id}
    mode: batch             # an ETL job copies it
    ready: {sql: "SELECT MAX(finished_at) FROM etl_runs WHERE job = 'orders'", wait: 10m}   # wait for a run to finish
    since: 7d               # only rows changed in the last week (needs from.changed)
    columns:
      total: {to: amount, rule: "round(2)"}
      status: {to: status_code, map: {PAID: P, NEW: N}}

Rules: same (the default), lower, upper, trim, round(n) (half up, as databases round), date, number, string, map, template (from the source row's columns), and default (for a null).

Kafka and APIs. A hop's side can also be a Kafka topic or an API:

yaml
databases:
  events:
    engine: kafka
    host: broker1:9092,broker2:9092
    user: qa-reader                     # SASL; leave out for a broker without sign-in
    password_env: KAFKA_PASSWORD
    options: {security_protocol: SASL_SSL, sasl_mechanism: SCRAM-SHA-512}
yaml
flows:
  signup_events:
    from: {db: digital, table: customers, key: id, changed: updated_at}
    to: {db: events, topic: customer-created, key: customerId}   # a field of the message; $key for the message key
    mode: realtime
    sla: 5m
    columns:
      email: {to: email, rule: lower}
      country: {to: address.country, map: {India: IN}}             # nested fields by their dotted names
  contacts_api:
    from: {db: crm, table: contacts, key: contact_id}
    to: {api: crm_api, path: "/contacts/{key}", key: id}           # an API app of the environment (kind: api)
    columns:
      email_address: email
  web_signups:
    from: {api: web_api, path: "/signups?status=new", key: id, items: data.items, next: links.next, changed: createdAt}
    to: {db: crm, table: contacts, key: contact_id}
    columns:
      email: email_address
  contacts_graphql:
    from: {db: digital, table: customers, key: id}
    to:
      api: crm_api
      path: /graphql
      method: POST                                                 # a lookup or a search: GET or POST only
      body: {query: "query($id: ID!) { contact(id: $id) { id email } }", variables: {id: "{key}"}}
      items: data.contact
      key: id
    columns:
      email: email
  • Topics are read without a consumer group, so no offset is committed and no other consumer is affected; a read stops at the end of the topic as it was when the check began. A topic can be a source (its message time is when a record changed) or a target. /db test events checks the brokers, and /db tables events lists the topics with the fields of their latest messages.
  • APIs are called at the base URL of the environment's API app, with the headers api.headers gives in forgeqe.yaml (Authorization: Bearer ${CRM_API_TOKEN}). As a target, an API answers for one key: {key} goes in its path (/contacts/{key}) or its body, one call a key; a 404 counts as missing; at most 500 keys a check, so narrow a large source with since:. As a source, it is a list endpoint (no {key}), read page by page through the response's next link; changed names the field that says when a record changed, for since: and SLAs. A record can be followed (flows trace, trace:) from an API list too.
  • items says where the records are in a response (data.items); without it, ForgeQE takes the response itself, or its first list among items, data, results, value, records and content.
  • Calls are GET or POST (a lookup, a search, a GraphQL query): a check never changes data, so PUT, PATCH and DELETE are refused. Redirects are not followed, and a next link that leaves the API's host is refused, so the headers never reach another server.

What a check reports for each flow:

  • Missing: source rows whose key is not in the target. For a realtime flow, a row is late once its change is older than the SLA, and still in flight before that (not a failure).
  • Wrong: rows where a mapped column does not hold the expected value, with the key, the source value, the value expected after the rule, and the value found. Numbers compare as numbers (12.5 and 12.50), date-times to the second, and trailing spaces of fixed-width text do not count.
  • Duplicated: target keys that appear more than once; with extra: true, target rows with no source row.
  • Batch: with ready, the check waits for a run of the job that finished after it began (up to wait), and says so when it had to check the last run's rows.

Following what a test case created. A case in a case file can follow the record its steps made through the flows; the case passes only when the record arrived, right, at every hop:

yaml
- id: SIGNUP-3
  title: A new customer reaches the CRM and billing
  steps:
    - action: Sign up with the email qa+{{unique}}@shop.test
      expected: The welcome page shows
  trace:
    from: digital.customers              # or flows: [signup_to_crm, crm_to_billing]
    where: {email: "qa+{{unique}}@shop.test"}
    wait: 120                            # seconds each hop may take

{{unique}} is a token made for each run of the case, so what it creates can be found again; data placeholders ({{data.customers.0.email}}) work in where too. A record that does not arrive, arrives twice, or arrives with a wrong value fails the case as a functional defect, with the hop and the values in its summary, the report, JUnit, and the result posted to Azure DevOps or Jira.

From the command line, for pipelines (no model):

bash
forgeqe flows map                                      # what feeds what
forgeqe flows check --junit flows.xml                  # exit 1 when a flow fails
forgeqe flows check --post-ado --run-name "Nightly data flows"   # also an Azure DevOps test run
forgeqe flows trace digital.customers 42 --wait 120    # follow customer 42 hop by hop
forgeqe flows impact digital.customers.email           # every column it becomes downstream

Each check is also kept in the reports folder, as flows-<time>/report.html and flows.json. The report opens on a map of your systems, left to right in the order data reaches them, with each hop coloured by health; a hop opens what is missing, late, wrong or duplicated, key by key. A record you follow (flows trace) is kept the same way, hop by hop. With --post-ado (or when you ask the agent to record the results), the check becomes an automated Azure DevOps test run with one result per flow, failed with what is missing, late, wrong or duplicated; a flow with ado_test_case: <id> records its result against that test case.

Everything is a read in a read-only session; keys go to each database as bound values. Masked columns (passwords, tokens and the like) show as ••• in results. A check compares up to 200,000 source rows a flow, by key.

11

Automated tests

Write, heal, find, run, and stabilise tests in your repository

Automated tests

Turn a passed run into an automated test

Scribe, the coding agent, writes a test for a passed browser run in the repository's own framework, page objects, and helpers; ForgeQE runs it until it passes and a reviewer approves it.

Ask it
  • Write a Playwright test for that run in ../app
What it needs
  • RequiredThe coding agent, Scribe: a live model profile (or code.model) and the agent runtime, which forgeqe setup installs
  • You givea repository to write the test in (default: the working directory)
Tools

write_test run_test

In detail

Tests

After a run passes, Scribe, ForgeQE's coding agent, turns it into a test in your repo.

In the shell and forgeqe run, every passed run then becomes a test, and so does each test case that passes when you ask the shell to run test cases: in the current folder, or in the repo you give per run or in forgeqe.yaml. --no-code turns it off; you can still ask for a test ("write a Playwright test for that run").

bash
forgeqe run "Log in and open the invoices page on staging.app.test" --repo ../app
yaml
code:
  repo: ../app              # relative to forgeqe.yaml
  max_attempts: 5           # test runs before giving up
  test_timeout_seconds: 600 # per test run
  stall_after: 5            # stop the agent's turn when one command it runs has failed this many times
  replay: true              # before the agent starts, run the steps as a Playwright script and hand over what worked
  live_browser: true        # once a step fails, the agent looks at that page in a Playwright browser
  graph: true               # the code graph: find page objects without reading files, and reuse what already locates an element
  # model: anthropic/claude-sonnet-4-5
  # runtime: /path/to/agent-runtime   # default: the runtime ForgeQE downloads

What happens after the run passes:

  1. ForgeQE writes handoff.md into the run directory: the goal and result, how ForgeQE's browser was set up (browser and version, headless or headed, user agent, navigator.webdriver, launch switches, extra headers, language, time zone, viewport), every step in order with the value typed (secrets as process.env.NAME) and the JavaScript the browser agent ran, the browser agent's own note before each group of steps (what it saw and why it acted, so a sign-in pop-up it closed or steps it redid after a banner covered the page read as such), each element's best locator and the others with their confidence %, and a short table of each visited page's other elements for assertions (weak locators under 30% are left out, and elements the steps already use are listed with the steps, so the handoff stays small; the full lists are in the page-memory files it points to). The agent's result and the final URL are marked as coming from one run on live data, to be checked as rules (the results load, the order holds, the fields have the right format), not copied as expected values. Pop-ups and banners the run met are handled wherever they appear (Playwright's locator handlers or the repository's helper), since the next run can show them at another moment or not at all; the agent's retries are not copied. A site can also serve different versions of a page to different visits (A/B tests, such as a classic search box or a free-text one), so the test handles every version the run or the agent saw, or waits for one control from either version.

    The handoff also lists the browser agent's checks: before it finishes, the browser agent records each result the goal asks to verify with the exact text the page showed (record_check; a text the page does not show is refused). They are saved in workflow.yaml under checks:. The test asserts these and nothing else.

  2. ForgeQE replays the run's steps as a Playwright script (<run>/replay/flow.mjs), in the run's browser setup (channel, headless or headed, user agent, language, time zone, viewport), with 10 seconds per step. Text is typed key by key, as the browser agent typed it, so search suggestions and other typing handlers fire. If the replay does not reach the run's final page, it runs again without Playwright's automation switches (navigator.webdriver false, as in ForgeQE's browser). The handoff gets the result: which steps worked, and for each failed step, Playwright's reason (for example a dialog that intercepts the click), the page, the dialogs open on it, how many elements each of the element's other locators matched, and a screenshot and page snapshot. It also saves Playwright's page snapshot of each page the replay visited (roles, accessible names, and text, as the replay left the page; the last page once it stops changing), so Scribe takes locators for its steps and assertions from real pages instead of guessing them. On the last page it looks up each of the browser agent's checks and puts the Playwright locator that matched its text into the handoff (a heading or text match, the container it sits in when there are several, or the form field that holds it), so assertions come with a locator that worked. For a heading it also gives the part of the page the heading names (an element labelled by it, the section it heads, or the element right after it), with its locator and how many links and list items it holds, so a check such as "an In the news section with at least one item" is asserted inside that part, not on the heading alone. A step that fails while every step after it still works and the replay reaches the final page (a click that did nothing on the run, or a control this version of the page doesn't have) doesn't count as a failed replay: Scribe decides from the step whether the goal needs it and ports the rest as it is. When only the second launch got through, the handoff says the browser setup is the difference and which launch settings fix it. The first time, Playwright (about 13 MB) is downloaded into ~/.forgeqe/tools; it drives the installed Chrome. A run that only opened a page still replays when it recorded checks, to look them up; with neither it skips this, and replay: false turns it off.

    Scribe first reads the repo (as a read-only agent: it cannot change files or use the live browser yet), while the replay runs when there is one, so the two overlap. What it learns (the framework, the command that runs one test, where page objects and config live, the conventions) is saved with page memory in scribe-notes.json, with the tests each job writes there. The next job in the same repo starts from those notes and that list instead of reading the repo again, and extends those tests' page objects when its flow overlaps; notes older than 30 days are read again.

  3. Scribe starts in the repo. It reads AGENTS.md, the test framework config, run scripts, page objects, fixtures, and helpers, and follows the imports between them. It writes the test in the repo's language and framework (a C# NUnit repo gets a C# NUnit test) and to fit its code: it extends an existing page object instead of duplicating it and reuses the login flow, test data, and config. A repo with no tests gets Playwright Test in TypeScript. It may change anything the test needs, including dependencies and config.

    Before Scribe starts, ForgeQE reads the repo into a code graph, without a model: every class, page object, method, property, and test (TypeScript, JavaScript, C#, Java, Python), the calls between them, and the locators each one builds, in any framework's spelling (Playwright, Selenium, Cypress, WebdriverIO, Testing Library). It is kept in ~/.forgeqe/cache/code-graph, nothing leaves the machine, and the next job reads again only the files that changed. The graph is tied to page memory, which knows each element by all its locators (its id, role and name, text, test id): so when the run clicked getByRole('button', { name: 'Login' }) and the repo's LoginPage.LoginButton uses #login-button, the handoff says under that step "in this repository: LoginPage.LoginButton … used by LoginPage.SignInAsync", and Scribe calls the existing method instead of writing a second locator. Scribe also gets tools that answer from the graph instead of grep and whole-file reads: code_find (classes, methods, and tests by name, or by the text a locator uses), code_outline (a file's or class's signatures and locators, or the source of one member), code_element (which member already locates an element), code_references (what calls a member, before it is changed), and code_impact (the tests a change reaches, through flows and base classes, whatever the test files are called). graph: false turns it off. The handoff also outlines the page objects its steps use and includes the repo's test that uses the most of them, so Scribe takes the test conventions from that test instead of reading other tests, and reads a settings file only to change it.

  4. It writes the test from the handoff and the replay script: steps that worked are ported with their locators, steps that failed are fixed first. Every locator, including those for assertions, comes from the replay script, the page snapshots, page memory, or a check on the page; steps where the browser agent ran JavaScript to look around are left out. It does not walk the flow again or build tests to explore. Once a step fails (in the replay, or in its test), ForgeQE switches on its live browser: a Chrome that Playwright drives, set up like ForgeQE's, where it looks at the failing step's page one tool call per action (open, snapshot, click, type, wait, screenshot, run JavaScript, console errors, failed requests). Until then the live browser tools are off, so no tokens go into looking at pages that work. The live browser needs Node.js (npx), and Microsoft's Playwright MCP server is downloaded on first use; with live_browser: false, or without npx, it looks with a small throwaway script instead. When its browser gets a different page than ForgeQE's did (an error, a challenge, a control that will not open), it compares the two browsers (user agent, navigator.webdriver, headless, failed requests and CORS errors, headers the framework adds, console errors) and fixes the difference in the repo's browser configuration. It never judges the site with curl or webfetch.

  5. Scribe does not run the test itself: it may build or compile, then reports the test file and the command that runs only that test, and ForgeQE runs it. If one command it runs fails stall_after times in a row within its work (the same build again and again, say), ForgeQE stops that turn and sends the command and its last output back, asking it to change approach. If the same command stalls again, the job stops as a failure with that output in the notes.

  6. ForgeQE runs that command (with CI=1) and checks the exit code, and it watches the processes the command starts for a browser launched by Playwright, Puppeteer, Selenium, or Cypress. On a failure it sends the command, exit code, and output back to Scribe. The output is kept short so the error is never cut off: the page dump Playwright puts into a failed assertion ("Aria snapshot:", often thousands of lines) is taken out and saved as <run>/test-runs/attempt-N-page.aria.yml, then the start (where the first error and its stack trace are) and the end (the summary) are sent; the full output is in <run>/test-runs/attempt-N.log, and the prompt names both files. A command that exits 0 without starting a browser also goes back: the test must drive the app in a browser. If the repo's browser runs on another machine (Selenium Grid, a cloud service, a container), the agent says where and the check is skipped.

  7. A passing test then goes to a separate reviewer agent, which can read files and git diff but not change anything. It checks the change against the goal and the test rules: every part of the goal done through the page and asserted, generic page objects with data in the test, no values or URLs copied from the run, browser settings in the repo's configuration, no leftover files. The changed files come inline in its prompt, so it reads nothing again, and it does not ask to replace locators or texts that came from the replay or the checks, since those matched the live site. It judges only what this job changed: ForgeQE records the working tree before the job, so your uncommitted edits, your untracked files, and ForgeQE's own files (.forgeqe/, forgeqe.yaml, llms.json) are never part of the review. It checks the diff of every file that existed before the job: removing or rewriting an existing test, even a commented-out one, is a correctness problem. Credentials come from the environment, except a public demo login that the application itself shows to anyone (such as the logins on saucedemo.com's sign-in page), which may stay in the repo's test data. It gets the outlines of the classes the changed code uses, the repo's browser settings, and the same code_* tools, so it checks reuse and settings without reading those files. The code graph also tells it what the job added that it can check in the code: a new member that locates an element an existing member already locates (to reuse instead), and new XPath or position-based locators. If it finds problems, they go back to Scribe as the next attempt. The terminal shows the exchange as it happens, in order: the reviewer's verdict with its reason and each issue (✗ correctness, ~ coding standard) with the file and the fix it asks for; then why Scribe tries again and what it is fixing (the reviewer's issues, or the test's first error); then what Scribe says it did and which files it changed; then the next verdict:

text
⏺ Reviewer asked for changes  attempt 1 of 5 · 1 to fix, 1 coding-standard
  ⎿ The test never checks that the pie chart is shown.
    ✗ Correctness · tests/PracticeTests.cs: No assertion on the SVG pie chart.
        Fix: Assert the chart's heading and SVG are visible.
    ~ Coding standard · tests/PracticeTests.cs: Test data is inline.
        Fix: Move it to the page object.
⏺ Scribe tries again · attempt 2 of 5  the reviewer asked for changes
  ⎿ → tests/PracticeTests.cs: No assertion on the SVG pie chart. — fix: Assert the chart's heading and SVG are visible.
  ⎿ Scribe (attempt 2): Added the pie chart assertion · changed tests/PracticeTests.cs, Pages/PracticePage.cs
⏺ Reviewer approved the test  attempt 2 of 5

The Activities panel has one line for each verdict and attempt, and forgeqe commands outside the terminal UI print the same blocks.

Scribe cannot edit ForgeQE's files or run folders, and cannot run git clean, git reset --hard, git stash, git restore, or git checkout -- <path>. If it still removes a file that existed before the job, or reverts your uncommitted edits, ForgeQE puts the file back after that attempt and tells the agent. 8. The job is done when ForgeQE's run passes with a browser, the reviewer approves, and the test passes again unchanged until it has passed code.confirm_runs times in a row (default 3; 1 runs it once). A test that passes and then fails is flaky: the next attempt is told which run failed and why, and to make that step wait for the page. It stops as a failure after max_attempts attempts, when the job passes code.max_tokens, when the reviewer rejects a passing test twice, if the review cannot finish, when the test fails the same way three times in a row (the same output apart from numbers such as timings and line numbers; the second time, Scribe is told its change did not change the failure and to look at the failing step before changing code), or straight away on an auth, API, or context-limit error from the model.

blocked is a last resort. The first time the agent reports that the app blocks it, ForgeQE sends it back to compare its browser with ForgeQE's and fix the difference; only a second blocked report, with what it compared, ends the job as blocked.

The transcript shows the handover and Scribe's work as it goes; files it writes or edits are marked ✎:

text
┃
┃  ▸ Handing over to the coding agent                         browser agent → coding agent
┃
┃  Flow      Log in and open the invoices page on staging.app.test
┃  Run       .forgeqe/reports/20260928-172700/
┃  Repo      ../app
┃
  ▸ Handoff written: .forgeqe/reports/20260928-172700/handoff.md (7 steps, 3 pages from page memory)
  ▸ Scribe, the coding agent (azure/gpt-5.4), reads the handoff and writes a test in /work/app
    ⚙ Reading .forgeqe/reports/20260928-172700/handoff.md
    ⚙ Reading tests/pages/LoginPage.ts
    ✎ Editing tests/invoices.spec.ts
    ⚙ Running npx playwright test tests/invoices.spec.ts
  ▸ ForgeQE runs the test: npx playwright test tests/invoices.spec.ts
  ✗ tests/invoices.spec.ts failed, exit 1 (attempt 1/5)
  ▸ tests/invoices.spec.ts passed in 9s in Google Chrome; a reviewer checks it against the goal
  ✓ tests/invoices.spec.ts passed and was approved (attempt 2/5)
┃
┃  ✓ Test passed                          2/5 attempts · 3m 12s · 88.4K tokens · azure/gpt-5.4
┃
┃  Added openInvoices() to DashboardPage and reused the loginAs fixture.
┃
┃  Test      tests/invoices.spec.ts  (in ../app)
┃  Command   npx playwright test tests/invoices.spec.ts
┃  Reused    LoginPage, fixtures/auth.ts
┃  Changed   tests/invoices.spec.ts, tests/pages/DashboardPage.ts
┃  Browser   Google Chrome
┃  Review    approved: Opens the invoices page through the dashboard and checks the table columns.

After ForgeQE's reply, the request ends with the files it produced:

text
┃  ✓ What this produced
┃
┃    Report    .forgeqe/reports/20260928-172700/report.html
┃  ✓ Test      ../app/tests/invoices.spec.ts  passed when ForgeQE ran it
┃    Command   npx playwright test tests/invoices.spec.ts
┃    Handoff   .forgeqe/reports/20260928-172700/handoff.md

When no test was written, the Test row says why: the run did not pass, the session has --no-code, or the run was not handed to the coding agent.

Scribe uses the active llms.json profile, or, with llm.routing: auto, the profile the router picks for the coding area (--profile and code.model still win; see docs/MODEL_ROUTING.md). The key stays in your environment: the agent runtime's config refers to it as {env:WP_AZURE_API_KEY} and never contains the value. An auth: entra profile reaches the model through ForgeQE's local token gateway (see Signing in to the model without a key). An Azure profile goes through the agent runtime's azure provider on <endpoint>/openai/v1 (the Responses API, with the deployment as the model). openai_compatible and ollama profiles use their base_url. A github_copilot profile runs Scribe through ForgeQE's Copilot gateway (see GitHub Copilot seats as the model). code.model: github-copilot/... is refused: the agent runtime's own Copilot provider signs in through a third-party app on your GitHub account. code.model: provider/model (or --code-model) uses one of the agent runtime's built-in providers instead, with that provider's usual key variable (for example ANTHROPIC_API_KEY for anthropic/...).

Scribe may edit files and run commands anywhere in the repo without asking. git push in any form is denied (pushing goes through ForgeQE's guarded push_branch tool, section 8), and it cannot touch files outside the repo except the run and memory directories, which it may read.

More ways to run it:

bash
forgeqe code --repo ../app                                 # the latest run in .forgeqe/reports/
forgeqe code .forgeqe/reports/20260928-172700 --repo ../app --attempts 3
forgeqe run "..." --no-code                                # no tests for this request

In the shell, forgeqe start --repo ../app writes the tests in that repo instead of the current folder and --no-code turns them off; you can also just ask ("write a test for the last run in ../app"). Esc stops the agent the same way it stops a run.

Exit codes for forgeqe code and for forgeqe run --direct with a repo: 0 test passed, 1 run or test failed, 2 stopped. The result (status, attempts, test file, command, what was reused and changed, the browsers ForgeQE saw, the review, the last test output, tokens) is saved as code.json in the run directory, next to handoff.md.

If Scribe's job is cut off (ForgeQE closed, the machine restarted, you stopped it, or the model could not be reached), run it again for the same run and repo, for example forgeqe code .forgeqe/reports/20260928-172700 --repo ../app. Scribe reopens the same session, with everything it read and said, and carries on with the files it already wrote; it doesn't read the repo again, and ForgeQE uses the Playwright replay it already finished. The session is kept in scribe-session.json in the run folder until the job ends with a result.

How Scribe writes a test

Reuse before rebuild, extend before duplicate. Before Scribe writes anything, write_test measures what the repository's tests already cover of the run, from evidence rather than guesswork: each step and check the browser agent made on an element is matched, through page memory, to the page-object member that locates it, and the code graph says which tests reach that member (through the page methods they call, or a property they assert, such as Expect(practicePage.AjaxStatus)). Steps on elements no page object locates yet are listed as not measured, since a test may do them its own way. Then:

What it findsWhat happens
One test covers every measured step and checkReuse: Scribe only finds the command that runs it, and changes nothing. If it fails, nothing is changed: you are asked whether to update it or write a new test.
One test covers most (40% or more)Extend: Scribe adds only the missing steps and checks to that test, keeping every step and assertion it has; the reviewer rejects a removed or weakened assertion, or the steps copied into a new file
Two or more tests cover most of itYou choose: the evidence and the options (reuse, extend with how many steps it adds, a new test, cancel)
Nothing covers muchNew: a new test, reusing the page objects and helpers as before

A card says which it did and why (Extending PracticeTests.Flow · covers 12 of 15; only 3 are missing). Say how when you know ("extend the login test", "write a separate test", "update it") and ForgeQE's agent passes that on without asking. In every mode Scribe adds page-object members rather than changing what an existing member does for the other tests that use it.

The same flow asked again. When a session writes tests and you ask for a flow an earlier job already wrote a test for, ForgeQE runs that test first, before any browser run. If it passes in a real browser, that is the answer: no browser run, no Scribe, and no new test file, in the seconds the test takes (✓ Existing test passed · 19s · no browser run or new test needed). If it fails, the flow runs in the browser and Scribe updates that test instead of writing another. ForgeQE's agent rewords a goal a little each time, so goals count as the same flow when they share their content words (endings, case, and phrasing aside) and the new goal keeps everything the earlier one checked; the same steps with a different final check, a shorter flow, or another site are a new flow. The agent also compares the earlier goal with your request and runs fresh when you ask for a check the earlier test does not have. Ask for "a fresh run" or "a new test" to skip the reuse.

The run as a test would do it. The browser agent works through the same page a test meets, and the handoff tells Scribe everything it did on the way:

  • A click on an element something covers (a confirmation after Add to cart, a pop-up) fails and names what covers it, so the agent closes it as a user would and that step is in the flow; the step after says what covered it, and "What covered the page before a click" lists them. The engine library would otherwise click the element from JavaScript, which a test cannot do. What it still does from a script (a click, typing to the page) is named on its step.
  • Locators are the ones Playwright finds: no link role for an <a> without href (Chrome calls it a link, Playwright does not), icon-font characters kept out of names (a name with one becomes a role_pattern), and a control that repeats on every row or card gets a locator scoped to its row (#product-1 a.cart_quantity_delete).
  • An element in a frame says which frame, and the replay reaches it through frameLocator. Advertisements (Google's full-page ads, ad frames) are marked, left out of the replay, and "Ads on these pages" says to block the ad hosts in the test instead of closing them.
  • A suite case runs from the state its test starts from: without the saved logins' sessions, so the agent signs in as the test will (suite.restore_sessions: true keeps them; a login with no username and password to sign in with keeps its session, the only way in) and from the case's own start page (its URL, first step, description, or preconditions). When a run did start with saved sessions, "State the run started with" says so.

When the flow cannot be reproduced. If ForgeQE's own replay of the run could not reach the run's final page and Scribe's test fails too, Scribe is told where the replay stopped and what was open on the page, since that is usually a pop-up, consent banner or ad the browser agent closed, or state its browser already had (signed in, items in a cart), and gets another attempt to handle it. The job stops, saying which replayed step failed and why, only when the test then fails the same way again, instead of spending its remaining attempts on a flow no script can repeat as recorded.

Why a test failed. When ForgeQE's run of Scribe's test fails, the ✗ line and the next attempt say why in the test's own words: dotnet test's Error Message, pytest's E lines, or the error with its Expected and Received values (Playwright, Jest, JUnit), not the runner's summary line.

Keeping Scribe's cost down. ForgeQE reads the repository's test setup itself before Scribe starts (stack, settings files and their browser values, the test id attribute, base classes, page objects and their methods, one test command) and hands it over, so Scribe does not discover it with dozens of file reads. Each attempt after the first starts a fresh session from a summary of the job (handoff, files changed, what each attempt did, the latest failure), so later attempts cost about what the first did. The summary has a "Tried already, and did not work" list: for each failed attempt, what Scribe said it did (it reports each change in tried), how it failed, the locator the test was waiting for and the element that covered it and took the click, both read from the test's output, and whether an earlier attempt already failed on that locator. Scribe must not try those again in the same form and must say what it does differently. forgeqe heal keeps the same list across its attempts. code.max_attempts (default 3) and code.max_tokens (default 1,500,000; 0 turns it off) cap a job, and the token budget is checked during an attempt too, so one long attempt is stopped when the total passes it. Cached input counts at its share of the profile's input price (a session re-reads its own context, and that is most of its input), and in full when no cached price is known, such as with another code.model. The reviewer sorts its issues into correctness (the test does not test what the goal says, or not reliably) and coding standards (test data or credentials in a page object, naming, hard waits, leftovers). Coding standards never fail a test that works: it is accepted, and the coding agent gets one more round (not counted in max_attempts) to fix them; if that round breaks the test, ForgeQE puts back the version that passed and lists the standards issues in the notes (code.tidy: false skips the round). A test the reviewer rejects twice for correctness stops the job with its issues, since that usually means the test case and the page disagree. When a test passed and the reviewer sent it back, and the changes for the review then break it (or the attempts or the token budget run out first), ForgeQE puts back the version that passed: the result is passed but not approved, with the reviewer's issues in the notes, so you get a working test to finish instead of none. Under each step, the handoff shows that step's part of the page (its section and neighbours, from the replay's page snapshot) instead of whole snapshots. When a test fails, ForgeQE shows the failing element on the test's page at the failure and on the replay's page, so most failures are fixed without opening a browser. Scribe's live browser is switched on only when the same failure repeats, and it does not send a page snapshot after every click or keystroke. code.max_steps (default 50) caps the tool calls in one of Scribe's turns. code.review_profile runs the reviewer on a cheaper llms.json profile.

Scribe's rulebook: the same mistake once. When a job passes after a failed test run or a reviewer's change, Scribe's report gives up to three one-line rules that would have avoided the mistake ("Wait for the cart badge with toHaveText before clicking Checkout; it updates after a request"). ForgeQE keeps them in .forgeqe/scribe-rules.json in the repository, and every attempt of later jobs, and every healing session, starts with them. A lesson from a job that did not pass, or that passed at once, is not kept: only a fix that worked becomes a rule. The book stays small, since it is sent with each model call: at most 15 rules of one line each; a lesson learned again is merged into its rule (shown as "learned 3 times") instead of added; when it is full, the rule confirmed least and longest ago goes, and a rule not learned again for 180 days expires. The rules are the coding agent's own words, so a rule with a link, something like a token, a destructive command, or wording that tells an agent to drop its instructions is refused, and the agents are told they are hints that never override their instructions or the team's guardrails. Commit the file to share the rules with the team and see each change in review; edit or delete a rule there (the agents cannot change .forgeqe). ForgeQE says each new rule as it learns it (Learned for next time: …). code.rulebook: false turns it off.

See Generated code.

Automated tests

Heal failing tests

Runs a repository's tests and updates the ones the application has outgrown against the live app, or reports a real application bug instead.

Ask it
  • Heal the failing tests in ../app
What it needs
  • RequiredThe coding agent, Scribe: a live model profile (or code.model) and the agent runtime, which forgeqe setup installs
  • You givea repository with tests
Tools

heal_tests

In detail

Fixing broken tests

When a redesign or a renamed button breaks tests, forgeqe heal fixes them, or tells you the app is what broke:

bash
forgeqe heal --repo ../app                               # the command from code.test_command, or the agent finds it
forgeqe heal "npx playwright test tests/checkout" --repo ../app
forgeqe heal -- npx playwright test --project=chromium   # everything after -- is the command
yaml
code:
  repo: ../app
  test_command: npx playwright test   # optional
  1. ForgeQE runs the command. If it passes, there is nothing to heal and no model is called.
  2. Scribe starts in the repo as a healing agent, with the failing output. For each failing test it reads the test and the page objects it uses, then re-runs the test's flow in the real browser with ForgeQE's browser_run (the goal in plain words, not the old selectors).
  3. If the browser run passes, the test is out of date. The agent reads that run's steps and locators with run_details, updates the test (in the page object when the locators live there), and runs it with run_test.
  4. If the browser run fails at the step where the test fails, the app is broken there. The test is left as it is and the failure is reported as an app bug.
  5. It never deletes or skips a test, loosens an assertion, or raises a timeout to make a test pass.
  6. ForgeQE runs each healed test's own command, then the whole command. A healed test that still fails goes back to the agent with the output, and so does any other failure left in the whole run, until everything passes or code.max_attempts rounds are used.
text
  ▸ ForgeQE runs the tests: npx playwright test
  ✗ Tests failed, exit 1 (41s)
  ▸ Scribe (azure/gpt-5.4) is healing the failing tests in app
    ⚙ read tests/pages/LoginPage.ts
  ▸ Browser: Sign in to staging.app.test and open the invoices page
  ✓ Task complete · 9 steps
    ⚙ edit tests/pages/LoginPage.ts
  ▸ ForgeQE runs: npx playwright test tests/login.spec.ts
  ✓ login › signs in passed in 6s
  ▸ ForgeQE runs the tests: npx playwright test
  ✗ Tests still fail, exit 1 (39s)
┃
┃  ✗ Tests still failing                   2/5 attempts · 2m 40s · 61.2K tokens · azure/gpt-5.4
┃
┃  ✓ login › signs in  healed
┃      The Sign in button is now "Log in"; LoginPage uses the new label
┃  ! invoices › totals  app bug
┃      The total column is empty; the browser run failed at the same step
┃
┃  1 failure is in the app, not the tests; the tests were left as they are.
┃  Command   npx playwright test  (exit 1 after 39s)
┃  Changed   tests/pages/LoginPage.ts
┃  Report    .forgeqe/reports/heal/20260929-101500/heal.md

The report is heal.md and heal.json in .forgeqe/reports/heal/<time>/: each test's verdict and reason, the files changed, the browser runs (their folders sit next to the report), and the test output before and after. Exit codes: 0 the tests pass (or nothing needed healing), 1 some still fail, 2 stopped. In the shell or with forgeqe run, "the checkout tests are failing after the redesign, fix them" does the same through ForgeQE's agent.

Automated tests

Find and run existing tests

Finds the automated tests that match a feature, tags, ids, a folder, or a sprint, confirms the selection, and runs it once the user agrees, with a verdict per test.

Ask it
  • Run the checkout tests
  • Run the tests added in sprint 12
What it needs
  • RequiredThe coding agent, Scribe: a live model profile (or code.model) and the agent runtime, which forgeqe setup installs
  • You givea repository with tests
  • OptionalAzure DevOps or Jira, to pick tests by sprint: set up under sources: in forgeqe.yaml, or sign in with /auth
Tools

find_tests run_tests list_sprints

In detail

Finding and running existing tests

Ask for the tests you want in your own words, in the shell or with forgeqe run:

text
Run all test cases related to login
Run the tests added this sprint
Which tests cover checkout? Don't run them
Run the tests added since 2026-09-01
  1. Scribe starts read-only in the repo. It works out the test framework and decides each test by reading it: its body, and the page objects and helpers it calls. A test named guest can pay that signs in through LoginPage counts as a login test; a test that only mentions login in its name but checks something else does not. For a date range or sprint, it reads git history and selects the tests added in that range, including new test functions in files that already existed. It cannot change files or run anything.
  2. It gives two commands in the framework's own terms: one that lists exactly those tests (playwright test --list, pytest --collect-only, dotnet test --list-tests, …) and one that runs them and writes JUnit XML or TRX results. ForgeQE runs the list command, and Scribe checks the listing is exactly its selection, fixing the command when it is not (up to three rounds).
  3. The list appears as a card: each test with its file and why it matches, the near misses that were left out, and the commands.
  4. Nothing runs until you say so. ForgeQE asks Run N tests? with Run and Don't run (in the shell a dialog; with forgeqe run a numbered prompt in the terminal). When you only asked which tests exist, it stops at the list.
  5. ForgeQE runs the tests itself and reads their results: a card with ✓ or ✗ for each test and the failure message, then the command, the result files, and the output. When tests fail, ForgeQE's agent offers to heal them with heal_tests.
text
┃  ▸ 3 tests found for "tests related to login" · Playwright (TypeScript)
┃
┃  • login › signs in with a valid user
┃      tests/login.spec.ts · Fills the sign-in form and checks the account menu
┃  • login › rejects a wrong password
┃      tests/login.spec.ts · Submits a bad password and checks the error
┃  • checkout › guest can pay
┃      tests/checkout.spec.ts · Signs in through LoginPage before paying
┃
┃  Left out:
┃  • signup › creates an account · Signs up; never signs in
┃
┃  List      npx playwright test --list --grep "login|guest can pay" (ForgeQE ran it: lists exactly these)
┃  Run       npx playwright test --grep "login|guest can pay" --reporter=junit

This sprint. For "tests added this sprint" or "last sprint", ForgeQE fetches the sprints from the Azure DevOps team (sources.azure_devops, iterations of the project's default team unless you name one) and the Jira scrum boards (sources.jira), and asks which one you mean: the current, the three latest past, and the next sprint of each team or board, with their dates. The sprint's start and end dates become the range. Name a sprint ("tests added in Sprint 41") and it is matched without asking; give dates ("since 2026-09-01") and no sprint is fetched.

The selection, the output, and the results are saved in .forgeqe/reports/tests/<time>/: selection.json, output.log, results.json, and the JUnit or TRX file. When a framework cannot write results (for example Jest without jest-junit), the exit code decides and the card shows the end of the output instead.

Through forgeqe mcp, ForgeQE cannot ask you, so run_tests returns the list and runs only when called again with confirmed: true; the MCP client should ask you first.

Reports for tests run outside ForgeQE

ForgeQE's HTML report is not only for runs it makes. run_tests and pr-tests --run write one, and so can any run in a terminal, an IDE's test runner, or CI:

bash
forgeqe report test-results/junit.xml               # from JUnit XML or TRX: any framework, after the run
forgeqe report setup                                # a report after every Playwright or pytest run, automatically

forgeqe report reads JUnit XML or TRX files, or the folder they are in (Playwright --reporter=junit, pytest --junitxml=results.xml, dotnet test --logger trx), and exits 1 when a test failed. Each report is saved in <out_dir>/results/<time>/, so its Trends and Flaky tests pages cover the earlier runs, whoever ran them. JUnit carries one line per failure; a skipped test is counted, not shown.

forgeqe report setup sets up the framework the repository uses:

  • Playwright: it copies a reporter to .forgeqe/playwright-reporter.mjs (commit it) and prints the line to add to playwright.config: reporter: [["list"], ["./.forgeqe/playwright-reporter.mjs"]]. Every run then ends with a report, from npx playwright test, the VS Code Playwright extension, or CI. With screenshot: "only-on-failure" and trace: "retain-on-failure", each failure shows its screenshot, the full error and stack, and the trace.
  • pytest: install forgeqe-cli in the environment that runs the tests (pip install forgeqe-cli). Its plugin loads by itself, from the terminal, VS Code's test explorer, or CI, in a folder with forgeqe.yaml or .forgeqe/ (elsewhere with pytest --forgeqe-report). A test that passes when pytest-rerunfailures runs it again is reported as flaky.

FORGEQE_REPORT=0 turns the reporters off for a run (ForgeQE's own run_tests does this, as it writes its own report). The reporters call forgeqe on PATH, or FORGEQE_BIN; without it they keep the results file and print the command that builds the report. Screenshots and traces are copied into the report only from the working folder, never from a hidden folder or a file named like a key.

Automated tests

Regression packs for a release

Builds a regression pack from what goes into a release (an Azure DevOps sprint or work items, a commit range, pull requests, or changes read from any connected tool): the tests those changes can break, each with why, plus risk-area and smoke tests, and the changes no test covers. Runs it locally, on TestMu AI, or in a pipeline, compares each run with the last, and triages each failure: flaky, environment, broken test code, heal, or an AI browser re-run to tell a test the app outgrew from an app bug.

Ask it
  • Build a regression pack for sprint 12 and run it on TestMu
  • Which tests should we run for the PRs merged this week?
What it needs
  • You givea repository with tests
  • OptionalAzure DevOps or Jira, to read a sprint's or release's work items: set up under sources: in forgeqe.yaml, or sign in with /auth
Tools

build_regression_pack run_regression_pack list_regression_packs

In detail

Regression packs for a release

A regression pack is the set of your repository's tests that a release's changes can break, saved under a name so you can run it again (here, on TestMu AI, or in a pipeline) and compare runs. Ask in your own words:

text
Build a regression pack for sprint 12 and run it on TestMu
Which tests should we run for the PRs merged this week?
Build a pack for Jira fix version 2.4 and the commits since release/2.3 in ../shop-app

Where the changes come from. Any mix of:

  • an Azure DevOps sprint, area, epic or feature, or work item ids: their stories and bugs, the test cases each is tested by, and the files their linked pull requests changed;
  • the commits between two refs of a repository (from and to): the product's repository, or the test repository's own; work items the commit messages name (AB#123, #123, PAY-12) are read too;
  • pull requests by number or URL, or the ones merged in the last N days (Azure DevOps or GitHub);
  • anything ForgeQE's agent can read through its connected tools (a Jira release, GitHub issues, a Confluence release note): it reads the items and passes them in with their text and links.

Why each test is in. A test can be in for more than one reason, and the card and <name>.md say each one:

ReasonMeaning
linkedit automates a test case that tests a changed work item (.forgeqe/test-cases.json, or a case id in the test's attributes)
test code changedthe change is in the test repository, in code the test uses
matches a changethe work item's or pull request's words name what the test does: the elements it uses, its page, its name
risk areathe release touches an area with defects or failures before, and the test works there
smokethe test is tagged smoke, so it is in every pack

Gaps. Changes no test is in for are listed as not covered, each with the closest existing test and what it lacks, so you can extend that test rather than write a new one.

Running it. ForgeQE asks before it runs anything.

  • This machine: ForgeQE builds one command for exactly the pack's tests, the way the repository already runs a test (for .NET, a --filter with each test, the settings an earlier job used, TRX results), runs it, and reads each result. A failed test runs once more; one that then passes is flaky.
  • TestMu AI: the same command, with the variable FORGEQE_PW_WS_ENDPOINT set to TestMu AI's Playwright endpoint for the browser and platform in testmu.yaml (the access key goes in the variable, never in the command, and is removed from the output). The tests must connect to it when it is set: for NUnit, override ConnectOptionsAsync in the test base class; for Playwright Test, set connectOptions from it in playwright.config. ForgeQE refuses a TestMu run when the tests do not read the variable, so a run never looks remote while it ran locally.

Each run is kept with the pack: the card says which tests fail now that passed in the last run, and which were fixed.

Triage: heal, re-run, or file a bug? After each run ForgeQE sorts every failed or flaky test into one route, from evidence that costs nothing first (the retry in the same run, the failure message, the test's history), so the AI is spent only where it decides something:

RouteWhenWhat happens
flakyit passed when run again in the same run, or its history flip-flopsnot healed: quarantine it, or fix the cause it flakes on
environmentnetwork errors, 5xx, a crashed browser, sign-in failingrun again once the environment works; no heal and no bug
test code brokena missing module, a compile error, a missing fixturefix the test's code; healing against the page cannot fix it
healan element the test uses is no longer found (high confidence when it passed its last runs)the healing agent re-runs the flow in the browser and updates the steps and locators
AI re-run decidesan expected value does not hold, a timeout, or something covers the elementthe healing agent re-runs the flow in the browser: heals the test when the flow works, reports an app bug when it fails at the same step

Failures that name the same element or the same message are shown as one cause (a page object or a shared step changed), so one fix can heal them together. When most failures are the environment, ForgeQE says to run the pack again before healing anything, and heal_tests refuses until you name the tests.

The triage is on the run's card and in triage.md and triage.json in the run's folder.
