To heal only the tests it sent to healing, ask "heal the failures of pack release-1.5", or run forgeqe pack heal release-1.5 (--tests for some of them): ForgeQE builds a command for just those tests and the healing agent re-runs each flow in the browser before it changes anything. forgeqe pack triage <name> shows the last run's triage again. Packs are saved in .forgeqe/packs/<name>.json and <name>.md; run output and results in .forgeqe/reports/packs/<name>/<time>/.

Automated tests

Flaky test detection and quarantine

Finds tests that flip between passing and failing in the repository's run history, with their likely cause, and quarantines them once the user agrees.

Ask it
  • Which tests are flaky?
  • Quarantine the flaky login test
What it needs
  • You givea repository whose tests ForgeQE has run
Tools

flaky_tests quarantine_tests

In detail

Flaky tests

Every run_tests and pr-tests run keeps each test's result in .forgeqe/test-history.json in the repository (commit it to share the history, or keep it between CI runs as a cache). A test that fails runs once more in the same run, with the framework's own option (Playwright --last-failed, pytest --lf, Jest --onlyFailures; otherwise the whole command again when the selection is small): when it then passes it is flaky, not failed, and the run does not fail because of it.

"Which tests are flaky?" (flaky_tests) reads the history: each test's last results (✓✗✓↻✓, where ↻ means it passed only when run again), its pass rate, a flakiness score (passes on retry plus pairs of flip-flops in the last 20 runs), and the likely cause from its failure messages, with an example:

CauseFrom messages like
Timingtimeouts, waiting for an element, detached or stale elements
An overlayanother element intercepts the click, the element is covered or not visible
Dataexpected one value, received another
Networknet::ERR_…, resets, 5xx responses
Environmentthe browser closed or crashed, the server was not ready

A test that only fails is broken, not flaky. Quarantine keeps a flaky test running, so its history goes on, but its failures stop failing the run and CI (pr-tests --run exits 0 when only quarantined tests fail); no test code changes. A quarantined test that passes 5 runs in a row is offered for release. The agent offers quarantine_tests (it asks you first) and heal_tests to fix the cause.

yaml
flaky:
  retries: 1            # runs of a failed test in the same run (0 to 3)
  quarantine: suggest   # suggest (the agent asks), auto (ForgeQE quarantines), or off (history only)
  flaky_after: 2        # flakiness score from which a test counts as flaky
12

Pull requests

Reviews, impact, and the tests a change can break

Pull requests

Review pull requests

Reviews an Azure DevOps or GitHub pull request from the local clone for bugs, security, requirements, and tests, and posts the review once the user agrees.

Ask it
  • Review PR 42 and focus on error handling
What it needs
  • RequiredThe coding agent, Scribe: a live model profile (or code.model) and the agent runtime, which forgeqe setup installs
  • You givea local clone of the repository
  • RequiredAzure DevOps, for Azure DevOps pull requests: set up under sources: in forgeqe.yaml, or sign in with /auth; or gh on PATH, for GitHub pull requests
Tools

review_pull_request post_pr_review

In detail

Reviewing pull requests

Ask for a review with the pull request's link or its number in the repo you are in:

text
Review PR 42
Review https://dev.azure.com/acme/Shop/_git/web/pullrequest/42 and focus on error handling
Review my open pull requests
  1. ForgeQE reads the pull request: title, description, source and target branch, and the linked work items with their acceptance criteria (Azure DevOps, through its REST API with sources.azure_devops), or the linked issues (GitHub, through gh, which must be signed in with gh auth login).
  2. It fetches both branches from your clone's remote and checks the pull request's head out in a temporary worktree. Your branch, your working tree, and your uncommitted changes are not touched, and the worktree and fetched refs are removed afterwards. The working directory (or repo) must be a clone of the pull request's repository.
  3. Scribe reviews the diff read-only, reading the code around each change: bugs, security, what the linked work items ask for, missing tests, performance, and compatibility. Each finding has a severity (blocker, major, minor, nit), the file and line, the problem, and a suggestion. code.review_profile picks a cheaper model for it, as for test reviews.
  4. The findings appear as a card, and are saved in .forgeqe/reports/reviews/<time>/ as review.md and review.json. Nothing is posted yet.
  5. To post, say so. ForgeQE asks Post the review? with Post review and Don't post. Findings on a changed line become comments on that line; the rest go in one summary comment with the verdict. On GitHub this is one review with event COMMENT: ForgeQE never approves, requests changes, or votes in your name. If the pull request got new commits after the review, posting is refused; review it again.

Through forgeqe mcp, post_pr_review posts only when called again with confirmed: true; the MCP client should ask you first.

Pull requests

Pull request impact and test selection

Works out which product areas, pages, requirements, and tests a pull request touches, picks the tests it can break, runs them, and posts the impact or results on the pull request once the user agrees.

Ask it
  • What does PR 42 impact?
  • Run the tests PR 42 can break
What it needs
  • You givea local clone of the repository
  • RequiredAzure DevOps, for Azure DevOps pull requests and their linked work items: set up under sources: in forgeqe.yaml, or sign in with /auth; or gh on PATH, for GitHub pull requests
Tools

analyze_pr_impact select_pr_tests post_pr_test_results comment_on_pr

In detail

What a pull request impacts in the product

"What does PR 57 impact?" (or analyze_pr_impact) says which parts of the product a pull request touches and what to test, also when the product's code is in another repository than the tests. Run it from the automation repository; give product_repo (a local clone of the product) when you have one, or just the pull request's link.

  • Reading the pull request: from a clone of the product repository (its diff, and its imports, so a change to a shared component counts for every page that uses it), or, without one, through the GitHub (gh pr diff) or Azure DevOps API (the changed files and their contents).
  • Work items: the ones the pull request is linked to, up through feature and epic, with their Area Paths.
  • Pages: the test ids, ids, labels, placeholders, button and link texts, and routes in the changed code, matched against page memory (every element ForgeQE met on each page of the application). A changed data-testid="submit-order" leads to every page that shows it; a changed route to the pages on that path; with a clone, a changed shared component to the page files that import it, then to their routes.
  • Tests: in the automation repository, the tests that visit those pages (by path, page title, or a changed selector they use, directly or through a page object), the test cases they automate, and the stories those test cases test.
  • Manual tests: the test cases of the impacted stories that no automated test covers yet.

ForgeQE's agent writes the impact report from this: the areas with their evidence and a risk, side effects the diff suggests, the automated and manual tests to run, and the areas with no test; comment_on_pr posts it on the pull request after you agree. Page memory is what ties code to pages, so the more of the application ForgeQE has run, the more it can tie; changed UI files it cannot tie to a page are listed. Backend-only changes are tied through their work items and the agent's reading of the diff.

Tests for a pull request

"Run the tests for PR 42" (or select_pr_tests) picks the automated tests a pull request can break, so only those run:

  • the test files it changes;
  • the tests that import a changed file, following imports through page objects and helpers (JavaScript, TypeScript, Python);
  • the tests that name a changed class or page object (every language, C# and Java included);
  • the tests mapped in .forgeqe/test-cases.json to the test cases of the work items the pull request is linked to (Azure DevOps "Tested By").

Each test comes with why it was picked, and the changed files no test reaches are listed as a coverage gap. With a model, Scribe checks the candidates by reading the tests, adds tests that drive the changed behaviour through the UI, and gives the exact list and run commands; run_tests then runs them after you agree, and post_pr_test_results puts the results on the pull request (it asks first). The tests run from your working tree: check out the pull request's branch to run its versions of the tests.

In CI, without a model:

bash
forgeqe pr-tests --base origin/main --run            # exit 0: passed or nothing affected; 1: a test failed
forgeqe pr-tests 42 --run --post                     # and post the results on pull request 42

Without --agent, the impacted files run as they are; this builds the command for Playwright and pytest projects (for other frameworks add --agent, or run the listed files).

13

API

Contracts and end-to-end scenarios, without a browser

API

API and contract testing

Builds API contracts (status, schema, key fields) from the calls a browser run saw or from an API description (OpenAPI, Swagger, WSDL, GraphQL, .proto, gRPC reflection), and end-to-end API scenarios from runs, descriptions, test cases, or plain English. Runs them straight against the API without a browser, or writes them as tests in the repository. REST, GraphQL, SOAP, and gRPC.

Ask it
  • Turn the last run's API calls into tests
  • Test the API in openapi.yaml
  • Write API scenario tests for the order lifecycle
What it needs
  • OptionalThe coding agent, Scribe: a live model profile (or code.model) and the agent runtime, which forgeqe setup installs
Limits
  • Functional API testing, not load or stress testing.
Tools

api_contracts run_api_contracts api_scenarios run_api_scenarios write_api_tests

In detail

API and contract tests

ForgeQE tests APIs directly too: REST, GraphQL, SOAP, and gRPC. API tests check the same logic as UI tests in a fraction of the time, and they do not break when the page changes. Contracts come from two places:

  • A browser run. Every browser run records the application's own API calls (XHR, fetch, event streams) in network.json in the run folder: method, URL, status, timing, and the request and response bodies when they are JSON, XML, or text. Secrets never reach the file: Authorization, cookies, and API key headers are dropped (only the kind of auth is kept, e.g. bearer), and fields named like a password, token, key, session, or card number are redacted in bodies and query strings. Analytics and monitoring calls are left out. "Turn the last run's API calls into tests" (api_contracts from run) groups the calls into operations: one per method and path, with ids in paths turned into {id} (GET /api/orders/{id}), GraphQL by operation name, SOAP by action, gRPC-web by method. Several calls to one operation make a better contract: a field in every answer is required, a value that differs between calls is not pinned. No model is used.
  • An API description, by link or file: OpenAPI 3 or Swagger 2 (JSON or YAML, or the Swagger UI page, which ForgeQE looks behind for the document), WSDL for SOAP, a GraphQL endpoint (read by introspection) or an SDL or introspection file, .proto files, or grpc://host:port for a gRPC server with reflection (through grpcurl). Each operation gets an example call from the description's examples; values it gives no example for are made up and the contract is marked as needing real values.

Each contract has one example call and what its answer must have: the status, the content type, the body's schema, and key fields (ids, statuses, names, totals: present, with their type; a value only where it does not change between calls, like a status). GraphQL answers must have no errors; SOAP answers no Fault and the expected elements; gRPC calls the OK status. Contract sets are saved in .forgeqe/reports/api/<id>/: contracts.json (edit the examples freely, e.g. to give real ids) and api.md.

Then:

  • Run them now (run_api_contracts, or forgeqe api run): ForgeQE makes each call and checks the answer, without a browser or a model. Calls that change data (POST, PUT, PATCH, DELETE, mutations, SOAP and gRPC calls not named like reads) are left out unless you allow them, on a test environment; the agent asks first. Calls with made-up values are skipped until you give real ones. Results go to results.json and junit.xml, with report.html (each contract a case, its checks the steps, with what the API answered) and suite.json beside them; a 401 or 403 says which header the call needs.
  • Write them as tests (write_api_tests, or forgeqe api tests): Scribe writes them in the repository's own framework and HTTP client (Playwright's request fixture, pytest with requests, REST Assured, RestSharp or HttpClient, supertest...) with the base URL from configuration and credentials from environment variables, and ForgeQE runs them until they pass.

Credentials for API calls go in forgeqe.yaml, as references to environment variables; the values are sent, never stored or shown to a model:

yaml
api:
  capture: true                 # record the application's API calls in every browser run (default)
  base_url: https://staging.shop.example   # where contracts run when not where they were recorded or described
  headers:
    Authorization: Bearer ${API_TOKEN}
    X-Tenant: ${API_TENANT}
  third_party: false            # keep calls to other sites than the page's (sign-in providers, CDNs)
  timeout_seconds: 30
bash
forgeqe api contracts --spec https://petstore3.swagger.io/api/v3/openapi.json
forgeqe api contracts --run                     # the latest browser run's API calls
forgeqe api run --base-url https://qa.shop.example   # exit 1 when a contract fails: a CI gate
forgeqe api tests --repo ../app                 # Scribe writes them as tests

End-to-end API scenarios

A scenario is API calls in order, where later calls use values earlier answers gave: create a cart, add an item to {{cartId}}, place the order, read {{orderId}}. Each step has its own checks (status, schema, key fields). {{$unique}} is new on every run, for emails, usernames, and references that must not clash; {{$uuid}}, {{$now}}, and {{$randomInt}} are there too. Scenarios are saved in the contract set's contracts.json (edit them freely) and listed in api.md. There are four ways to make them (api_scenarios):

FromHowModel
A browser run (--run)The calls the application made, in order. A value an answer gave (an order id) that a later request sends, in its path, query, or body, becomes a variable: saved from that answer, used in that request. A GET made again (polling) is kept once. Emails and usernames sent to sign-up endpoints get {{$unique}}. The UI flow becomes the same flow at the API level.no
An API description (--spec, or a saved set)For each resource with an item path (/pets and /pets/{petId}): its lifecycle (create, read, update, read, delete, then read gives 404), browsing (the list, then its first item), the description's OpenAPI links, and negative cases: an id that does not exist gives 404, no credentials give 401 or 403, an empty body gives 400 or 422. The id comes from the links, else the field named like the path parameter, else id.no
Test cases (--cases ado:2238,jira:SHOP-12)One scenario per test case, planned by the model against the set's operations: the calls, the data, the values to carry, and the case's expected results as checks. Cases with nothing to test are left out with the reason (the same rules as the test case check). The scenario keeps its test case.yes
Plain English (--text "...") or a story"Create a user, log in, add two items, check out, verify the order total." Planned the same way.yes

The planner only uses operations the set has: a step no operation does is listed as unmatched, never invented. Give it the API's operations with --spec (an OpenAPI or Swagger link or file) or a saved set; without a description, make contracts from a browser run first. Every plan is checked before it is saved (each operation exists, each variable is saved before it is used) and shown step by step; nothing runs until you run it.

run_api_scenarios (or forgeqe api run-scenarios) runs them against the API, without a browser or a model: each step in order, the values passed on, every step's checks. A failed step stops its scenario, and the scenario's cleanup steps (deleting what it created) run, those whose values exist. Scenarios that change data run only after you agree (in CI: --allow-writes), on a test environment. Results go to scenario-results.json and scenarios-junit.xml, with report.html (each scenario a case, its calls the steps) and suite.json beside them. API runs count in the team report.

On your existing API automation. write_api_tests with scenarios (or forgeqe api tests --scenarios) has Scribe write each scenario as an end-to-end test in the repository, built on what is already there:

  1. It maps the repository's API layer first: clients, request helpers, auth and base URL fixtures, data builders, schemas.
  2. Each step calls the helper that already calls that operation; only operations without one get a new helper, in the same style.
  3. It reuses helpers, never tests: a scenario never runs another test as a step, and sets up its own data.
  4. A shared helper is only added to, never changed in a way its tests rely on. When Scribe changes one, ForgeQE also runs the tests that use it, and sends any failure back until both pass.
  5. A scenario test for a test case is named after it and recorded in .forgeqe/test-cases.json, and set as the Azure DevOps test case's Associated Automation when write-back is on.
bash
forgeqe api scenarios --run                                  # the latest browser run's flow, chained
forgeqe api scenarios --spec https://shop.test/openapi.json  # lifecycles, browsing, links, negative cases
forgeqe api scenarios --cases ado:2238,ado:2239 --spec openapi.yaml
forgeqe api scenarios --text "create a user, add two items, check out, the total is the sum" --spec openapi.yaml
forgeqe api run-scenarios --allow-writes --base-url https://qa.shop.test   # exit 1 when one fails
forgeqe api tests --scenarios --repo ../api-tests            # on the repo's existing helpers
14

Quality

Accessibility, page performance, and visual changes

Quality

Accessibility and page performance

Checks every page a run visits against WCAG rules (axe-core) and measures its Core Web Vitals, against budgets, with trends over time per page.

Ask it
  • Is the checkout page accessible?
  • Did page performance get worse this week?
What it needs
  • You givea browser run (from browser_run or run_test_cases); new runs need a live model
Limits
  • Per-page Core Web Vitals, not load testing under many users.
Tools

quality_report quality_trends

In detail

Accessibility and performance budgets

Every browser run checks each page it visits and times it, with no extra step:

  • Accessibility: axe-core (installed with ForgeQE) runs the rules of the standard you choose on each page as the agent finds it. The report lists the rules that passed and each rule that failed, with its WCAG success criterion and level, its impact (critical, serious, moderate, minor), the elements, and how to fix it. Automated checks find a part of the WCAG problems (often 30–50%): they are a pre-check, not conformance, and a manual review still matters. quality.accessibility.engine: basic uses ForgeQE's own smaller set of checks instead.
  • Performance: each page's Core Web Vitals, measured in the browser the agent drives: LCP (largest contentful paint), CLS (layout shift), INP (the slowest interaction the agent made), FCP, TTFB, load time, requests and transfer size. These are lab values from one visit, not field data from real users.

A run fails on them only when a budget is exceeded. Without budgets they are reported and tracked, never failed. When a budget is exceeded, the run's steps still passed: forgeqe run exits 1, the browser run says budget_exceeded, a suite case counts as failed (a bug is filed when bugs are set up), and a test is still written for the flow.

yaml
quality:
  accessibility:
    standard: wcag21aa        # wcag2a, wcag21aa (default), wcag22aa, wcag21aaa, section508, en301549, best-practice
    best_practice: false      # also axe-core's best-practice rules
  budgets:
    accessibility:            # most violations per page by impact; unset impacts have no limit (or: accessibility: true for 0 critical, 0 serious)
      critical: 0
      serious: 0
      rules: [color-contrast, label]   # must pass everywhere
      ignore: [region]                 # not counted
    performance:              # or: performance: web-vitals (LCP 2.5s, CLS 0.1, INP 200ms, FCP 1.8s, TTFB 800ms)
      preset: web-vitals
      lcp_ms: 3000
      load_ms: 5000
      pages:                  # by path prefix; the longest match wins
        /search: { lcp_ms: 4000 }

Over time: every run adds each page's violations and timings to .forgeqe/reports/quality-history.json (keep it between CI runs as a cache). "Did accessibility regress?" or "is checkout getting slower?" (quality_trends, or forgeqe quality trends [page]) shows each page's violations over the last runs, the rules that newly fail or were fixed since the previous run, and each timing against its usual value (the median of the last 10 runs; slower or faster only beyond noise). forgeqe quality [run] (quality_report) shows one run's WCAG results, timings and budgets.

bash
forgeqe quality                     # the latest run: WCAG rules passed and failed, Core Web Vitals, budgets
forgeqe quality trends shop.test    # per page over time; exits 1 when a page got worse
Quality

Visual comparison with baselines

Compares each page a run captures with its approved baseline and shows what changed; intended changes become the new baseline once the user agrees.

Ask it
  • What changed visually since the last run?
  • Approve the new header
What it needs
  • You givea browser run (from browser_run or run_test_cases); new runs need a live model
Tools

visual_compare visual_approve

In detail

Visual comparison

Every browser run also captures each page it visits and compares it, pixel by pixel, with that page's approved baseline:

  • Capture: the state the flow leaves each page in, with animations and transitions frozen, the text caret hidden, and the regions you mask hidden (their space kept), so a page compares on what changed, not on what moves. ForgeQE's own overlay is never in the picture. The viewport by default; full_page: true captures the whole page (up to 8000 px tall).
  • Baselines: in .forgeqe/visual-baselines/ next to forgeqe.yaml (commit them to share), one per page, screen size and operating system, since fonts render differently. A page's first capture becomes its baseline (auto_baseline: false turns that off), and baselines.json records which run each baseline came from and why.
  • Compare: each page is same, changed (the share of pixels that differ, and a diff image with the changed pixels in red), or new. Anti-aliasing is ignored; threshold sets how different two colours must be to count. A taller or wider page counts its new area as changed.
  • Budget: a run fails only when quality.budgets.visual is set and a page changes by more than that share of its pixels (a page can have its own limit). Without it, changes are reported, never failed.
  • Approve: when a change is intended, "approve the new home page" (visual_approve, or forgeqe visual approve [run] --page /home) makes the run's capture the new baseline, after you agree. An unintended change is a visual defect.

The report's Visual page shows the baseline, this run, and the difference side by side for every page, changed pages first; click any image to see it full size.

yaml
quality:
  visual:
    mask: [".clock", "#ad-slot", "[data-testid=carousel]"]   # hidden on every page
    pages:
      /news: { mask: [".ticker"], max_diff_percent: 5 }       # by path prefix
    full_page: false
    threshold: 0.1            # colour difference that counts (0–1)
    auto_baseline: true
  budgets:
    visual: 0.5               # most a page may change, in percent of its pixels (or: visual: true for 0.1%)
bash
forgeqe visual                       # the latest run's pages against their baselines
forgeqe visual approve --page /home  # make this run's /home the baseline (asks first; --yes to skip)
Quality

Defect intelligence: trends and prediction

Groups failures into defects, keeps their history across suites (new, known, gone, back again), and gives defect trends and where a change is likely to cause defects, with the cases to run first.

Ask it
  • Which defects keep coming back?
  • What can the changes since main break?
What it needs
  • You givesuites run into one folder (suite.out_dir)
  • Optionalgit on PATH, for predictions from the product's repository
Limits
  • Prediction is a heuristic from the saved suites and the change, not a trained model; its accuracy shows once suites have scored it.
Tools

defect_trends predict_defects

In detail

See Bugs for failures.

Defect trends and prediction

forgeqe defects (or "which defects keep coming back?") reads the suites saved in suite.out_dir and their defect-history.json, and writes defect-trends.md next to them: how many defects are still there, gone, or came back after a fix; the time they took to go away; the most seen ones with their bugs; defects by area (a page or an API route, by its path without ids); defects per suite; and how often predictions were right. The report's Defects page shows the same under Across runs.

forgeqe defects predict [--repo DIR] [--base REF] [--head REF] (or "what can the changes since main break?") says where a change is likely to cause defects. It reads the change between the merge base of base (default: the remote's default branch, else main or master) and head in defects.product_repo (else the working directory). A change touches an area when it changes a file named after it (server/cart.ts for /api/cart/:id) or its diff mentions the route. Each touched area gets a score from what the saved suites know:

SignalScore
The diff mentions the area's route+3
A changed file is named after it+2
Each earlier defect there (three at most)+2
Defects there came back after a fix+2
A defect there is still open+1
Its cases failed in 20% (50%) of recent runs+1 (+2)

6 or more is high, 3 or more medium. It lists the cases that ended on the high and medium areas as the ones to run first, and the changed product code that no case reaches. It is a heuristic, not a trained model: each prediction is saved in defect-predictions.json, and the first suite after it scores it (did its defects fall in the areas rated medium or high; did the high areas get one), so forgeqe defects gives its accuracy.

Release readiness uses the defects too: a regression on a case in scope blocks the release (NO-GO), and a new defect with no bug is a risk.

15

Release

Requirement coverage and a release verdict

Release

Requirement coverage and release readiness

Gives requirement coverage and a GO / GO WITH RISKS / NO-GO verdict for a sprint, area, epic, or stories, from test cases, results, bugs, and flaky tests; can publish it to the wiki.

Ask it
  • Are we ready to release?
  • Coverage of sprint 12
What it needs
  • RequiredAzure DevOps or Jira: set up under sources: in forgeqe.yaml, or sign in with /auth
Tools

release_readiness

In detail

Requirement coverage and release readiness

"Are we ready to release sprint 12?", "coverage of epic 300" (or release_readiness) builds the coverage matrix for an Azure DevOps scope: a sprint (current or a name), an area path, an epic or feature and every story under it, or story ids.

  • Each story with the test cases that test it ("Tested By"), whether each is automated (Automation status, or .forgeqe/test-cases.json), and its last result: the newest test point outcome in Azure DevOps, or, where no plan has run it, ForgeQE's own runs (suite reports, then the automated test's history).
  • Gaps: stories with no test case, test cases nobody automated, cases that have not run.
  • Open bugs in the scope and linked to its stories, by severity.
  • Flaky and quarantined tests from the test history.
  • The verdict: NO-GO when something blocks (open critical or high bugs past the limit, a story with a failing test case, the pass rate below the minimum), GO WITH RISKS when something is missing (stories without test cases, cases not run or blocked, flaky or quarantined tests, other open bugs), else GO; each with its reasons.

The report is saved as readiness.md (and readiness.json) under .forgeqe/reports/readiness/, shown as a card, and published to the project wiki with publish: "/Release readiness/Sprint 12" after you agree. The agent can also build an Azure DevOps dashboard for it with create_ado_dashboard.

yaml
release:
  max_critical: 0      # open severity-1 bugs allowed
  max_high: 0          # open severity-2 bugs allowed
  min_pass_rate: 95    # percent of executed test cases that must pass
  require_tests: true  # a story without a test case is a risk
Release

Reports you can share

Writes up what the Orchestrator found, such as open defects across projects, release status, or pipeline health, as a page with filters (project, state, priority, assignee, …), charts, and a sortable table, plus Markdown and CSV. Every table is built from evidence: the results of the tool calls the agent made, or a read-only query ForgeQE runs itself, listed on the page's Sources with each raw result kept beside the report.

Ask it
  • Create a release report for all projects
  • Write up the open defects as a report I can share
What it needs
  • RequiredA live model profile in llms.json (an API key or Microsoft Entra ID sign-in)
Limits
  • Rows come only from tool results in the session or a query ForgeQE runs; the agent writes only the summary, which is not checked figure by figure.
  • A WIQL query reads up to 1000 work items; the page says when a query matched more.
  • The page is not published anywhere unless the reports folder is served (notify.report_url).
Tools

write_report

In detail

Reports from the agent

Ask for a report and ForgeQE's agent writes one you can share: "generate a defect report for all open defects", "create a release report for all projects", "summarise this sprint's test results as a report". Every table in it is built from evidence, not typed by the model:

  • ForgeQE keeps the result of every tool call in the session (its own tools, and the Azure DevOps and Jira MCP servers). A dataset of the report names the calls it comes from, and ForgeQE takes the rows from their results.
  • Or the dataset is a read-only query that ForgeQE runs itself when it writes the report: a WIQL query reads up to 1000 work items with their project, type, state, priority, severity, assignee, area, iteration, dates, and a link. The agent uses this for "all" of something.
  • Or the dataset is ForgeQE's own past results (history:): suites (one row per run, with its run type in suite_scope), case_results, step_results, writebacks, readiness, readiness_stories, or readiness_cases, from every run on this machine and the bundles teammates shared with forgeqe bi export (see Team report). Every row has user and machine.
  • The agent writes only the summary paragraph.

The report goes to the reports folder (export.out_dir, .forgeqe/reports/ by default):

text
.forgeqe/reports/open-defects-all-projects-20261013-101512/
  report.html         # one file: Overview (summary, analytics, charts), the rows (filters, sortable table), Sources
  report.md           # the same, to edit, paste into a wiki, or attach
  open-defects.csv    # each dataset's rows
  evidence/E1.json    # the raw result of each tool call the report used

The page has three parts in its menu:

  • Overview: the summary, then what the data says. When the rows are work items (defects, bugs, tickets), it works out from them: how many are open, critical and high, unassigned (and how many of those are critical or high), their median and oldest age, and how many had no change in 30 days; Needs someone now, the critical and high ones with nobody on them or untouched for 30 days; severity by project, who has them (the same person written "Khan, Javed" or "Javed Khan" counted once), how old they are, how many were opened each week, and the most used tags. Other data gets a count by each of its columns. Click a bar to see its rows.
  • The rows (named after the data, such as Open defects): filters for the columns that are in the data (Project, State, Type, Priority, Severity, Assigned To, Area, Iteration, …, and other columns with a short list of values), a search, a table sorted by any column, 50 rows at a time, and Export CSV for the rows you filtered.
  • Sources: each tool call, when it ran, and how many rows it gave, with a link to its raw result.

The analytics are arithmetic on the rows, worked out in the page; the model writes none of them.

The chat gets a short summary and a card with the paths as links, and Open the report appears as a next step. To post it, ask for it ("post the report to the release channel") once notify.channels is set up; the reports folder is linked when it is served (notify.report_url).

Team report

forgeqe bi builds one report from every run in the reports folder: browser suites, monitor rounds, release readiness, single browser runs, desktop and mobile runs, exploratory testing, and API runs, each tagged with its run type. It builds an Evidence site (needs Node.js); without Node.js, or when Evidence fails, it writes ForgeQE's own page, which opens straight from disk (bi: in forgeqe.yaml; see README.md).

bash
forgeqe bi init                              # scaffold the Evidence project and install its packages
forgeqe bi build                             # refresh the data and build (ForgeQE's own page if Evidence cannot build)
forgeqe bi build --static                    # ForgeQE's own page only, no Node.js needed
forgeqe bi sync                              # refresh the datasets and pages, without building
forgeqe bi export [--out FILE|DIR]           # your runs, in one file to share
forgeqe bi build --from //share/qa-reports   # this machine's runs plus every bundle there (repeatable)

For one report across the team, each person runs forgeqe bi export, which writes their runs and readiness reports to one file in .forgeqe/reports/shared/. Whoever builds puts the files in <out_dir>/collected/ (.forgeqe/reports/collected/ by default), or lists shared folders under bi.collect, or passes --from, and runs forgeqe bi build. Every table gets user and machine columns; the report adds Results by run type and Case results by person tables. A run that arrives twice (in two bundles, or a bundle and this machine) counts once. A bundle is read as data only: a file that is not one, or is larger than 64 MB, is skipped with a note.

In the shell, /report builds ForgeQE's own page from the same data, /report export writes your bundle, and /report <what> asks the agent for a report (see Reports from the agent).

Static BI

forgeqe bi adds an offline BI layer on top of those reports. It exports curated CSV datasets from ForgeQE's own suite history and release-readiness reports, plus optional Azure DevOps WIQL and Jira JQL datasets from bi.datasets in forgeqe.yaml, into an Evidence project that reads them through its CSV source, then runs evidence sources and evidence build to produce a static site in the configured build directory. ForgeQE uses the open-source Evidence line (@evidence-dev/evidence 40.x, pinned in the generated package.json), because the newer Evidence Studio CLI has no local build and keeps data in its cloud. Building it needs Node.js 18 or later; serve the folder to view it (npx serve), since it cannot load its data when opened from disk. When Node.js is missing or the Evidence build fails, forgeqe bi build writes ForgeQE's own self-contained page from the same data instead, which opens straight from disk, and says why; --static always does that, and --evidence fails instead of falling back. Dataset ids become table names (forgeqe.<id>), so they use letters, digits, and _. Pages ForgeQE generates carry a "Managed by forgeqe bi" comment in their front matter; a page without it is yours and is never overwritten. Tables with no rows yet stay out of the Evidence project (Evidence cannot build over an empty table), and their sections read "None yet." No backend service stays up between runs.

yaml
bi:
  title: ForgeQE BI
  datasets:
    ado_current_sprint:
      kind: ado_wiql
      project: Shop
      wiql: |
        SELECT [System.Id]
        FROM WorkItems
        WHERE [System.TeamProject] = @project
          AND [System.WorkItemType] IN GROUP 'Microsoft.RequirementCategory'
    jira_open_bugs:
      kind: jira_jql
      jql: project = SHOP AND issuetype = Bug AND statusCategory != Done
bash
forgeqe bi init            # scaffold the Evidence project and install its packages
forgeqe bi build           # refresh the data and build the site (ForgeQE's own page if Evidence cannot build)
forgeqe bi build --static  # ForgeQE's own page only, no Node.js needed

One report for the whole team

Each person runs forgeqe bi export, which writes every kind of run they have (suites, monitor rounds, single browser runs, desktop, mobile, exploratory and API runs, and release-readiness reports) to one JSON file in .forgeqe/reports/shared/, each run tagged with its run type. Whoever builds the report puts the files they receive in .forgeqe/reports/collected/ (or lists shared folders under bi.collect in forgeqe.yaml, or passes --from FILE|DIR, repeatable) and runs forgeqe bi build. Every table gets user and machine columns, the home page and ForgeQE's own page gain "Results by run type" and "Case results by person" tables, and a run that arrives twice (from two bundles, or a bundle and this machine) counts once. A bundle is read as data only: anything that is not one, or is larger than 64 MB, is skipped with a note, and a malformed run inside one is skipped whole.

In the shell, /report builds ForgeQE's own page from the same data, /report export writes your bundle, and /report <what you want> asks the agent, which can now build write_report datasets from these past results (history: case_results, suites, readiness, …) as well as from live Azure DevOps or Jira queries.

bash
forgeqe bi export                          # your runs, in one file to share
forgeqe bi build --from //share/qa-reports # this machine's runs plus every bundle in that folder

Model prices and usage

Prices. ForgeQE prices every model call at the model's list price, from two public price lists: models.dev (the list the agent runtime uses) and LiteLLM's model price list, which also has Azure's Data Zone prices. It keeps the few fields it needs in ~/.forgeqe/prices.json, waits a few seconds for it the first time, refreshes it once a day in the background, and uses the last copy offline; only the public lists are downloaded, nothing about your runs is sent (FORGEQE_PRICES=off stops the downloads). Costs at list price say so: $1.73 (list price of gpt-5.4 (Azure Global) from models.dev, 2026-10-06). For an Azure Data Zone deployment, which costs about 10% more, set "deployment_type": "data_zone" on the profile.

Model parameters. Models differ in what a request may carry: reasoning models (o1, o3, o4, gpt-5) take max_completion_tokens instead of max_tokens and only the default temperature, and some OpenAI-compatible servers refuse max_completion_tokens, reasoning_effort or response_format. ForgeQE works this out by itself. A profile is a reasoning model when its model or deployment name says so (an Azure deployment called chat-prod with "model": "gpt-5.4" counts), or when it sets "reasoning": true; "reasoning": false says it is not. When the API still refuses a parameter, the browser, desktop and mobile agents send the call again without it and leave it out of later calls to that model. Nothing needs setting for a new model.

A profile's own "price": { "input": 2.5, "output": 15, "cached_input": 0.25 } (per million tokens) wins, for a negotiated or enterprise rate. Leave currency out and reports use this machine's currency, or set "currency": "USD". Without cached_input, cached input gets the model's cache discount from its list price, and the cost is marked as an estimate (≈$1.52); with no list price for the model either, cached input is charged at the full rate and the cost is an upper bound (up to $6.11). When your price differs from the list price, ForgeQE says so (forgeqe models, the shell, and the start of each command), since that is usually a typo or an old price. Anthropic's cache writes are priced at their own rate. A provisioned (PTU) deployment is billed per hour, not per token, so its costs are what the tokens would cost at list price. Requests over a model's long-context threshold (272K tokens for GPT-5.4) cost more and are priced at the normal rate.

Usage after every job. Every browser run, Scribe job, heal, suite, and monitor round ends with a Usage line: tokens in (and how many of them were cached), tokens out, and the cost at the profile's price. A request to ForgeQE's agent in the shell, and every forgeqe run, code, suite, and heal command, ends with the total for everything it started, by role:

text
This request used 1.1M tokens (1.1M in, 30.0K of it cached · 34.7K out) · $3.07 — ForgeQE's agent 42.0K · $0.12, browser agent 52.7K · $0.15, Scribe 1.0M · $2.80

Spending budgets

A budget warns you before model spend gets out of hand. Set it under budget: in forgeqe.yaml, in the currency of your prices (see Model prices and usage):

yaml
budget:
  run: 2          # one browser run
  day: 25
  month: 400
  # currency: USD # default: the currency of the model's price

Set any of the three. A budget only warns; it never stops a run. Each budget warns once at 80%, once at 90%, and once at 100%. When spend jumps past more than one of these at once, you get one warning, for the highest:

text
Budget: this month's model spend is at $245.10, 82% of the $300.00 month budget (budget.month in forgeqe.yaml). Nothing is stopped.
  • Where you see it. In the shell, the warning is a line in the conversation. Every other forgeqe command writes it to the terminal's error output.
  • What counts. run is the spend of one browser run. day and month add up every ForgeQE process on this machine (days and months are in UTC). Runs that the agent starts through the MCP server count too, because every process writes its model calls to the usage ledger in ~/.forgeqe/usage/ (FORGEQE_USAGE_DIR moves it). The ledger holds counts, times, and names, never prompt or response text. With FORGEQE_USAGE=off, nothing is kept on disk, and a budget counts only the calls of the process that warns.
  • What does not count. A call with no price, such as a github_copilot profile without a price, and a call priced in a different currency than the budget.
  • Spend from before. If you start ForgeQE when the day or month is already past 80%, it does not repeat that warning. It warns only for a threshold that spend passes from then on.
  • In reports. The Trends and forecast page of a report's Tokenomics shows spend on this machine and the next days at the recent pace, against the budget.
16

Systems

Azure DevOps, Jira, and Confluence

Systems

Work with Azure DevOps, Jira, GitHub, and Confluence

Reads anything in the configured systems (work items, issues, queries, boards, pages), makes changes the user asks for, puts run evidence (screenshots, reports) on work items and issues as attachments or comments, and builds Azure DevOps dashboards and wiki pages.

Ask it
  • Show the open bugs in this sprint
  • Read the checkout requirements from Confluence
  • Make a dashboard of this sprint's test results
  • Add the last screenshot of that run to ADO 256 as a comment
  • Run the GitHub issues labelled login as test cases
What it needs
  • RequiredAzure DevOps or Jira or Xray or Confluence: set up under sources: in forgeqe.yaml, or sign in with /auth
Tools

read_confluence create_ado_dashboard write_ado_wiki_page attach_to_work_item

In detail

Azure DevOps, Jira, and other systems

ForgeQE's agent connects to each system's MCP server, configured from the same sources: settings in forgeqe.yaml: organization, site URL, and the names of the environment variables that hold the tokens. You don't write any MCP config yourself. Azure DevOps's server is the organization, so a project is optional and only limits ForgeQE's own test, sprint, and bug calls.

SystemMCP serverNeeds
Azure DevOps ServicesMicrosoft's Azure DevOps MCP server (npx @azure-devops/mcp), with all its tools, each turn getting the groups it needs: work items, boards, test plans, pipelines, repos, wiki pages, search, and security alertsNode.js, and ADO_PAT or az login
Jira Cloud, Server/Data Centermcp-atlassian (uvx), with issues, JQL search, comments, and transitions. mcp: rovo uses Atlassian's hosted server instead (Cloud, and an admin must allow API tokens)uv; JIRA_EMAIL and JIRA_API_TOKEN (Cloud) or a personal access token (Server/DC)
Confluencemcp-atlassian, shared with Jira (or its own when Jira isn't set up); Atlassian's hosted server covers it on the same siteconfluence: true on a Jira Cloud site, or confluence.url + tokens
GitHubGitHub's hosted MCP server with every toolset, each turn getting the groups of tools it needs (sources.github.toolsets limits them), on in a repository with a GitHub remote or when sources.github is set. GitHub issues are also a test case source (github:#12, github:acme/shop#12,#13, github:label=login, github:q=login in:title): results go back as issue comments, and sources.bugs.system: github files bugs as issues. GitHub has no API to upload a screenshot to an issue, so a bug names the run folder insteadGITHUB_TOKEN, or the GitHub CLI's sign-in (forgeqe auth login github)
Xray, TestRail, Zephyr Scale, qTestnone; ForgeQE's APIXRAY_CLIENT_ID / XRAY_CLIENT_SECRET, TESTRAIL_EMAIL / TESTRAIL_API_KEY, ZEPHYR_API_TOKEN, QTEST_TOKEN
Anything elseservers you add under mcp:
yaml
sources:
  azure_devops: { org_url: https://dev.azure.com/acme }   # every project; token in ADO_PAT. project: Shop limits ForgeQE's own calls
  jira: { url: https://acme.atlassian.net }                              # JIRA_EMAIL + JIRA_API_TOKEN
mcp:
  github:
    url: https://api.githubcopilot.com/mcp/
    headers: { Authorization: "Bearer {env:GITHUB_TOKEN}" }

Tokens never go into the config: servers get them through {env:NAME} references that the agent runtime fills in, and the agent never sees them. forgeqe mcp list shows what your settings give. When the agent starts, the shell and forgeqe run print which servers connected.

Whatever has no MCP server, or whose server didn't connect, goes through ForgeQE's own tools, which call the REST APIs directly: find_test_cases and run_test_cases for test cases and posting results back, read_confluence for Confluence pages, and query_api to read anything else from Azure DevOps, Jira, Xray, or Confluence. query_api only reads (GET requests and Xray GraphQL queries) and only reaches the configured hosts. sources.azure_devops.mcp: false or sources.jira.mcp: false uses the API only.

Microsoft's Azure DevOps server can't create dashboards, so "create a dashboard in QI-Payments with all defects and this sprint's stories" goes through ForgeQE's create_ado_dashboard: one shared query per widget under Shared Queries/ForgeQE, then a dashboard with a work item list or count tile for each. Your ADO_PAT needs Work Items (read and write) and permission to edit dashboards. Chart widgets aren't made through the API; for those the agent uses the browser, signed in to dev.azure.com with a saved login.

Its wiki tools can only write into a wiki that already exists, so wiki pages go through ForgeQE's write_ado_wiki_page. If the project has no wiki yet, that tool creates the project wiki first, along with any missing parent pages. The PAT needs Wiki (read and write).

Pull requests are opened with ForgeQE's create_ado_pull_request ("create a PR from feature/cart to main in the shop repository"), since the repositories tool group is not loaded by default. It checks that both branches exist, and returns an active pull request between the same branches instead of opening a second one. The PAT needs Code (read and write).

Requirements work too: "write and run test cases for the checkout rules page in Confluence" makes the agent read the page, write one case per rule or acceptance criterion (with the main negative paths), show them, and run them. It saves them into a test system only when you ask.

The agent creates or changes items (a bug, a comment, a status change) only when you ask it to; bugs for failed cases come from sources.bugs, not from the agent.

Systems

Read any data in Azure DevOps, Jira, Xray, and Confluence, and open Azure DevOps pull requests

Reads through a system's REST API what its MCP tools do not cover: pull requests and repositories, test plans and suites, classic releases, and anything else in Azure DevOps, Jira, Xray, or Confluence. Opens Azure DevOps pull requests, which the API reads cannot. Sets a pull request reviewer as required, which the MCP server's update_reviewers cannot. Given on every turn once a system is set up, so the agent never turns to the browser or the shell for them.

Ask it
  • Are there open pull requests in any repository of QI-Claims?
  • List the test plans in QI-MobileBanking with their suites
  • Create a pull request from demo_mai_qiclaims to main in the QI-Claims Typescript repository
  • Add javed@example.com as a required reviewer on PR 55
What it needs
  • RequiredAzure DevOps or Jira or Xray or Confluence: set up under sources: in forgeqe.yaml, or sign in with /auth
Tools

query_api create_ado_pull_request set_ado_pr_reviewer

In detail

See Azure DevOps, Jira, and other systems (from the README).

17

Reference

MCP, tuning, logs, environment variables, and every command

MCP

Servers ForgeQE's agent connects to

When ForgeQE's agent starts, it connects to an MCP server for each system set up under sources:. The server settings come from the same place as everything else: the organization and project, the site URL, and the names of the environment variables that hold the tokens. You don't write MCP config for these.

Every tool, without paying for every tool. Each server is started with everything it has: Azure DevOps with all its tool groups, Jira and Confluence with TOOLSETS=all, GitHub with every toolset. Every tool given to the model is sent with each of its requests (GitHub's are about 79K tokens, Jira's and Confluence's about 29K, Azure DevOps' about 21K), so a turn gets only the groups of tools its request needs, the way it gets ForgeQE's own capabilities:

  • ForgeQE asks each server for its tools when the session starts and keeps the list for a week (~/.forgeqe/cache/mcp-tools), then groups them by their names. No server needs a table of its own: tools named by prefix (repo_…, wit_…, jira_…) are grouped by prefix, and a large prefix again by what its tools are about (Jira's sprints, boards, versions); tools named verb first (list_pull_requests) by what they act on (pull requests, issues, Dependabot alerts). A group is named <server>:<group>, such as github:pull-request or atlassian:jira-sprint.
  • A turn gets a group when its request names what the group is about ("create a PR", "the current sprint", "dependabot alerts"), when its tools ran in the last turns, or when the agent asks for it with use_capabilities: every group is in the list of capabilities the agent has, so none is out of reach and the browser is never the way round a missing tool. A request that names a system ("the GitHub repository", "in Azure DevOps") gets that system's groups from its words, not another's.
  • A small server (under about 3K tokens) is one group, on every turn. A server ForgeQE could not ask keeps all its tools on every turn, as before.
  • agent.capabilities.always and off take group ids too: always: [github:issue], off: [github:copilot].

Azure DevOps. Microsoft's Azure DevOps MCP server, run with every tool group it has: projects and teams (azure-devops:core), iterations and boards (azure-devops:work), work items (azure-devops:wit), pipelines (azure-devops:pipeline), repositories and pull requests (azure-devops:repo), wiki (azure-devops:wiki), test plans (azure-devops:testplan), search (azure-devops:search), and security alerts (azure-devops:advsec). The first four are on every turn (about 21 tools, roughly 10.7K tokens); the others come as described above. ForgeQE's own tools cover dashboards, wiki pages in a project without a wiki, and opening pull requests (create_ado_pull_request) and reviewing them, and query_api reads anything else, such as classic releases. The organization comes from org_url (https://dev.azure.com/acme or https://acme.visualstudio.com). ForgeQE passes the PAT from token_env (default ADO_PAT) the way the server wants it; without a PAT it uses your az login session. Its tools are azure_devops_*. mcp_domains says which groups are on every turn (default [core, work, work-items, pipelines]; [all] for every group, at about 21K tokens a request); agent.capabilities.off switches a group off for good ([azure-devops:advsec]). It needs Node.js. Azure DevOps Server (on-premises) isn't supported by Microsoft's server, so it uses ForgeQE's API.

yaml
sources:
  azure_devops:
    org_url: https://dev.azure.com/acme   # the MCP server takes the organization, not a project
    # project: Shop                       # optional: limit ForgeQE's own API calls to one project
    token_env: ADO_PAT
    mcp: true                     # false: API only
    mcp_domains: [core, work, work-items, pipelines, repositories]   # optional: groups on every turn; the others come when needed

Microsoft's server has no dashboard tools and can't save queries. Ask for a dashboard ("create a dashboard in QI-Payments with all defects in every state and the current sprint's stories") and ForgeQE's own create_ado_dashboard tool does it through the REST API with ADO_PAT: it runs each WIQL query once to check it, saves it as a shared query under Shared Queries/ForgeQE (updating one of the same name), and creates a team or project dashboard with a work item list or count tile per query, plus optional markdown text. The PAT needs Work Items (read and write) and permission to edit dashboards. Chart widgets are not made through the API; for those the agent uses the browser, which has to be signed in to dev.azure.com (forgeqe login <name> with a login for it under logins:).

Microsoft's wiki tools (azure_devops_wiki, azure_devops_wiki_upsert_page) only work in a wiki that already exists. In a project without one they answer "No wiki found". Ask for a wiki page and ForgeQE's write_ado_wiki_page tool writes it through the REST API instead. If the project has no wiki, the tool creates the project wiki (<Project>.wiki) first, then any missing parent pages (empty), then the page itself. A page that already exists is replaced. Code wikis are written on their published branch. The PAT needs Wiki (read and write). A login added to forgeqe.yaml while ForgeQE is running is picked up the next time the agent lists logins or signs in.

Jira. mcp-atlassian, run with uvx mcp-atlassian, for Jira Cloud (JIRA_EMAIL and an API token in JIRA_API_TOKEN) and Server/Data Center (a personal access token in token_env). Its tools are atlassian_jira_*: get and search issues (JQL), create and update issues, comments, transitions, and fields. It needs uv, which ForgeQE's engine uses anyway. For Jira Cloud you can use Atlassian's hosted Rovo MCP server instead with mcp: rovo. It authenticates with your email and API token, but an organization admin must first turn on API token authentication for it.

yaml
sources:
  jira:
    url: https://acme.atlassian.net
    mcp: mcp-atlassian            # rovo | false

Xray. Xray has no official MCP server, so ForgeQE's own tools cover it. You can add a community server under mcp: if you want one.

Other servers. Add any MCP server under mcp:. A local server has a command (and optional args, env, cwd); a remote one has a url (and optional headers). Write secrets as {env:NAME}; the agent runtime fills them in from the environment. A server with the same name as a built-in one (azure_devops, atlassian) replaces it, and enabled: false turns one off.

yaml
mcp:
  github:
    url: https://api.githubcopilot.com/mcp/
    headers: { Authorization: "Bearer {env:GITHUB_TOKEN}" }
  xray:
    command: npx -y @triparc/xray-mcp
    env: { XRAY_CLIENT_ID: "{env:XRAY_CLIENT_ID}", XRAY_CLIENT_SECRET: "{env:XRAY_CLIENT_SECRET}" }

forgeqe mcp list prints the servers your settings give, with secrets shown only as {env:…}, and says why any system is left out. When the agent starts, the transcript shows the result:

text
  ▸ Connecting to Azure DevOps MCP, Jira MCP (mcp-atlassian)…
  ✓ Azure DevOps MCP connected
  ✗ Jira MCP (mcp-atlassian) did not connect (…). ForgeQE's API tools cover it.

Without an MCP server. A system with no server, or whose server didn't start, still works through ForgeQE's own tools, which call the REST APIs with the same settings:

  • find_test_cases and run_test_cases read test cases and post results back (test runs, comments, Xray Test Executions), whether or not an MCP server is connected.
  • read_confluence reads Confluence pages (by id, link, or CQL) as text, with table cells kept apart.
  • query_api reads anything else: work items, WIQL results, builds, Jira issues and JQL searches, Xray tests and executions (GraphQL on Cloud, /rest/raven on Server/DC), Confluence spaces and pages. It only reads (GET requests and GraphQL queries, never mutations), only reaches the configured hosts, and cuts large answers to fit the agent's context.

The agent runs test cases with run_test_cases even when an MCP server is connected, so reports and write-back work the same either way. It uses the MCP tools for everything else, and creates or changes items (a bug, a comment, a transition) only when you ask.

ForgeQE as an MCP server

forgeqe mcp serves ForgeQE's tools to other agents over stdio (MCP):

json
{ "mcpServers": { "forgeqe": { "command": "forgeqe", "args": ["mcp", "--repo", "/path/to/app"] } } }
ToolWhat it does
browser_runCarry out a goal in a real browser and report pass or fail, steps, and the run folder
find_test_casesRead cases from Azure DevOps, Jira, Xray, TestRail, Zephyr Scale, qTest, files, or text without running them
run_test_casesRun cases as a suite: step verdicts, reports, JUnit, write-back, and bugs
run_detailsA run's steps and locators with confidence, for writing a test
run_testForgeQE runs a test command and returns the exit code and output
page_memoryWhat page memory knows about a host or page, the recorded flows, or clear a host's memory (clear asks first; confirmed: true over MCP, only after the user agreed)
read_confluenceConfluence pages as text, by id, link, or CQL
query_apiRead anything from Azure DevOps, Jira, Xray, or Confluence through their APIs, with ForgeQE's credentials
push_branchPush to a new branch; never the default branch, never forced
set_ci_secretStore an environment variable as a GitHub or Azure Pipelines secret without revealing it
write_testTurn a passed run into a test in the repo; ForgeQE runs it until it passes in a browser and a reviewer approves it (up to code.max_attempts)
heal_testsforgeqe heal: fix out-of-date tests, report app bugs, and confirm by running the tests
find_testsFind a repository's existing tests that match a request, a date range, or a sprint, confirmed with the framework's list command; nothing runs
run_testsRun a find_tests selection after the user confirms (confirmed: true over MCP), with a result per test
list_sprintsRecent sprints from Azure DevOps and Jira with their dates
monitorOne monitor round, start/stop/status of a background monitor, or a test alert
loginsList logins, forget sessions, or sign in by hand (not over stdio, which has no one to wait for)
forgeqe_setupProfiles, MCP servers, the docs by topic, installing the engine, scaffolding a config, and listing, showing, or creating skills
databasesThe databases set up for the project (never passwords or connection strings); with test, connect to one
database_schemaWhat a database holds: its tables, or the tables a question needs with their columns and joins
run_sqlOne checked SQL statement: reads in a read-only session with a row cap; a write only on a writable database, after the user agrees (confirmed: true over MCP)
generate_test_dataTest data from a spec that fits the schema, or a masked subset of real rows: previewed, written to files, or inserted after the user agrees
test_data_batchesThe batches generate_test_data inserted; remove one again after the user confirms
data_flowsThe data flows in flows.yaml: list, map, impact of a table or column, or suggest, validate and save a flow
check_flowsCheck each hop of the flows: every row arrived, mapped columns hold the expected values, no duplicates, on time
trace_recordFollow one record through the flows, hop by hop
read_mapping_fileRead a mapping sheet in the project (.xlsx, .xlsm, .csv, .tsv), to write data flows from
mobile_uploadUpload an Android or iOS build to TestMu AI (LambdaTest) after the user agrees; returns its lt:// id
generated_attributionThe footer for a pull request and the trailer for a commit that include ForgeQE's code: the runs' fq: ids and how much they wrote
forgeqe_commandA forgeqe command no other tool covers, in an allowlisted shape; reading commands run at once, the others after the user agrees (confirmed: true over MCP)

--repo sets the working directory (default: where it starts), and --profile, --url, and --headed apply to browser runs. Progress goes to stderr; stdout carries only MCP messages. Long calls send progress notifications, and a cancelled call stops its browser. The shell and forgeqe run use the same tools, served to the agent runtime over local HTTP with a one-time token.

Engine tuning

Engine settings are FORGEQE_ENGINE_* variables. Set them in the shell or .env; they win over forgeqe.yaml:

VariableDefaultEffect
FORGEQE_ENGINE_TIMEOUT_SECONDStimeout_seconds (600)Whole-run limit
FORGEQE_ENGINE_STEP_TIMEOUT180Per-step limit
FORGEQE_ENGINE_FAST_MODEtrueFlash mode, no planning
FORGEQE_ENGINE_FULL_AGENT_MODEfalseThe engine's full agent mode instead of fast mode
FORGEQE_ENGINE_MAX_ACTIONS_PER_STEPengine defaultActions per model call
FORGEQE_ENGINE_MAX_FAILURESengine defaultConsecutive failures before stopping
FORGEQE_ENGINE_LOW_VISION_DETAILfrom vision_detailLow-detail screenshots
FORGEQE_ENGINE_CONTROL_LOOP_MAX_RETRIES4Repeats of the same click, input, search, or select before the agent is told to change approach; the same number again after that stops the run
FORGEQE_ENGINE_CHANNEL / FORGEQE_ENGINE_EXECUTABLE_PATHchromeBrowser to launch
FORGEQE_ENGINE_SKIP_ERROR_RECOVERY_PROMPTfalseDrop the recovery instructions

Logs for debugging

Every session (forgeqe start, run, suite, heal, monitor, code, mcp, login, setup) writes a log to .forgeqe/logs/ next to forgeqe.yaml (the newest 30 are kept). It has the versions and settings in use, what you asked, ForgeQE's agent's answers and every tool call with its result, every browser step and result, the browser engine's own log lines, and errors. Secrets are replaced before anything is written: API keys, tokens and passwords from the environment, keys in llms.json, and credentials you gave in a message.

Each session has two files with the same events:

  • <time>-<command>.log, to read: a header with what ran, where, with which model and settings, then one entry per event with the time on this computer, where it came from (BROWSER, AGENT, TOOL, MODEL, RUN, ERROR…) and what happened in words, its details indented below. Paths inside the project are written from it, and errors stand apart with a blank line around them:

    text
    15:58:44  BROWSER   Step 2 · Checkout
                    Page    https://shop.example.com/checkout
                    Goal    Pay with the saved card
                    Action  CLICK #pay
15:58:46  BROWSER   ✗ Step 2 failed
                    Error   #pay not found

  • <time>-<command>.jsonl, the same events as data, one {"at", "kind", "data"} object a line, for scripts and tools (and what ForgeQE's own pages read). Logs written before this change are one file with a line of data each, and are still read.

To share a session, type /logs in the shell (/logs shots adds screenshots), or run forgeqe logs bundle afterwards. Either writes one .tar.gz with the session log, the run folders it mentions (run.json, steps.json, trace.md, resume.json, workflow.yaml, the failure screenshot), your forgeqe.yaml, your profiles without keys, and the versions. forgeqe logs lists recent logs.

Start with --debug (or set FORGEQE_DEBUG=1) for more: the browser engine logs at debug level, and long values such as tool results are kept whole instead of cut at 8,000 characters.

Which tools ForgeQE's agent gets

Every model call carries the definitions of the tools the agent has, so it does not get all of them. Each turn gets:

  • the core: help, setup, use_capabilities, and the test environments and their apps;
  • the capabilities the request is about, picked without a model call from its words (each capability's keywords in src/capabilities/registry.ts), the apps of the environment it names (a mobile app means the mobile tools), and links (the browser);
  • capabilities used in the last few turns, so a conversation keeps its tools.

A capability this project cannot use yet (no mobile apps, no Jira) is not picked from words alone. When a request needs a capability the turn did not get, the agent calls use_capabilities, and ForgeQE continues the same request with its tools. With fewer tools in view, the agent also cannot mix up similar ones (browser_run, desktop_explore, mobile_explore). forgeqe context shows what a turn carries, and what each capability adds. The session log (/logs) records which capabilities each turn got and why (agent.scope).

Pin capabilities on, or switch them off for a project, in forgeqe.yaml (ids from forgeqe features):

yaml
agent:
  capabilities:
    always: [regression-packs]
    off: [desktop-apps]

FORGEQE_TOOL_SCOPE=off gives every tool on every turn.

MCP clients of forgeqe mcp (Claude Desktop, Cursor, Claude Code) list tools once rather than per turn, so they get the core and every capability this project can use (no desktop tools without desktop apps, no tracker tools without a tracker). use_capabilities adds others and sends notifications/tools/list_changed, which tells the client to read the list again. For a client that ignores it, forgeqe mcp --all-tools lists every tool.
